### <span id="page-38-0"></span>*DMWR studies completed in the last 10 years*

### *Short- and Long-term Resource Use by the Pacific Imperial-Pigeon*

*Seamon, J. O., V. Vaivai, A. Tualaulel'ei, and R. C. B. Utzurrum.

--- SENTENCE 494 ---
Short-and-long term resource use by a generalist frugivore, the pacific imperial-pigeon.

--- SENTENCE 495 ---
DRAFT1 ASG-DMWR Report #56665-2010-1.*

--- SENTENCE 496 ---
A long term study was conducted in order to systematically document resource use by the pacific imperial pigeon (*Ducula Pacifica)* over ten years at six different sites throughout the Island of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 497 ---
This project assessed the potential role of the pacific imperial-pigeon in structuring the plant communities in Tutuila by 1) visually collecting data on plant resource use over a decade and 2) examining short-term resource use by comparing gastro-intestinal contents from 26 birds collected over two days with two independent measures of concurrent resource availability.

--- SENTENCE 498 ---
The study documented use of at least 29 plant species, including flowers and leaves and results suggest that the pacific imperialpigeon is important for dispersal for many native and introduces species.

### *Coexistence and Long-term Population Dynamics of Three Frugivorous Birds*

*Seamon, J. O., R. C. B. Utzurrum, A. Tualaulelei, S. Fa'aumu, V. Vaivai, and R. Meyer.

--- SENTENCE 499 ---
Coexistence and long-term population dynamics of three frugivorous birds.

--- SENTENCE 500 ---
DRAFT2 ASG-DMWR Report #56665-2010-2.*

--- SENTENCE 501 ---
Three frugivorous bird species (Many-colored Fruit Dove, Pacific Imperial Pigeon, and Samoan Starling) were studied utilizing monthly data taken over a decade across six survey sites on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 502 ---
Densities for each species were estimated using distance methods, and abundances by using GIS to determine sampled areas.

--- SENTENCE 503 ---
Temporal dynamics in abundances were modeled using time series methods and forecasts were created to predict abundances.

--- SENTENCE 504 ---
The study found that Many-Colored Fruit Dove was not abundant even at sites where it regularly occurred, and was unsuitable for abundance estimation using Distance methods.

--- SENTENCE 505 ---
There was significant variation in long-term mean abundances of the three species among the six sites (Leone, Nuuuli, Amalau, Malaeimi, Masausi, Maloata).

--- SENTENCE 506 ---
*Effects of Climate and Land Use on Diversity, Prevalence, and Seasonal Transmission of Avian Hematoza in American Samoa*

*Atkinson, C. T., R. B. Utzurrum, J. O. Seamon, M. A. Schmaedick, D. A. LaPointe, C. Apelgren, A. N. Egan, and W. Watcher-Weathermax.

--- SENTENCE 507 ---
Effects of climate and land use on diversity, prevalence, and seasonal transmission of avian hematozoa in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 508 ---
Hawai`i Cooperative Studies Unit University of Hawai`i at Hilo.

--- SENTENCE 509 ---
*

The study documented diversity of vector borne parasites on Tutuila and Ta'u Islands over a 10-year period to expand earlier observations of *Plasmodium*, *Trypanosoma*, and filarial parasites, to provide better parasite identifications, and to create a better baseline for detecting new parasite introductions.

--- SENTENCE 510 ---
They also identified potential mosquito vectors of avian *Plasmodium* and *Trypanosoma*, determined whether land clearing and habitat alterations associated with subsistence farming within the National Park of American Samoa can influence parasite prevalence, and determined whether parasite prevalence is correlated with seasonal changes in rainfall, temperature and wind speed.

--- SENTENCE 511 ---
Three taxonomically distinct lineages of *Plasmodium* were identified from mosquito vectors and forest birds based on partial sequence data from parasite mitochondrial genes.

--- SENTENCE 512 ---
All three have been described from passerine and galliform birds in Australasia.

--- SENTENCE 513 ---
Two lineages, SCEDEN01 and ORW1, had elongate gametocytes and large schizonts that were consistent with species of *Plasmodium* in the subgenus *Giavannolaia*, but were taxonomically distinct from known morphological species of *Plasmodium* based on a Bayesian phylogenetic analysis of a 478 bp region of the parasite cytochrome b gene.

--- SENTENCE 514 ---
Both are candidates for description as new species.

--- SENTENCE 515 ---
The third lineage (GALLUS02) was detected only in mosquito vectors on Tutuila and was similar in cytochrome b sequence to *P. juxtanucleare*, a pathogenic species of *Plasmodium* from chickens and other galliform birds from Australasia, Africa, and South America.

--- SENTENCE 516 ---
*Plasmodium relictum*, the malarial parasite that has had such a devastating impact on Hawaiian forest birds, was not detected.

--- SENTENCE 517 ---
They observed large, striated trypanosomes in avian hosts from both Tutuila and Ta'u Islands that fell within the same taxonomic clade as *T. corvi* and *T. culicavium* based on 18S ribosomal DNA sequence.

--- SENTENCE 518 ---
They also observed sheathed microfilariae with pointed tails that had some morphological similarities to microfilaria from species of *Pelecitus, Struthiofilaria and Eulimdana*, but identification will require recovery and examination of adult filarial worms from the connective tissue or body cavities of infected birds.

--- SENTENCE 519 ---
They also observed one or more species of *haemococcidians* (Isospora , synonym = Atoxoplasma) within circulating lymphocytes from multiple avian host species.

--- SENTENCE 520 ---
Overall prevalence of *Plasmodium* was higher on Ta'u (22%, 75/341) than Tutuila (9.2%, 27/294), with most infections occurring in Polynesian starlings, Samoan starlings, Wattled honeyeaters, and Cardinal honeyeaters.

--- SENTENCE 521 ---
Prevalence was relatively constant from year to year and between seasons at individual study sites, but varied among study sites, with highest rates of infection in areas with agricultural activity at Faleasao (37.4%, 73/195, Ta'u Island) and Amalau Valley (9.7%, 21/216, Tutuila Island).

--- SENTENCE 522 ---
Prevalence in more remote areas of the National Park of American Samoa was lower, ranging from 1.4% (2/146) at Laufuti and Luatele on Ta'u to 7.7% (6/78) at Olo Ridge on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 523 ---
Similar trends were evident for infections with *Trypanosoma* and filarial worms.

--- SENTENCE 524 ---
Overall prevalence was not influenced significantly by warmer, wet (summer) or cooler, dry (winter) season.

--- SENTENCE 525 ---
They detected Plasmodium infections in *Culex sitiens* and *C. quinquefasciatus* through either salivary gland and midgut dissections or PCR amplification of parasite cytochrome b genes in pooled or individual samples of mosquitoes that were collected on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 526 ---
Pooled or individual *Aedes oceanicus, A. polynesiensis, A. tutuilae, A. upolensis, A. nocturnus, Aedes* ( Finlaya ) (mixed pools of *A. samoanus, A. oceanicus, A. tutuilae*), *Aedes ( S. tegomyia* )

--- SENTENCE 527 ---
(mixed pools of *A. aegypti, A. upolensis, A. polynesiensis* ), and *C. annulirostris* were negative for *Plasmodium* , but they detected infections with *Trypanosoma* through midgut and salivary gland dissections in a single *C. sitiens* from Amalau Valley, Tutuila and three *A. oceanicus* from Faleasao, Ta'u.

--- SENTENCE 528 ---
Two of the *A. oceanicus* from Faleasao amplified successfully with *Trypanosoma* primers, but sequences were distinctly different from those obtained from avian hosts.

--- SENTENCE 529 ---
The project found a strong association between land use and prevalence of mosquitotransmitted parasites on Ta'u Island with odds of being infected more than 20 times greater in agricultural plots than more remote native forest.

--- SENTENCE 530 ---
This relationship was evident on Tutuila Island but not statistically significant because of the close proximity of study sites and observed movement of birds between native forest and agricultural land.

--- SENTENCE 531 ---
Our data support previous studies that have suggested that *Plasmodium* and other vector-borne parasites are part of the indigenous parasite fauna in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 532 ---
Transmission dynamics appear to be affected by environmental changes associated with land use practices.

### *Evolutionary Origins of the Samoan Avifauna*

*Modak, T. 2011.

--- SENTENCE 533 ---
Evolutionary origins of the Samoan avifauna.

--- SENTENCE 534 ---
*

This study used DNA sequences of the mitochondrial ND2 gene to reconstruct the phylogenetic relationships of common Samoan land bird species (starlings, shrikebills, fantails, honeyeaters, kingfishers, whistlers, white-eyes) along with closely related taxa in American Samoa and neighboring islands.

--- SENTENCE 535 ---
All but one, Samoan Fantail (Rhipidura nebulosi), of the Samoan populations sampled forms a genetically distinct, monophyletic lineage.

--- SENTENCE 536 ---
For example, wattled honeyeaters (Foulehaio carunculata) in Samoa are 4.8% divergent in ND2 sequence from wattled honeyeaters in Vanua Levu, Fiji, suggesting longterm isolation and independent evolution.

--- SENTENCE 537 ---
While nine of the ten Samoan populations sampled are genetically distinct, the depth of historical divergence from the most closely related population elsewhere varies greatly among taxa.

--- SENTENCE 538 ---
In addition, tree topologies for different taxa do not conform to a common evolutionary or biogeographic history, although birds from nearby Fiji are often the closest relatives of Samoan birds.

--- SENTENCE 539 ---
These results suggest that each lineage has had its own idiosyncratic history and that the assembly of the Samoan avifauna has proceeded gradually over time.

--- SENTENCE 540 ---
The genetic distinctiveness of Samoan species warrants a reevaluation of their taxonomy and heightens concerns for their conservation.

--- SENTENCE 541 ---
The comparative phylogenetic analyses showed that the Samoan birds lack a common evolutionary and biogeographic history.

--- SENTENCE 542 ---
Multiple colonization events over an extended period of time from various sources likely led to the assembly of the current diversity of avian species in the Samoan Islands.

--- SENTENCE 543 ---
These results are consistent with the theory of island biogeography, which envisions the accumulation of species on oceanic islands as a stochastic process that continues over time and eventually leads to ongoing turnover as old species go extinct and new species arrive.

### <span id="page-41-0"></span>*3.2.2.3 MAMMALS*

### <span id="page-41-1"></span>*Terrestrial mammals*

Discounting domestic animals, there are eight species of terrestrial mammals present in islands of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 544 ---
Of these, three are indigenous bats, four are exotic murids (*Mus musculus*, *Rattus exulans*, *R. norvegicus*, and *R. rattus*), and one is a feral pig (*Sus scrofa*) that is considered to be a Polynesian introduction.

--- SENTENCE 545 ---
The three native mammals are all members of the Order Chiroptera: *Emballonura semicaudata semicaudata* (Family Emballonuridae), and *Pteropus samoensis* and *P. tonganus* (Family Pteropodidae).

--- SENTENCE 546 ---
All three species are considered of conservation concern, with *E. semicaudata, which was listed as Endangered under the ESA in September 2016,*  possibly extinct in the Territory and deserving the highest priority action.

--- SENTENCE 547 ---
The introduced species, on the other hand, are of concern due to their potential impact on native species or the habitat.

--- SENTENCE 548 ---
Introduced rats may prey on most ground nesting birds, and recent works have documented their presence in high elevation areas where native species-of-concern (sea birds and the spotless crake) are known to occur and/or nest.

--- SENTENCE 549 ---
The impact of feral pigs on forest regeneration and seedling survivorship in native habitats, added to the possibility that they may facilitate dispersal of invasive plants, has led the NPAS to conduct regular removal trapping in Park areas in the past (R. Cook, *pers.

--- SENTENCE 550 ---
The degree of threat or impact of these introduced species should be determined so that appropriate control or mitigation may be established.

### <span id="page-42-0"></span>*Marine mammals*

The dearth in scientific information on species and abundances of marine mammal species occurring in American Samoa in the face of: a) global concerns for the status of marine mammals; b) efforts to establish regional conservation and protection programs particularly for migratory species (SPREP, 2005); and c) a local initiative declaring Territorial waters as marine mammal and turtle sanctuaries (American Samoa Government Executive Order No. 005-2003) has elevated this group into the American Samoa's list of Species of Conservation Concern (see Subsection 5.10).

--- SENTENCE 551 ---
Following is an overview of the diversity in the region, in general, and in waters surrounding American Samoa, in particular, as excerpted from the Dolar (2005) report commissioned by DMWR:

--- SENTENCE 552 ---
"To date, 33 marine mammal species have been reported to occur in the tropical South Pacific either as resident, seasonal migrant or occasional visitor.

--- SENTENCE 553 ---
The list includes 30 species of cetaceans, one sirenian and two species of pinnipeds (Reeves et al., 1999).

--- SENTENCE 554 ---
Most populations of the commercially important large whales have been greatly reduced by whaling, and hunting of medium-sized and small cetaceans still occurs in some areas in the South Pacific Ocean (*e.g*.

--- SENTENCE 555 ---
(Reeves, et al. 1999, Dawbin, 1984; Kahn, 2004).

--- SENTENCE 556 ---
Of the 33 species of marine mammals recorded present in the region, thirteen have been confirmed from the waters surrounding the islands of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 557 ---
Two of these are mysticetes: minke whale (*Balaenoptera acutorostrata or B. bonaerensis*), and the humpback whale (*Megaptera novaeangliae*)*;* the remaining eleven are odontocetes: sperm whale (*Physeter macrocephalus*), short-finned pilot whale (*Globicephala macrorhynchus*), killer whale (*Orcinus orca*), common bottlenose dolphin (*Tursiops truncates, Tursiops aduncus),* pantropical spotted dolphin (*Stenella attenuata*), spinner dolphin (*Stenella longirostris)*

--- SENTENCE 558 ---
(Reeves et al., 1999), striped dolphin (*Stenella coeruleoalba*) rough-toothed dolphin (*Steno bredanensis*), Cuvier's beaked whale (*Ziphius cavirostris)*

--- SENTENCE 559 ---
(Utzurrum pers, comm.; Craig, 2005) and false killer whale (*Pseudorca crassidens*) (Craig, 2005).

--- SENTENCE 560 ---
### <span id="page-43-0"></span>**3.3 THE MARINE FAUNA AND CONSERVATION PRIORITIES**

### <span id="page-43-1"></span>*3.3.1 THE MARINE INVERTEBRATES*

Various marine invertebrates are fished for both subsistence and commercial fisheries.

--- SENTENCE 561 ---
Marine invertebrates threatened by habitat degradation, predation and global climate change impacts.

--- SENTENCE 562 ---
The species of greatest concern are two species of giant clams, three holothurians or sea cucumbers and the 5 hard corals now listed as threated under the Endangered Species Act.

--- SENTENCE 563 ---
There are three native species of giant clams ('faisua'): *Tridacna maxima*, *T. squamosa* and *Hippopus hippopus*.

--- SENTENCE 564 ---
*Tridacna derasa* was introduced to the Territory during a giant clam mariculture project in the 1980's.

--- SENTENCE 565 ---
'*Faisua*' is a delicacy in Samoan culture and they are highly exploited.

--- SENTENCE 566 ---
Giant clams owing to their photosynthetic symbionts are mostly found in coral reefs that receive adequate sunlight.

--- SENTENCE 567 ---
Radtke (1985) estimated the population size and density, age structure, growth rate, maturity and mortality of *T. maxima* in Rose Atoll.

--- SENTENCE 568 ---
Green and Craig (1999) surveyed *T. maxima* in six islands including Rose Atoll.

--- SENTENCE 569 ---
The NOAA CRED conducted macroinvertebrate surveys that included giant clams in 2002, 2004, and 2006.

--- SENTENCE 570 ---
Green and Craig (1999) and Radtke (1985) showed that *T. maxima* populations have slow turn-over rate and clams have slow individual growths.

--- SENTENCE 571 ---
Both studies showed that Rose Atoll has relatively high densities.

--- SENTENCE 572 ---
The NOAA CRED data showed that Tau had the highest densities in 2002, 2004 and 2006.

--- SENTENCE 573 ---
The inner lagoon of Rose Atoll also had high densities of giant clams.

--- SENTENCE 574 ---
Most of the recorded clams were *T. maxima*.

--- SENTENCE 575 ---
*T. squamosa* are occasionally recorded in the commercial spearfisheries.

--- SENTENCE 576 ---
*Hippopus hippopus* abundance is very low based on anecdotes of a few shells found in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 577 ---
There are 9 species of holothurians recorded in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 578 ---
Lawrence (2014, unpublished) reported that four species, namely, *Holothuria whitmaei*, *Actinopyga mauritiana*, *Stichopus chloronotus*, *Holothuria atra*, were harvested commercially in the last 2 years.

--- SENTENCE 579 ---
Holothurians are found in various habitats in coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 580 ---
The governor passed an executive order banning the commercial harvest of holothurians.

--- SENTENCE 581 ---
There have been reports of continued violations and it is suspected that the trade has gone underground.

--- SENTENCE 582 ---
The species of greatest concern are *Holothuria fuscogilva*, *Holothuria whitmaei*, and *Actinopyga mauritiana*, all of which have been listed as threatened by IUCN.

--- SENTENCE 583 ---
Dr. Douglas Fenner has recorded over 200 species of corals in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 584 ---
The six hard corals that have been listed as threatened under the Endangered Species Act are: *Acropora globiceps*, *Acropora speciosa*, *Acropora retusa*, *Isopora crateriformis*, *Euphyllia paradivisa* and *Pavona diffluens*.

--- SENTENCE 585 ---
All of these corals have been deduced to have declined in abundance for the last 50 to 100 years although no species-specific information are available.

--- SENTENCE 586 ---
*Acropora globiceps* is a digitate coral that is known to occur on upper reef slopes, reef flats and adjacent habitats from 0 to 8 m in water depth.

--- SENTENCE 587 ---
This coral is fairly common in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 588 ---
Its morphology is similar to 6 other species, taxonomy being an underlying issue in corals.

--- SENTENCE 589 ---
*Acropora speciosa* has a morphology described as thick cushions or bottlebrush branches.

--- SENTENCE 590 ---
It is known to occur on lower reef slopes and walls.

--- SENTENCE 591 ---
It has been observed to be similar in appearance to two other corals.

--- SENTENCE 592 ---
Its depth range is 12 to 40 meters, and has been found in mesophotic (>100 m depth) habitats.

--- SENTENCE 593 ---
*Isopora crateriformis* form encrusting plates to over a meter in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 594 ---
There is a moderate level of taxonomic uncertainty for this coral.

--- SENTENCE 595 ---
Veron (2014) stated that *I. crateriformis* can be easily confused with *I. cuneata*.

--- SENTENCE 596 ---
This coral is commonly in shallow, high-wave energy environments, from low tide to at least 12 meters deep, and has been reported from mesophotic depths.

--- SENTENCE 597 ---
*I. crateriformis* is one of the most common species on upper reef slopes of southwest Tutuila Island.

--- SENTENCE 598 ---
Both *E. paradivisa* and *P. diffluens* are very rare in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 599 ---
*E. paradivisa* is a sub-massive coral and its habitat is shallow or mid-slope reef environments protected from wave action, from five to 20 meters depth.

--- SENTENCE 600 ---
*P. diffluens* is a sub-massive coral that looks similar to two other species of *Pavona*.

--- SENTENCE 601 ---
It is known to occur in upper reef slopes, mid-slopes, lower reef crests, reef flats, and lagoons, in depths ranging from five to at least 20 m. Colonies of *Acropora retusa* are flat plates with short, thick finger-like branches, with the branches appearing rough and spiky because its radial corallites are variable in length.

--- SENTENCE 602 ---
Colonies are typically brown or green in color.

--- SENTENCE 603 ---
This coral is a hermaphrodite, containing both male and female gametes.

--- SENTENCE 604 ---
*Acropora retusa* occurs in shallow reef slope and back-reef areas, such as upper reef slopes, reef flats, and shallow lagoons, and its depth range is 0 to 5 meters.

--- SENTENCE 605 ---
It is a rare coral.

### <span id="page-44-0"></span>*Threats to marine invertebrates*

Overexploitation is the main threat to the giant clams and sea cucumbers in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 606 ---
Other threats include habitat degradation, sedimentation and global climate change.

--- SENTENCE 607 ---
Giant clams thrive in clear, oceanic waters that can be negatively impacted by high sedimentation with run-offs from high-islands like Tutuila Island.

--- SENTENCE 608 ---
Giant clams also harbor photosynthetic symbiotic zooxanthellae that are expelled when stressed during bleaching events triggered by increased water temperature.

--- SENTENCE 609 ---
The commercial fishery of the holothurians has spread from the Southeast Asian region to remote Pacific areas like American Samoa and Hawaii.

--- SENTENCE 610 ---
This indicates a trend of overexploitation fishery expansion to countries that are remote from the main Chinese market and areas with no historic commercial fishery of sea cucumbers.

--- SENTENCE 611 ---
The ESA-listed threatened corals have been deduced to be vulnerable to ocean warming, disease, ocean acidification, trophic effects of fishing, nutrients, and predation, sedimentation, sea-level rise, and collection and trade.

--- SENTENCE 612 ---
In American Samoa, predation by crown-of-thorns starfish, sedimentation and sea-level rise are probably the biggest shortterm threats.

--- SENTENCE 613 ---
<span id="page-45-0"></span>*Marine invertebrates' general conservation priorities*

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 614 ---
We propose *to conduct active coral reef restoration methods* to mitigate the impacts of marine threats.

--- SENTENCE 615 ---
The coral reef restoration should include restocking and development of hatcheries.

--- SENTENCE 616 ---
For example, hatcheries for giant clams have been developed in several sites in the Pacific and re-stocking of hatchery-produced giant clams have been successful.

--- SENTENCE 617 ---
We also propose to develop ocean nurseries for corals where we will re-grow colonies from fragments.

--- SENTENCE 618 ---
These re-grown colonies will subsequently be transplanted to denuded reefs.

--- SENTENCE 619 ---
We have already adapted this technology to a limited extent in the Territory and propose to expand the implementation.

--- SENTENCE 620 ---
We propose *to conduct vulnerability assessment of islands and coral reefs and predict area-specific impacts of global climate change* in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 621 ---
This assessment will help identify resilient areas and prioritize areas of conservation.

--- SENTENCE 622 ---
In addition, this plan will continue to support an ecosystem or ridge-to-reef approach to conservation and management of marine protected areas that are already in place in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 623 ---
We propose *to develop a program to eradicate coral reef predators*.

--- SENTENCE 624 ---
The Territory has been hit by an outbreak of the crown-of-thorns starfish for the last four years.

--- SENTENCE 625 ---
We will conduct the eradication in collaboration with various government agencies.

--- SENTENCE 626 ---
The proposed program will also involve the AS Environmental Protection Agency as COTS outbreaks have been hypothesized to result from significant nutrient influx to coral reefs.
- 4.

--- SENTENCE 627 ---
There is a need *to conduct basic life history research* on these invertebrates of concern.

--- SENTENCE 628 ---
Basic research on growth rate, longevity, reproduction, recruitment, population structure and distribution are critical to our understanding of ther biology and the threats they are facing.

### <span id="page-45-1"></span>*3.3.2 THE MARINE FISHES*

### <span id="page-45-2"></span>*3.3.2.1 OCEANIC FISHES*

Wass (1984) recorded 991 fish species in American Samoa, 45 of which are considered pelagic surface fishes.

--- SENTENCE 629 ---
The Pelagic Fisheries Ecosystem Plan by the Western Pacific Regional Fisheries Management Council lists 30 pelagic species as management units: 7 tuna and tuna-relative species, six billfishes, 9 sharks, three squids, dolphinfish, moonfish, wahoo, oilfish and pomphret.

--- SENTENCE 630 ---
Their designation as management units reflect their status as important to the pelagic fisheries.

--- SENTENCE 631 ---
The species of concern are the big-eye tuna *Thunnus obesus*, the striped marlin *Kajikia audax*, the silky shark *Carcarhinus falciformis* and the oceanic white tip *Carcarhinus longimanus*.

--- SENTENCE 632 ---
Both the big-eye tuna and the striped marlin constitute minor contributions to the longline fishery based in American Samoa that mainly targets the Southern Pacific albacore *Thunnus alalunga*.

--- SENTENCE 633 ---
The silky shark and the oceanic white tip shark are by-catches of the longline fishery.

--- SENTENCE 634 ---
There is currently a zero-retention for sharks in the US fleets of longline and purse seine fisheries.

--- SENTENCE 635 ---
These sharks, however, are targeted by the pelagic fisheries in other countries.

--- SENTENCE 636 ---
The current stock assessments by the Science Committee of the Western and Central Pacific Fisheries Commission for the big-eye tuna, the striped marlin, the silky shark and the oceanic white tip shark indicate that these stocks are overfished and experiencing overfishing.

--- SENTENCE 637 ---
The big-eye tuna (BET) has similar latitudinal distributions with the yellow-fin tuna (YFT).

--- SENTENCE 638 ---
BET, however, has evolved to exploit cooler, deeper and more oxygen poor waters and when compared to YFT is pointed as an example of adaptive niche partitioning.

--- SENTENCE 639 ---
This has been accomplished through a combination of physiological and behavioral thermoregulation and other anatomical adaptations (Lowe et al. 2000; Fritsches and Warrant 2001).

--- SENTENCE 640 ---
The BET has life history traits characterized by rapid growth during the juvenile stage, movements between temperate and tropical waters to feed and spawn, equatorial spawning with high fecundity, preference for cool water foraging and a protracted maturity schedule, an extended life span and the potential for broad spatial movements.

--- SENTENCE 641 ---
Maximum fork length of BET is about 200 cm.

--- SENTENCE 642 ---
The growth of juveniles appears to depart somewhat from von Bertalanffy type growth with the growth rate slowing between about 40 and 70 cm FL (Lehodey et al. 1999).

--- SENTENCE 643 ---
BET becomes reproductively mature at 100 cm (around 3 years old) and 100% of individuals >120 cm are sexually mature.

--- SENTENCE 644 ---
As with other tunas, sex ratio changes at sexual maturity with more males at larger sizes.

--- SENTENCE 645 ---
Spawning occurs throughout the Pacific the whole year in tropical waters with water temperatures above 24°C and seasonally at higher latitudes (Kume 1967; Miyabe 1994).

--- SENTENCE 646 ---
The highest reproductive potential was considered to be in the EPO based on size frequencies and catch per unit of effort inferred abundance (Kikawa 1966).

--- SENTENCE 647 ---
It has been suggested that sexual maturity, or more accurately, the development into active spawning condition appears to be linked to mixed layer water temperatures above 26E C (Mohri 1998).

--- SENTENCE 648 ---
Hisada (1979) reported that BET require a mixed layer depth of at least 50 m with a sea surface temperature (SST) of at least 24°C.

--- SENTENCE 649 ---
Estimates of natural mortality for bigeye tuna is 0.5 yr-1 for >40 cm FL (Hampton 2000).

--- SENTENCE 650 ---
Maximum age is around 16 years.

--- SENTENCE 651 ---
Analysis of mtDNA and microsatellites did not reveal significant genetic differentiation in the Pacific Ocean (Grewe and Hampton 1998).

--- SENTENCE 652 ---
This is consistent with taggin experiments, some of tagged bigeye tuna traveled 4,000 miles.

--- SENTENCE 653 ---
However, majority of those tagged were recaptured much closer to their release points (Schaefer and Fuller 2002; Hampton and Williams 2005).

--- SENTENCE 654 ---
It is hypothesised that while bigeye tuna in the far eastern and western Pacific may have relatively little exchange, those in the central part of the Pacific between about 180° and 120°W may mix more rapidly over distances of 1,000 – 3,000 nautical miles.

--- SENTENCE 655 ---
Like many tunas, BET exists as 'semi-discrete' stocks with most fish tending to stay close around islands, atolls and seamounts with some fish moving wider distances.

--- SENTENCE 656 ---
The combination of relative site-fidelity, longer lifespan, slow growth, bigger sizes at sexual maturity, and fishing pressure makes big-eye tuna more vulnerable than yellow-fin tuna.

--- SENTENCE 657 ---
The striped marlin *Kajikia audax* has a distribution extending through equatorial to temperate waters with highest catch rates within subequatorial and subtropical areas in the Pacific Ocean (Nakamura 1985).

--- SENTENCE 658 ---
Most catches of striped marlin have been reported from surface waters less than 100 m deep (Brill et al. 1993, Domeier et al. 2003).

--- SENTENCE 659 ---
The striped marlin is vulnerable to surface fisheries (longline, recreational and purse seine method fisheries) from a relatively young age.

--- SENTENCE 660 ---
There is little data on the biology and ecology of striped marlin as a result of their relatively low abundances, low catch rates, highly mobile nature and low priority for research funding.

--- SENTENCE 661 ---
Based on the observed distribution of larval striped marlin, spawning occurs between November and December in the southwest Pacific Ocean (10–30ºS) (Nakamura 1985).

--- SENTENCE 662 ---
Striped marlin displays very high initial growth rates, attaining up to 45% of their maximum size in the first year of life (Melo-Barrera et al. 2003) and perhaps 70-75% of their maximum size by the second year of life (Kopf et al. 2009, 2011).

--- SENTENCE 663 ---
Growth rates slows down following the onset of maturity (Melo-Barrera et al. 2003, Kopf 2009) at around 140– 180 cm fork length, 27–40 kg weight and at approximately 2 years of age (Skillman and Yong 1976, Nakamura 1985, Kopf 2011).

--- SENTENCE 664 ---
Sexual differences in growth rates have been reported, and females tend to be heavier than males (Kopf et al. 2011).

--- SENTENCE 665 ---
Striped marlin live for at least 10–12 years (Melo-Barrera et al. 2003, Kopf et al. 2005, 2011) and can exceed more than 300 cm (lower jaw–fork length, LJFL) and 240 kg.

--- SENTENCE 666 ---
Estimated natural mortality rates (M) vary between sexes, being lower in males (0.57–0.79 year-1) than females (0.82– 1.33 year-1) (Boggs 1989, Hinton and Bayliff 2002).

--- SENTENCE 667 ---
Large striped marlin tends to move further into temperate regions on a seasonal basis.

--- SENTENCE 668 ---
However, current tag-recapture data suggest some level of broader sub-regional fidelity (Bromhead et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 669 ---
The silky shark *Carcarhinus falciformis* is a circumtropical species found in tropical waters of the Pacific Ocean.

--- SENTENCE 670 ---
It is found in the coastal and oceanic waters of the Western and Central Pacific Ocean and one of the most commonly caught sharks in the tropical tuna fisheries (Clarke et al. 2011a).

--- SENTENCE 671 ---
There is limited data on the biology, ecology and movement patterns of silky sharks (Bonfil 2008).

--- SENTENCE 672 ---
Available data indicates that silky sharks show a preference for warmer tropical waters above 23˚C (Last and Stevens 1994).

--- SENTENCE 673 ---
For the first few years of its life, silky sharks in the Pacific Ocean lead demersal/semipelagic lifestyles usually associated with reefs and deeper parts of the continental and insular shelves before moving to more offshore and pelagic environments as sub-adults (Bonfil (2008).

--- SENTENCE 674 ---
Silky sharks then switch to a more oceanic habitat at around 130 cm in total length, where they often join schools of large pelagic fish (such as tuna) and may disperse seasonally from the equator to higher latitudes (Strasburg 1958, Bonfil 2008).

--- SENTENCE 675 ---
Adult silky sharks are known to return seasonally to feed and reproduce in shelf waters although near term pregnant females and neonates are also found in oceanic waters (Bonfil 2008).

--- SENTENCE 676 ---
The silky white shark is viviparous with placental embryonic development with 8-10 pups per litter and a 9-12 month gestation period with pups with a sex ratio of 1:1 (Oshitani et al. (2003).

--- SENTENCE 677 ---
Newborn silky sharks size at birth is 63.5 cm (Joung et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 678 ---
Spawning season in the Pacific spans over much of the year (February- August)

--- SENTENCE 679 ---
(Branstetter 1987, Bonfil et al. 1993; Bonfil 2008).

--- SENTENCE 680 ---
There is a positive correlation between maternal size and litter size (Cadena-Cárdenas 2001, Oshitani et al. 2003).

--- SENTENCE 681 ---
Estimated sizes at 50% maturity for silky sharks in the western Pacific are 212.5 cm TL for males and 210-220 cm TL for females (Joung et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 682 ---
The Von Bertalanffy growth coefficient (k) ranges from 0.08 to 0.15 with a longevity of 36 years for females.

--- SENTENCE 683 ---
The population intrinsic rate of increase and natural mortality are 0.102 and 0.17-0.21 respectively (Cortés, 2002).

--- SENTENCE 684 ---
The oceanic whitetip shark *Carcarhinus longimanus* is a circumtropical species found in tropical waters of the Pacific Ocean.

--- SENTENCE 685 ---
This shark was once considered one of the most common sharks in all tropical oceans of the world (Bonfil et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 686 ---
It is frequently caught in commercial fisheries and their fins command high commercial value (Bonfil et al. 2008;

--- SENTENCE 687 ---
Clarke et al. 2005, 2006).

--- SENTENCE 688 ---
The oceanic whitetip shark is truly oceanic and show a clear preference for the open ocean water between 10˚S and 10˚N, but can be found in decreasing numbers out to latitudes of 30˚N and 30˚S with decreasing abundance with increasing proximity to continental shelves (Backus et al. 1956, Strasburg, 1958; Compango, 1984, Bonfil et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 689 ---
Little is known about possible migration paths for oceanic whitetips in the Pacific although tagging studies in the Atlantic ocean indicate movement along the equator and from southern latitudes (off Brazil) to the equator (Kohler et al. 1998, Bonfil et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 690 ---
The oceanic whitetip is viviparous with placental embryonic development, an average fecundity of 6 pups per female and a 9-12 month gestation period (Seki et al. (1998).

--- SENTENCE 691 ---
Estimated sizes at maturity are 168-196 cm for males and 175-189 cm TL for females.

--- SENTENCE 692 ---
Seki et al. (1998) found a weak positive correlation between maternal size and litter size.

--- SENTENCE 693 ---
Seki et al. (1998) estimated a growth coefficient of k=0.103 corresponding to a theoretical L∞ of approximately 340 cm (TL) which corresponds to an age of approximately 36 years.

--- SENTENCE 694 ---
Oceanic whitetip growth rate is considered slow compared to other pelagic sharks, namely blue, mako, and silky (Branstetter, 1990).

--- SENTENCE 695 ---
In the Pacific, oceanic white tips reach sexual maturity after about between 4-7 years (170-200 cm).

--- SENTENCE 696 ---
Estimates of the intrinsic rates of increase range from 0.081 to 0.11 (Smith et al. 1998, Cortés, 2002).

--- SENTENCE 697 ---
Overall the biology of oceanic white tip, indicates a species with low resilience to fishing – even among shark species - and minimal capacity for compensation.

### <span id="page-49-0"></span>*3.3.2.2 CORAL REEF AND NEARSHORE FISHES*

Out of the 991 fish species by Wass (1984), 890 are considered shallow-water or reef inhabiting fish.

--- SENTENCE 698 ---
The fish species of concern are:

- 1.)

--- SENTENCE 699 ---
The humphead wrasse *Cheilinus undulatus* is the largest member of the wrasses family Labridae.

--- SENTENCE 700 ---
It attains a maximum size of over 200 cm.

--- SENTENCE 701 ---
It is usually not common in coral reefs, even relatively rare in undisturbed habitats.

--- SENTENCE 702 ---
It is a protogynous hermaphrodite fish and forms pair-spawning and aggregations.

--- SENTENCE 703 ---
It attains sexual maturity at 5 to 7 years of age and males live up to 25 years and females up to 30 years.

--- SENTENCE 704 ---
So unlike many other coral reef fishes, the humphead wrasse attains sexual maturity at 20% of its maximum size.

--- SENTENCE 705 ---
Juveniles are mostly found in high coral reef cover.

--- SENTENCE 706 ---
Growth is very rapid and indeterminate among males.

--- SENTENCE 707 ---
Growth in females seem to asymptote around 15 years.

--- SENTENCE 708 ---
Because of its naturally low abundance, aggregative spawning behavior and relative slow sexual maturity, it is sensitive to even slight fishing pressure.

--- SENTENCE 709 ---
Life history data has been limited and Choat et al.

--- SENTENCE 710 ---
(2006) suggested additional and more detailed analysis of demographic and abundance in other areas.

--- SENTENCE 711 ---
The bumphead parrotfish *Bolbometopon muricatum* is the largest member of the parrotfishes (family Scaridae) growing up to 130 cm and weighing 46 kg.

--- SENTENCE 712 ---
Adults develop a bulbous forehead and their teeth plates are exposed, partly covered by fleshy lips).

--- SENTENCE 713 ---
Owing to its corallivorous diet, it is an important coral reef bioeroder and species of ecological significance on ecosystem resilience (Bellwood et al. 2003).

--- SENTENCE 714 ---
Each fish is estimated to ingest 5 tons of reef carbonates in a year.

--- SENTENCE 715 ---
There is limited life history data for this large parrotfish.

--- SENTENCE 716 ---
Size-at-age data is limited to a few samples and only in the Great Barrier Reef.

--- SENTENCE 717 ---
Maximum age is around 33 years and growth rate is relatively slow.

--- SENTENCE 718 ---
There is generally low recruitment and perhaps owing to its diet, this fish is mostly found in coral reef habitats.

--- SENTENCE 719 ---
(Choat and Robertson 2002, Hamilton 2003).

--- SENTENCE 720 ---
Juveniles are mostly found in seagrass beds and adults in lagoons and reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 721 ---
Adults usually rove and sleep in groups (Myers 1999) making this species highly vulnerable to fishing.

--- SENTENCE 722 ---
Although this species has a very wide range, population sizes have been declining.

--- SENTENCE 723 ---
It is rarely encountered at a few islands of American Samoa during intensive diver surveys on research cruises by NMFS Pacific Islands Fisheries Science Center.

--- SENTENCE 724 ---
*Plectropomus areolatus* and *Plectropomus Laevis*.

--- SENTENCE 725 ---
*P. areolatus* inhabits lagoon and seaward reefs rich with coral growth.

--- SENTENCE 726 ---
It feeds exclusively on fishes and may be ciguatoxic in some areas.

--- SENTENCE 727 ---
This grouper forms spawning aggregations in association with specific lunar phases, with variable seasonality and lunar cycle associated with spawning by locale.

--- SENTENCE 728 ---
It commonly co-aggregates with the camouflage grouper, *Epinephelus polyphekadion*, and brownmarbled grouper, *E. fuscoguttatus* throughout its range.

--- SENTENCE 729 ---
The IUCN assessment stated that although *P. areolatus* is heavily fished and is hence declining at a rate of at least 30% over the last 20 to 30 years.

--- SENTENCE 730 ---
The species is particularly vulnerable due to fishing targeted at spawning aggregations (Pet et al. 2005) and larger size at sex change observed with very low proportion of males.

--- SENTENCE 731 ---
Size-at-age data indicated that *P. areolatus* is a fast-growing, early maturing and relatively short-lived aggregation-spawning grouper (Rhodes et al. 2013).

--- SENTENCE 732 ---
Females and males sexually mature at 2 and 3 years, respectively, suggesting protogynous hermaphroditism.

--- SENTENCE 733 ---
Mature females ranged from 318-469 cm SL, while males were found between 390-570 cm SL.

--- SENTENCE 734 ---
Both sexes reach 90% of asymptotic length by age 3 years.

--- SENTENCE 735 ---
Maximum age is around 12 years.

--- SENTENCE 736 ---
The related grouper *P. laevis* is a widespread species in the Indo-Pacific but naturally uncommon (average of less than 1 individual per 1000 sq m).

--- SENTENCE 737 ---
It is a monandric protogynous hermaphrodite and is conspicuous on outer coral reef slopes occurring in depths between 4 and 90 m (Heemstra and Randall 1993).

--- SENTENCE 738 ---
However, this grouper has very few sightings in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 739 ---
*P. laevis* appears to be a relatively fast-growing species and reaches 50 cm in less than four years; females achieve maturity in under three years (Davies et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 740 ---
Growth parameters based on the von Bertalanffy model ranged from *K* = 0.14 to 0.19 year–1 (Davies et al. 2006, Grandcourt 2005).

--- SENTENCE 741 ---
Maximum age is about 16 years, and age at first maturity around 1 year with the size range over which sex change occurs.

--- SENTENCE 742 ---
*Epinephelus lanceolatus* is the largest grouper and reef-dwelling fish in the world (Gomon et al. 1994) and is much sought after by line and spearfishers.

--- SENTENCE 743 ---
It is also the mostly widely distributed grouper being found throughtout the Indo-Pacific from the Red Sea to Pitcairn islands (Sadovy and Cornish 2000).

--- SENTENCE 744 ---
It tends to be solitary and inhabits lagoon and seaward reefs at a depth up to 100 m.

--- SENTENCE 745 ---
Large individuals often home in specific sites in which they frequently stay (Myers 1991).

--- SENTENCE 746 ---
Being such a large predator, it is rare even in areas unexploited by fishing (Randall and Heemstra 1991) and it has nearly been extirpated in areas where it has been heavily fished especially for the live reef food-fish trade (Lieske and Myers 1994).

--- SENTENCE 747 ---
Very little is known about the life history of the giant grouper in addition to the information that grows up to 2 meters and over 450 kgs.

--- SENTENCE 748 ---
The estimated size of sexual maturation for *E. lanceolatus* is 105–110 cm TL, which means that all individuals are collected in the live fish trade before they reach sexual maturity.
- 5.)

--- SENTENCE 749 ---
Several sharks are species of concern: (a) the scalloped hammerhead shark *Spyrna lewini*; (b) the whale shark *Rhincodon typus*; (c) the tawny nurse shark *Nebrius ferrugineus*; (d) the

zebra shark *Stegostoma fasciatum*; (e) the sicklefin lemon shark *Negaprion acutidens*; (f) the grey reef shark *Carcarhinus amblyrhynchos*; (g) the black tip shark *Carcarhinus melanopterus*; and (h) the white tip shark *Triaenodon obesus*.

--- SENTENCE 750 ---
These sharks have traits that are a combination of low in abundance and with a life history characterized by slow growth, late maturing, low fecundity (or high mortality during the early stages), site fidelity and long life.

--- SENTENCE 751 ---
The late age of maturity and the high maximum age are apparently the two life history traits that make sharks susceptible to fishing.

--- SENTENCE 752 ---
It has been hypothesized that both historical and current overfishing have cause the decline in their abundance (Nadon et al. 2010).

--- SENTENCE 753 ---
In addition, the populations of these fish probably encompass various island countries and territories in the south Pacific where fishing regulations are ineffective or lacking.

--- SENTENCE 754 ---
- (a) The scalloped hammerhead shark *S. lewini* is a circumglobal species found in coastal warm temperate and tropical seas, from intertidal and surface up to 275 m in depth (Compagno 1984).

--- SENTENCE 755 ---
It is a highly mobile and partly migratory (Maguire et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 756 ---
It is a viviparous shark and parturition occurs in coastal waters where the young tend to stay in up to 2 years before moving to oceanic habitats as it grows older.

--- SENTENCE 757 ---
Maximum size ranged from 219-340 cm TL for males and 296-346 cm for females.

--- SENTENCE 758 ---
Males mature between 140- 198 cm TL and females at around 210-250 cm TL (Miller et al. 2014).

--- SENTENCE 759 ---
The scalloped hammerhead shark is considered long-lived (20 to 30 years) and late maturing (10 to 15 years).

--- SENTENCE 760 ---
and slow growing (k <0.1 per year).
- (b) The Whale Shark (*Rhincodon typus*), the world's largest living fish, is a cosmopolitan tropical and warm temperate species.

--- SENTENCE 761 ---
It is found in both coastal and oceanic habitats but spends the majority of time in the epipelagic zone.

--- SENTENCE 762 ---
The largest recorded whale shark was approximately 20 m TL and 42 t in mass was reported from Taiwan.

--- SENTENCE 763 ---
Length at first maturity ranges from 7 to 10 m TL.

--- SENTENCE 764 ---
It is an ovoviviparous shark and the smallest freeswimming neonate found in the wild, from the Philippines, was 46 cm TL.

--- SENTENCE 765 ---
Whale Sharks are highly mobile, with mean daily movement rates of 24–28 km.

--- SENTENCE 766 ---
Two major subpopulations exist, in the Atlantic Ocean and Indo-Pacific, respectively.

--- SENTENCE 767 ---
Age and growth data on whale shark are sparse.

--- SENTENCE 768 ---
Growth band deposition estimated that male sharks begin maturing at ~17 years and females at 19–22 years.

--- SENTENCE 769 ---
Generation time is about 25 years (reviewed in Pierce and Norman 2016)
- (c) The tawny shark *N. ferrugineus* is widely distributed in the continental and insular shelves of the Indian, west and central Pacific Oceans.

--- SENTENCE 770 ---
It is found in the shallows water habitat (5 to 30 m) such as lagoons, channels, outer edges of coral and rocky reefs, in areas with seagrass and sand on reefs, sandy areas near reef and off sandy beaches.

--- SENTENCE 771 ---
It also prefers crevices and caves.

--- SENTENCE 772 ---
Although the young tawny shark prefers shallow lagoons, adults are more wide ranging.

--- SENTENCE 773 ---
There is very limited life history data available for the tawny nurse

shark.

--- SENTENCE 774 ---
The maximum size is 320 cm TL.

--- SENTENCE 775 ---
Size of male maturity is 250 cm TL and size of female maturity ranges from 230 to 290 cm TL.

--- SENTENCE 776 ---
There are no data on growth rates (reviewed in Pillans 2003).

- (d) The zebra shark *Stegostoma fasciatum* is a broadly distributed in the continental and insular shelves of the Indian, west and central Pacific Oceans.

--- SENTENCE 777 ---
It is found from the intertidal zone to a depth of 62 m over the continental and insular shelves.

--- SENTENCE 778 ---
They are frequently found in coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 779 ---
Compared to other sharks, they have limited movement (<140 km).

--- SENTENCE 780 ---
The zebra shark attains maximum size at 235 cm TL.

--- SENTENCE 781 ---
It is an oviparous species and hatchlings measure 20 to 36 cm.

--- SENTENCE 782 ---
Sexual maturity is around ranges from 147 to 183 cm TL for males and 169 to 171 cm TL for females.

--- SENTENCE 783 ---
Maximum age is around 25-30 years.

--- SENTENCE 784 ---
(reviewed in Pillans and Simpfendorfer 2003).
- (e) The sicklefin lemon shark *Negaprion acutidens* is widely distributed in the tropical Indo-west and central Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 785 ---
It is usually associated with waters with limited visibility in coral reefs, lagoons and mangrove estuaries and insular shelves up to 92 m.

--- SENTENCE 786 ---
It attains maximum size at 300 cm.

--- SENTENCE 787 ---
Size of male and female sexual maturity is 220 cm TL.

--- SENTENCE 788 ---
It is a viviparous shark.

--- SENTENCE 789 ---
Size at birth is 60 cm TL and growth rate in juveniles is 12.5 to 15.5 cm per year.

--- SENTENCE 790 ---
Tagging experiments showed limited movement with individual movements averaging 1.3 km and maximum distance 5 km.

--- SENTENCE 791 ---
(reviewed in Pillans 2003).
- (f) The grey reef shark *C. amblyrhynchos* is a widespread species occurring in the central Pacific and westwards to the eastern Indian Ocean.

--- SENTENCE 792 ---
It is social species usually common in clear, tropical, coastal waters and oceanic atolls.

--- SENTENCE 793 ---
Its restricted habitat, site fidelity, inshore distribution, small litter size, and relatively late age at maturity, along with increasing fishing pressure suggests that this species may be under threat.

--- SENTENCE 794 ---
The species is found in clear tropical waters from 10 m to more than 50 m around coral reefs, particularly near drop-offs and passes of fringing reefs.

--- SENTENCE 795 ---
It is more common at ancient atolls, and less common at high profile islands with extensive human habitation, or in turbid continental waters.

--- SENTENCE 796 ---
Males mature at 120-140 cm total length (TL) and attain a size of 185 cm; females mature at about 125 cm TL and attain 190 cm at about seven years.

--- SENTENCE 797 ---
Litters are small, up to six embryos (reviewed in Smale 2009).

--- SENTENCE 798 ---
- (g) The blacktip reef shark *C. melanopterus* is a common and wide-ranging species, regularly caught by inshore fisheries.

--- SENTENCE 799 ---
This shark range from 30-50 cm at birth.

--- SENTENCE 800 ---
Adults reach total lengths of up to 180 cm and mature between 90-110 cm.

--- SENTENCE 801 ---
Due to small litter sizes (2-4 pups) and long gestation periods (10-16 months), the blacktip reef shark is vulnerable to depletion (reviewed in Heupel 2009).

--- SENTENCE 802 ---
- (h) The whitetip reef shark *Triaenodon obesus* is closely associated with coral reefs in clear, tropical waters within 10-40 m depth.

--- SENTENCE 803 ---
Primarily nocturnal, they shelter in caves by day, often communally.

--- SENTENCE 804 ---
Maturity is attained at about 105 cm, and pups are born at 52-60 cm after a gestation period of at least five months.

--- SENTENCE 805 ---
Litter size is from 1-5.

--- SENTENCE 806 ---
Growth is slow at 2.1- 4.2 cm year-1, sexual maturity at eight to nine years and life span of 16 years.

--- SENTENCE 807 ---
Maximum size is around 200 cm TL but adults are very rare over 160 cm (reviewed in Smale 2009).
- 6.)

--- SENTENCE 808 ---
Three rays are species of concern: the black-blotched stingray *Taeniura meyeni*; the thorny stingray *Urogymnus asperrimus*, and; the reef manta ray *Manta alfredi*.

--- SENTENCE 809 ---
These fishes share similar life history characteristics and threats with sharks.

--- SENTENCE 810 ---
- (a) The black-blotched stingray *T. meyeni* is one of the largest stingrays and can attain a size of up to 180 cm disk diameter.

--- SENTENCE 811 ---
Little is known of its biology.

--- SENTENCE 812 ---
It is generally nocturnal, bottom-dwelling and found close to shore and up to 20-60 m in depth.

--- SENTENCE 813 ---
It is viviparous and pups measure around 30 cm in disk diameter.

--- SENTENCE 814 ---
Males attain sexual maturity around 100 to 110 cm.

--- SENTENCE 815 ---
The maturity size of females is unknown (reviewed in Kyne and White 2015).

--- SENTENCE 816 ---
- (b) The thorny stingray *U. asperimus* is a bottom-dweller found in sand, coral rubble and seagrass habitats in inshore waters up to a depth of 30 m. It is wide ranging in its distribution but relatively uncommon compared to other stingrays.

--- SENTENCE 817 ---
It attains maximum size of 120 to150 cm disk diameter.

--- SENTENCE 818 ---
It is viviparous and juveniles are found in mangrove areas.

--- SENTENCE 819 ---
Juveniles seem to be site-attached.

--- SENTENCE 820 ---
Sexual maturity is attained at 90 cm diameter size for males and 100 cm diameter sixe for females.

--- SENTENCE 821 ---
Most of its life history data is unknown.

--- SENTENCE 822 ---
Longevity may be 21.5 years based on a related stingray (reviewed in Chin and Compagno 2016).
  - (c) The reef manta ray *M. alfredi* is the second-largest ray known with a maximum known size at 480 disc width.

--- SENTENCE 823 ---
It is found in shallow, coastal habitats in the tropical and subtropical regions of the Pacific, Indian and Atlantic Oceans.

--- SENTENCE 824 ---
They are often encountered feeding and traveling in schools.

--- SENTENCE 825 ---
This manta ray been documented to travel distances of up to 500 km.

--- SENTENCE 826 ---
Although it is wide ranging, populations are sparse and highly fragmented with seemingly low inter-population movement.

--- SENTENCE 827 ---
This species has an extremely low reproductive output.

--- SENTENCE 828 ---
Females bear on average a single pup every 2–3 years.

--- SENTENCE 829 ---
This ray is ovoviviparous and the pup is about 150 cm in disc width staying in a shallow-water environment for a few years.

--- SENTENCE 830 ---
The reef manta seems to be relatively long-lived, living up to 30 years.

--- SENTENCE 831 ---
Female mantas sexually mature around 8 to 10 years and may live up to 40 years.

--- SENTENCE 832 ---
Females mature at 400 cm disc width and males at 300 disc width.

--- SENTENCE 833 ---
Fecundity is very low with one pup per gestation with an annual reproductive cycle (reviewed in Marshall et al. 2011).

--- SENTENCE 834 ---
### <span id="page-54-0"></span>*3.3.2.3 MESOPHOTIC REEF FISHES*

Wass (1984) listed 56 deep-bottom fish species that are found in 60 to 500 m deep in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 835 ---
Several of these fishes are targeted by bottomfishing fishermen.

--- SENTENCE 836 ---
This fishery is characterized by a few commercial boats and numerous unaccounted recreational and subsistence fishery.

--- SENTENCE 837 ---
In Aunuu Island, a few alia bottomfish catch deepwater fish mostly for family social events or *fa'alavelave*.

--- SENTENCE 838 ---
Life histories of most deep-water fishes are characterised by extended longevities, slow growth, late maturity, and low fecundity, indicating low production potential and resilience (Koslow et al. 2000).

--- SENTENCE 839 ---
These deep-water fishes are, therefore, vulnerable to even low levels of fishing.

--- SENTENCE 840 ---
The species of great concern are the ruby snapper *Etelis carbunculus*, the flame snapper *Etelis coruscans* and the pink snapper *Pristopomoides filamentosus*.

--- SENTENCE 841 ---
This group, also known as eteline snappers, inhabits steep coral and rock reefs, residing at depths usually exceeding 100 m, and can attain sizes exceeding 100 cm (Parrish1987).

--- SENTENCE 842 ---
Their geographic range extends throughout the tropical and subtropical Indo-Pacific from the Hawaiian Archipelago and the central western Pacific Ocean to the western Indian Ocean and south along northwestern and northeastern Australia (Allen 1985; Kramer et al. 1994; Newman 2006).

--- SENTENCE 843 ---
There is no data on the life-history of these species in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 844 ---
There are some data collected in other parts of the south Pacific such as Australia, the Marianas and Hawaii.

--- SENTENCE 845 ---
The available data provides a growing evidence of geographic differences in life history traits for these snappers (Andrews et al. 2012).

--- SENTENCE 846 ---
There is also wide concern that available data for deep-water snappers underestimate longevity and that length and age are probably decoupled.

--- SENTENCE 847 ---
Data on size-at-maturity, maximum age and size-at-age are critical for stock assessment and sustainable exploitation of these deep-water fishes.

### <span id="page-54-1"></span>*Threats to marine fishes*

Over-exploitation is the major threat to these fish species of concern.

--- SENTENCE 848 ---
Some of the fishes such as *Cheilinus undulatus* and *Bolbometopon muricatum* are already uncommon so they are susceptible to even low levels of exploitation.

--- SENTENCE 849 ---
Other life history traits such as site fidelity, relatively high maximum age (>30 years), slow growth, relatively late sex maturity, and low fecundity exacerbate the impacts of fishing making the populations of these fishes less resilient.

--- SENTENCE 850 ---
There are no formal assessment of these fishes' vulnerability to climate change.

--- SENTENCE 851 ---
We hypothesize that shallow coral reef fishes may be more susceptible to changes in the climate.

--- SENTENCE 852 ---
Sea-level rise and mortality of corals due to bleaching are expected to affect the habitats of *C. undulatus* and *B. muricatum*.

--- SENTENCE 853 ---
These fishes are mostly found in reefs with high coral cover so increased coral mortality associated with bleaching events is expected to lead to declines in fish abundance.

--- SENTENCE 854 ---
Habitat degradation due to increased sedimentation rates with high rainfall, sea level rise and stronger hurricanes and storms will exacerbate coral reef loss.

### <span id="page-55-0"></span>*Marine fish general conservation priorities*

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 855 ---
The development of scientific framework for catch limits and its implementation will support sustainable exploitation of these highly exploited fish.

--- SENTENCE 856 ---
Such frameworks are being implemented by Regional Fisheries Management Organizations such as the Western and Central Pacific Fisheries Commission and the Western Pacific Regional Fishery Management Council.

--- SENTENCE 857 ---
We propose *to collect life history data such as length-at-first maturity, size-at-age, maximum age, movement and population structure critical for stock assessment models that develop biological reference points for sustainable fisheries*.

--- SENTENCE 858 ---
For fishes with no or extremely low sightings, species prohibitions should be considered.

--- SENTENCE 859 ---
In addition, we propose *to conduct population genetic connectivities for various species among the islands in the Samoan Archipelago*.

--- SENTENCE 860 ---
Data on the genetic structure would complement life history in delineating fish populations and stocks for fisheries management and conservation.

--- SENTENCE 861 ---
We propose *to support an ecosystem and integrated approach to conservation using ridge-to-reef framework* as variously implemented in the Territory by the National Park System, the National Marine Sanctuaries and the Community-Based Fisheries Management Project under the Department of Marine and Wildlife Resources.

--- SENTENCE 862 ---
Such approach is appropriate given that various factors are important in sustaining healthy fish populations.

--- SENTENCE 863 ---
In addition, there is a need *to conduct formal climate change vulnerability assessment for coral reefs will help identify priority sites for conservation*.

### <span id="page-56-0"></span>**4 AN OVERVIEW OF THE HABITATS**

### <span id="page-56-1"></span>**4.1 TERRESTRIAL HABITATS**

### <span id="page-56-2"></span>*4.1.1 VEGETATION TYPES*

Whistler (2002) recognized six main categories of plant communities, of which five can be found on the high volcanic islands in American Samoa: littoral, wetland, upland scrub, disturbed, and rainforest vegetation.

--- SENTENCE 864 ---
Although American Samoa is subject to relatively frequent hurricanes, vegetation patterns suggest these disturbances do not typically affect large areas of forest (Mueller-Dombois & Fosberg 1998, but *cf.*

--- SENTENCE 865 ---
Webb & Fa'aumu 1999).

--- SENTENCE 866 ---
The most extensive natural terrestrial habitat in American Samoa is lowland tropical rainforest (Whistler 1992).

--- SENTENCE 867 ---
Due to the presence of few permanent streams and lakes, wetland habitats are the least extensive.

--- SENTENCE 868 ---
There is significant heterogeneity within these broad habitat categories, which can be differentiated into a large number of habitat subtypes (Whistler 1980; also see Mueller- Dombois & Fosberg for a discussion on habitat subtypes).

--- SENTENCE 869 ---
However, the flora of American Samoa has very low rates of endemism (van Balgooy et al. 1996).

--- SENTENCE 870 ---
The two atolls in the territory are dominated by agroforest with remnants of littoral species (Swains Island; Whistler 1983) or by natural littoral communities (Rose Atoll; Whistler 2002).

### <span id="page-56-3"></span>*4.1.2 HABITAT STATUS AND PROGNOSIS*

The current extent of native habitats in American Samoa compares favorably with the situation found in most tropical countries.

--- SENTENCE 871 ---
However, there is increasing pressure on forest habitats, especially on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 872 ---
The pattern of conversion of habitats into types less suited for wildlife is best modeled in Figure 3, and actual documented changes are illustrated in Figures 4a and b.

--- SENTENCE 873 ---
**The general net trend is toward decreasing habitat quality**.

--- SENTENCE 874 ---
Except in parts of the Tafuna Plain, much of the land converted was previously classed as an agroforest type (Cole et al. 1988).

--- SENTENCE 875 ---
Human population growth and economic development are important drivers in the pattern of land use conversion (American Samoa Government 2002; Department of Commerce 2003), as is the resurgent production of highly profitable, blight-resistant taro (*Colocasia esculenta*) and various leafy vegetables such as *Brassica rapa*.

!

--- SENTENCE 876 ---
**FIGURE 3.** PROJECTED LANDSCAPE CHANGES BASED ON OBSERVED PATTERNS OF LAND USE (Green arrow projects a positive reversal to higher quality habitat; brown arrow represents a loss in habitat quality.

--- SENTENCE 877 ---
D ifference in line thick ness reflect differential rates of conversion between types of habitat with rates proportional to thickness.)

--- SENTENCE 878 ---
A comparison of satellite and aerial images indicate that rainforest areas remain largely intact (Figure 4).

--- SENTENCE 879 ---
Given that approximately 40% of the land area are characterized by slopes 30o or greater, a significant portion of the island remains forested and is projected to remain forested through the foreseeable future.

--- SENTENCE 880 ---
Under a 50-year lease agreement between local villages and the federal government, a combined total of approximately 3,240 ha. of select forested tracts on Tutuila and Ta'u were established as the National Park of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 881 ---
A significant portion of areas with slopes less than 30o consist of tracts of agriculture intermixed with forest patches or tree species (Department of Commerce 2003) and remain suitable habitat for a wide array of vertebrate and invertebrate species.

--- SENTENCE 882 ---
In Manu'a (particularly Ta'u Island), areas of previously cultivated land have been left to fallow and are reverting to natural vegetation (Figure 4b).

--- SENTENCE 883 ---

--- SENTENCE 885 ---
The dark green areas represent native vegetation types, the lighter green areas are disturbed and agroforest areas, while the beige areas are built up or urbanized habitats.

--- SENTENCE 886 ---
The lower map in each figure shows an Ikonos (Space Imaging Systems LLC) satellite image from 2001, partially obscured by scattered clouds.

--- SENTENCE 887 ---
The green cross-hatched areas indicate Cole et al. (1988) habitat classes that have experienced a significant reversion toward native types.

--- SENTENCE 888 ---
The pink cross-hatching indicates approximate areas of habitat classes that experienced significant decrease in suitability or quality for wildlife.

--- SENTENCE 889 ---
These changes are not specific to any one habitat type.

--- SENTENCE 890 ---
For example, native habitat can experience a decrease in suitability by being converted to agroforest, while agroforest can become less suitable by being converted to more residential areas (see Figure 3).

**Figure 4b**

Wetland and riparian habitats have experienced the most severe declines in coverage.

--- SENTENCE 897 ---
It is estimated that at least 48% of the original wetlands has been degraded, and that approximately 450 acres of wetland habitat remains, largely in the Nu'uuli Pala, Leone Pala, and in Aunu'u (Department of Commerce 2003).

--- SENTENCE 898 ---
There are anecdotes that the Aunu'u pala is being degraded by rising sea levels.

--- SENTENCE 899 ---
Of these tracts, the Nu'uuli Pala and Leone wetlands are declared Special Management Areas and as such are afforded protection under the Territorial Coastal Zone Management Plan.

--- SENTENCE 900 ---
Littoral vegetation remains largely in stable and good condition in most islands other than Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 901 ---
On Tutuila, recent development in Fogagogo and Vaitogi have resulted in removal of significant areas of *Pandanus*, *Barringtonia*, and *Callophyllum* (pers.

--- SENTENCE 902 ---
Still, a significant portion of remaining littoral vegetation on Tutuila are along steep coastal slopes, and it is unlikely that development and urban growth in the near future will result affect these areas (although see Section 5.4.4 regarding threats to beaches).

--- SENTENCE 903 ---
Current administrative provisions aimed at slowing down decline in habitat quality, or outright loss of native vegetation cover are inadequate (see subsections 8.2.1 & 8.2.2 for a thorough discussion).

--- SENTENCE 904 ---
Yet it is recognized that the protection and conservation of the Territory's natural vegetation landscape is integral to the conservation of its wildlife populations (see subsection 4.3 below).

--- SENTENCE 905 ---
For this reason, this plan highlights the protection of native habitats, through research & outreach (subsection 6.3), and through legal means (subsection 8.3), as a critical strategy for conservation.

--- SENTENCE 906 ---
In particular, four critical habitat types are defined and designated priority conservation areas fitting these habitat types are indicated in Figure 4.

### <span id="page-61-0"></span>*4.1.3 MAPPING OF WILDLIFE HABITAT IN AMERICAN SAMOA*

Habitat maps offer many benefits to natural resource managers and wildlife biologists in managing wildlife species and their habitat.

--- SENTENCE 907 ---
Habitat maps provide biologists with the ability to spatially quantify wildlife habitat, and to monitor habitat quality and land use change.

--- SENTENCE 908 ---
Further, they can facilitate the inventory and monitoring of wildlife species and provide an important variable for use in habitat suitability and species distribution modeling.

--- SENTENCE 909 ---
Historically, the available aerial and satellite imagery for the American Samoa islands have been highly inadequate for delineation of the extent of the vegetation categories defined by Whistler (2002).

--- SENTENCE 910 ---
Past efforts to generate a land cover map by the Pacific Northwest Research Station Forest Inventory and Analysis (FIA) Program resulted in portions of disturbed vegetation (*sensu* Whistler 2002) characterized as managed lands (agroforest and coconut plantations) and were lumped together with other forest types (undisturbed, disturbed secondary, etc) as part of the "forest" coverage (Donnegan et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 911 ---
Consequently, assessments of the status and prognosis of habitats across the seven islands of American Samoa was limited by the then available information and focused largely on wetlands and rainforests (collectively lowland, montane, cloud forests [including upland scrub]; undisturbed and disturbed).

--- SENTENCE 912 ---
In 2009, DMWR initiated an effort to map wildlife habitat on the islands of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 913 ---
The impetus for this effort originated as a result of the findings of the 2005 CWCS for American Samoa which identified a deficiency in updated and detailed habitat maps for the islands (Utzurrum et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 914 ---
Although the vegetation of American Samoa has been extensively mapped over the past 30 years (Whitesell et al. 1988, Donnegan et al. 2004, Liu et al. 2011)

--- SENTENCE 915 ---
these products are either outdated or too generalized to meet the needs of local wildlife biologists.

--- SENTENCE 916 ---
The goal was to produce detailed and accurate habitat maps using a habitat classification scheme specific to American Samoa that would provide a useful management tool for wildlife biologists.

--- SENTENCE 917 ---
Habitat mapping based on traditional ground surveys typically require time and cost intensive efforts, especially when mapping large areas.

--- SENTENCE 918 ---
Remote sensing data provides an efficient and cost effective method to map large geographical areas (Wulder et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 919 ---
Recent advances in remote sensing sensors and technology are producing imagery with higher spatial and spectral resolutions, providing the ability to map land features with more detail and spatial accuracy.

--- SENTENCE 920 ---
Also, LIDAR (light detection and ranging) is an emerging technology that has been demonstrated to be an effective tool in ecological studies for measuring topography and vegetation height, structure, and cover (Lefsky et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 921 ---
Very high resolution (VHR) orthoimagery and LIDAR data were made available for Tutuila and the Manua Islands in August of 2012.

--- SENTENCE 922 ---
The availability of this high quality remote sensing data provided the opportunity to investigate the synergistic effect of LIDAR and VHR imagery on the ability to produce accurate and detailed habitat maps for the islands.

--- SENTENCE 923 ---
It was anticipated that the incorporation of LIDAR derivatives would improve the ability to map habitats in the classification scheme developed for the project which is largely based on vegetation lifeform and successional stages.

--- SENTENCE 924 ---
The habitat classification scheme combines and integrates remote sensing based classification schemes (Anderson et al. 1976, Dobson et al. 1995) with Whistler's (Whistler 2002) classification of Samoan Rainforest.

--- SENTENCE 925 ---
Habitats were first divided into 8 major life forms which can be extracted from the remote sensing data: developed land, barren land, grassland/herbaceous, shrub/scrub land, forest, water, wetlands and cultivated land.

--- SENTENCE 926 ---
Subsequent levels of the scheme further divide habitats into subtypes based on land use, successional stage, and plant community associations.

--- SENTENCE 927 ---
Table 1 provides the classes defined in the scheme for the habitat mapping project.

### <span id="page-63-0"></span>*TABLE 2. HIERARCHICAL HABITAT CLASSIFICATION SCHEME FOR AMERICAN SAMOA.

Processing methods for producing the habitat maps integrated LIDAR derivatives and VHR orthoimagery (Figures 5 and 6) using image segmentation and object based image analysis (OBIA).

--- SENTENCE 929 ---
Automated classification of image objects using classification tree analysis (CTA) was supplemented with visual interpretation of the imagery to overcome limitations of the remote sensing data and to map habitat classes at a greater level of detail.

--- SENTENCE 930 ---
As part of the mapping effort, ground truth data was collected for training and accuracy assessment purposes.

--- SENTENCE 931 ---
Given the difficulties working on remote islands and the steep, often inaccessible terrain of American Samoa, an attempt was made to create a sample design which minimized sampling effort but adequately sampled the variation in habitats across the islands of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 932 ---
A point centered quarter method with a 10m circular plot was used for collection of canopy, subcanopy and ground cover vegetation metrics.

--- SENTENCE 933 ---
Three general components of the biotic and physical environment were assessed at each ground truth site: forest tree density, habitat composition, and microhabitat characteristics.

--- SENTENCE 934 ---
Approximately 727 ground truth sites across the islands of American Samoa were validated (Figure 7).

--- SENTENCE 935 ---
The ground truth data collected for the project represents one of the largest samples of vegetation collected for the American Samoa islands and will provide biologists with an additional tool for inventory and monitoring of wildlife species, and the ability to minimize sampling effort for rare, threatened or endangered species.

### <span id="page-67-0"></span>*4.1.4 HABITAT AND WILDLIFE:

--- SENTENCE 950 ---
INTERDEPENDENT SYSTEMS*

For many vertebrate species in American Samoa, agricultural crops can be an important resource.

--- SENTENCE 951 ---
Cultivation of traditional plants such as coconut (*Cocos nucifera*), breadfruit (*Artocarpus altilis*), papaya (*Carica papaya*), nonu (*Morinda citrifolia*) and banana (*Musa spp.*) may potentially have increased some wildlife populations, especially bats, nectarivorous birds, and *Aplonis atrifusca* (Utzurrum & Seamon 2001).

--- SENTENCE 952 ---
Clearing for plantations has traditionally occurred in a shifting or rotational scheme, primarily in secondary or disturbed forests (Nunn 2001, Myllyntaus et al. 2002).

--- SENTENCE 953 ---
After initial intensive production, areas cleared for agriculture become fallow and regenerate into heterogeneous forests initially characterized by colonizing species such as *Macaranga harveyana* and *Pipturus argenteus*.

--- SENTENCE 954 ---
Taro and vegetable cultivation, in contrast, is often monocultural and may create areas that are both of little use to wildlife and prone to erosion (*pers.

--- SENTENCE 955 ---
Although even extensively cleared areas will regenerate (e.g., Elmqvist et al. 2001), the absence of other species (such as coconut or remnant native trees) in taro plantations presumably reduces visits by

disperser species, slowing regeneration.

--- SENTENCE 956 ---
Likewise, although residential areas can in theory provide valuable resources for many birds and bats (Rudd et al. 2002, Ricketts & Imhoff 2003), many of the trees planted near dwellings are exotic species of little use to wildlife (*unpub.

--- SENTENCE 957 ---
However, in spite of the utility of agricultural areas to some wildlife species, there can be no substitute for maintaining extensive areas of native forests.

--- SENTENCE 958 ---
This is particularly true for those species with somewhat narrow niches (Swihart et al. 2003).

--- SENTENCE 959 ---
Even generalist species, such as many of American Samoa's native birds, are significantly more abundant in undisturbed native forests (Freifeld 1999), and the extent of deforestation has been shown to be crucial in predicting overall species richness (Steadman et al. 1999).

--- SENTENCE 960 ---
Fruiting and flowering episodes in native species may also cause temporal shifts in animal movements (Webb et al. 1999).

--- SENTENCE 961 ---
Conversely, wildlife are equally important to native habitats and plant species.

--- SENTENCE 962 ---
For example, Rainey et al. (1995) found that interactions among pollinators and plants may structure plant pollination and seed dispersal systems.

--- SENTENCE 963 ---
Although the interactions between plants and animals have been the subject of numerous studies (e.g., see Howe & Westley 1988, Hunter et al. 1992, Fenner 2000 and Levey et al. 2001 for numerous examples from various ecosystems), there may be salient differences in plant-animal interactions between island systems such as American Samoa and more continental systems (Rainey et al. 1995).

--- SENTENCE 964 ---
For example, more than half of the native floras of Samoa are species dispersed by birds (Carlquist 1996).

--- SENTENCE 965 ---
Many of the frugivores in American Samoa are generalist consumers of native fruits (e.g., Trail 1994, Webb et al. 1999), and elsewhere birds and bats have been shown to be instrumental in colonizing new islands (Shanahan et al. 2001).

--- SENTENCE 966 ---
The conservation of this native fauna was predicted by Webb & Fa'aumu (1999) to be "essential in retaining the potential for regeneration of native forest after large- scale disturbance."

--- SENTENCE 967 ---
Recent studies have indeed shown the crucial importance of wildlife in facilitating regeneration from the catastrophic disturbances that characterize this ecosystem (Elmqvist et al. 2001, Hjerpe et al. 2001).

--- SENTENCE 968 ---
These crucial interdependencies between plants and animals must therefore guide our approach to conservation in the territory.

--- SENTENCE 969 ---
In most cases, we have attempted a two-tiered approach to conservation, focusing both on characteristics of the species of interest, and on its habitat associations.

--- SENTENCE 970 ---
Only by obtaining adequate information on both aspects of a species ecology can we make truly informed management decisions.

--- SENTENCE 971 ---
### <span id="page-69-0"></span>**4.2 MARINE HABITATS**

American Samoa has a land area of 197 km2 distributed among 5 islands and 434,503 km2 of EEZ (0-200 nm shoreward).

--- SENTENCE 972 ---
The ratio of land to sea is 0.045% so over 99% of the Territory is oceanic water.

--- SENTENCE 973 ---
This emphasizes the importance of the marine habitats in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 974 ---
Fringing the islands is an estimated 291.7 km2 of coral reef area.

--- SENTENCE 975 ---
Most of these reef ecosystem is located in Tutuila Island (243 km2 or 83%).

--- SENTENCE 976 ---
The banks have at least 35 km2 of reef area.

--- SENTENCE 977 ---
The reef area has been estimated by both satellite image analyses and is limited only up to 30 m water depth resolution.

--- SENTENCE 978 ---
Mesophotic reefs occur in 30 to 150 m water depths so reef areas are underestimated.

--- SENTENCE 979 ---
The underestimation must be significant.

--- SENTENCE 980 ---
For instance, the submerged shelf around Tutuila covers an area of 358 km2, but 86% of this shelf is at depths below 30 m. The Manua Islands have much smaller seafloor area from 30 to 150 m depth: 23 km2 for Ofu-Olosega (66%) and 10 km2 in Tau (50%).

### <span id="page-69-1"></span>*4.2.1 SHALLOW-WATER CORAL REEFS*

The status of the coral reefs in the Territory have been monitored by the NOAA Coral Reef Division since 2004.

--- SENTENCE 981 ---
The data collected are percent cover of hard corals, frondose algae, and crustose coralline algae.

--- SENTENCE 982 ---
The analyses indicated relatively stable cover of these benthic groups from 2004 to 2010 with surveys conducted every two years (PIFSC 2011).

--- SENTENCE 983 ---
Hard coral cover ranged from 20 to 30% with the following overall-ranking: Swains (31%)> Tutuila (21%)

--- SENTENCE 984 ---
= Tau (21%) >

--- SENTENCE 985 ---
Ofu & Olosega (20%) >

--- SENTENCE 986 ---
These values are within the average to the upper range of percent hard coral cover published in the south Pacific (Doug Fenner, pers comm.).

--- SENTENCE 987 ---
There was a reported moderate increase in live coral cover in Tutuila, Ofu and Olosega from 2004 to 2010.

--- SENTENCE 988 ---
