These increases have been attributed to coral cover recovery after various disturbances such as bleaching events in 2002 and 2003 and tropical storms.

--- SENTENCE 989 ---
Changes in the cover of the frondose algae and crustose corraline algae are relatively faster compared to live coral cover.

--- SENTENCE 990 ---
Algae are some of the fastest growing organisms that can quickly track changes in the environment perhaps owing to their non-mineralized structures.

--- SENTENCE 991 ---
Changes in algal cover have been attributed to changes in temperature, wave energy, nutrient availability, herbivore grazing pressure and also allelopathy (interactions with other sessile organisms).

--- SENTENCE 992 ---
For instance, there were sharp declines recorded for the cover of the macroalgae, turf algae and crustose coralline algae in response to the severe and widespread outbreak of a tunicate that affected Swains coral reef (Vargas-Angel et al. 2009).

--- SENTENCE 993 ---
Frondose algae and crustose coralline algae later recovered after the tunicate cover declined.

--- SENTENCE 994 ---
Rose Atoll is an isolated and unique coral reef ecosystem because it is geomorphologically distinct from the other volcanic islands in the archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 995 ---
The coral reef benthos in the forereef slopes are dominated by the encrusting coralline red algae.

--- SENTENCE 996 ---
Crustose coralline algae cover is twice the hard coral cover and has been relatively stable across years.

--- SENTENCE 997 ---
Coral diversity is high with more than 300 species recorded belonging to 60 scleractinian and hydrozoan genera (Birkeland et al. 1987, Green et al. 1999, DiDonato et al. 2005).

--- SENTENCE 998 ---
There was a positive correlation between the number of coral genera and the size of the island consistent with the theory of island biogeography (MacArthur and Wilson 1967).

### <span id="page-70-0"></span>*4.2.2 MESOPHOTIC REEFS*

Deep, zooxanthellate, scleractinian coral reefs have been documented in both the Pacific and Atlantic Oceans and often occur around islands in clear tropical oceanic waters (Lang 1974;

--- SENTENCE 999 ---
Fricke and Meischner 1985; Reed 1985; Kahng and Maragos 2006).

--- SENTENCE 1000 ---
These mesophotic coral ecosystems are found at depths of 30 to 40 m up to 150 m and have been exploited by bottomfishing fishermen mainly targeting snappers and emperors.

--- SENTENCE 1001 ---
Fishermen have reported catching eteline snappers up to 300 m depth.

--- SENTENCE 1002 ---
These ecosystems have been previously unstudied as they were beyond the capacity of regular scientific scuba diving.

--- SENTENCE 1003 ---
But the recent availability of new technology and equipment such as drop cameras, remotely operated vehicles, autonomous underwater vehicles, submersibles, and advancements in technical scuba diving practices, have opened up these previously inaccessible depths to study these deeper zooxanthellate reefs (Menza et al. 2007).

--- SENTENCE 1004 ---
Some surveys conducted in in Tutuila Island have shown evidence of flourishing coral reefs in mesophotic depths.

--- SENTENCE 1005 ---
Most of these mesophotic coral reef ecosystems are found on midshelf patch reefs and on banks around the periphery of the shelf.

--- SENTENCE 1006 ---
Coral cover was highest at 30-40 m depths and declined with increasing depths.

--- SENTENCE 1007 ---
Corals were observed as deep as 102 m. There were patterns of changes in the morphology across the surveyed depth ranges.

--- SENTENCE 1008 ---
Encrusting and massive corals were dominant in 30-40 m depths while plate-like and branching corals were dominant in 40-90 m depths.

--- SENTENCE 1009 ---
An understanding of mesophotic reefs is important since these ecosystems may serve as refugia and larval source for the shallow ecosystems (Glynn 1996; Reigl and Piller 2003).

--- SENTENCE 1010 ---
Mesophotic coral reefs will probably not experience sedimentation, eutrophication, heat-related bleaching events nor tropical storms and cyclones to the level and extent that impact shallow coral reef ecosystems.

--- SENTENCE 1011 ---
### <span id="page-70-1"></span>*4.2.3 OCEANIC REALM*

Pelagic species are closely associated with their physical and chemical environments.

--- SENTENCE 1012 ---
The physical environment depends on gradients in temperature, oxygen, or salinity, all of which are influenced by oceanic conditions on various scales.

--- SENTENCE 1013 ---
Isotherm and isohaline boundaries often determine whether the surrounding water mass is suitable for pelagic fish.

--- SENTENCE 1014 ---
Fronts and eddies are important areas of congregation for different trophic levels for foraging, migration, and reproduction for many species (Bakun 1996).

--- SENTENCE 1015 ---
Skipjack, yellowfin tuna and blue marlin prefer warm surface layers, where the water is well mixed by surface winds and is relatively uniform in temperature and salinity.

--- SENTENCE 1016 ---
Albacore, bigeye tuna, striped marlin, and swordfish, prefer cooler, more temperate waters, in higher latitudes or greater depths.

--- SENTENCE 1017 ---
Preferred water temperature often varies with the size and maturity of pelagic fish, and adults usually have a wider temperature tolerance than subadults.

--- SENTENCE 1018 ---
During spawning, adults of many pelagic species usually move to warmer waters, the preferred habitat of their larval and juvenile stages.

--- SENTENCE 1019 ---
Large-scale oceanographic events (such as El Niño) change the characteristics of water temperature and productivity across the Pacific, and these events have a significant effect on the habitat range and movements of pelagic species.

--- SENTENCE 1020 ---
Tuna are commonly most concentrated near islands and seamounts that create divergences and convergences, which concentrate forage species, and also near upwelling zones along ocean current boundaries and along gradients in temperature, oxygen, and salinity.

--- SENTENCE 1021 ---
Swordfish and numerous other pelagic species tend to concentrate along food-rich temperature fronts between cold upwelled water and warmer oceanic water masses (NMFS 2001).

--- SENTENCE 1022 ---
For instance, Domokos et al. (2007) showed that south Pacific albacore catch rates in longlines were higher in boundaries of mesoscale eddies caused by the South Equatorial Current and South Equatorial Countercurrent.

--- SENTENCE 1023 ---
These areas of higher horizontal shears are associated with higher micronekton abundance and thus constitute as significant foraging areas of the south Pacific albacore.

### <span id="page-72-0"></span>**5 SPECIES OF GREATEST CONSERVATION CONCERN**

For the terrestrial wildlife, native species 1) whose conservation status are unknown (Conservation Class I), 2) known to the extent that they are considered to be of concern by virtue of threat and/or small population sizes (Conservation Class II), or 3) considered high priority in the past and are currently the focus of on-going research, conservation, & management efforts (Conservation Class III) include several invertebrates (a butterfly, a land crab, and endemic terrestrial snails), five reptiles (2 marine turtles, a snake, and 2 skinks), nine birds, and a variety of mammals (3 bats, and cetaceans) (see Table 2 for a listing).

--- SENTENCE 1024 ---
For the marine species, we prioritized species of greatest conservation concern only classified under Conservation Class I and II, with high data reliability and low abundance.

--- SENTENCE 1025 ---
These species included three giant clams, three sea cucumbers (holothurians), six hard corals, and three eteline snappers.

--- SENTENCE 1026 ---
With the exception of a few that are largely unknown, these species or species groups are presented in this Section in greater detail.

--- SENTENCE 1027 ---
Abundances, distribution, and threats to each are provided to the extent that data are available.

--- SENTENCE 1028 ---
Priority actions deemed essential for the conservation or future conservation planning are enumerated at the end of each descriptive subsection.

--- SENTENCE 1029 ---
The conservation needs of those poorly known species excluded from this Section but deserving priority attention have been outlined in the overviews presented in Section 3.

### <span id="page-73-0"></span>**5.1** *Papilio godeffroyi*

!

--- SENTENCE 1030 ---
[](_page_73_Picture_3.jpeg)

Local common name: Unknown

Distribution: endemic to Samoan Archipelago

Abundance: uncommon to moderately common

Laboratory-hatched *P. godeffroyi*

--- SENTENCE 1031 ---
(M. Schmaedick, ASCC/Land Grant)

The Samoan swallowtail butterfly, *Papilio godeffroyi*, appears to be a single-island endemic on Tutuila Island, reduced from its former range which included Upolu and Savai'i.

--- SENTENCE 1032 ---
Despite its conspicuous appearance, the butterfly has remained largely unnoticed in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 1033 ---
Larvae occur on *Micromelum minutum* (N. Gurr, *pers.

--- SENTENCE 1034 ---
comm*.), a relatively common plant species in secondary forest understory.

--- SENTENCE 1035 ---
It is possible that other species in the Rutaceae are also food plants for the larvae but this has not been studied in American Samoa (M. Schmaedick, *pers.

--- SENTENCE 1036 ---
A research collaboration between DMWR, NPS, USGS, and ASCC Land Grant evaluated parasitism on *P. godeffroyi* immature stages and documented habitat variables at field sites on Tutuila Island (R. Peck et al. unpublished data).

--- SENTENCE 1037 ---
The butterfly was found to be not rare, but present at low densities at secondary forest sites in the east, west, and central areas of the island, both inside and outside the National Park.

--- SENTENCE 1038 ---
Although analysis continues, eggs were found to be attacked at a rather high rate by an undescribed parasitoid species.

--- SENTENCE 1039 ---
The parasitoid may be native, as early reports from the 1800s also noted high egg parasitism of *P. godeffroyi.

--- SENTENCE 1040 ---
* 

### <span id="page-73-1"></span>*5.1.1 THREATS*

Specific threats are difficult to identify owing to the lack of information on the butterfly's biology in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 1041 ---
It is unlikely that food is a limiting factor given the abundance of potential food plants.

--- SENTENCE 1042 ---
It is unclear whether parasitism is a threat or perhaps even plays a stabilizing role by preventing overexploitation of host plants.

--- SENTENCE 1043 ---
The most immediate threats might be overcollecting for the lucrative price the butterfly may fetch in the collectors' market and/or possibly predators such as introduced exotic ants.

### <span id="page-73-2"></span>*5.1.2 CONSERVATION PRIORITIES*

The dearth of information on the species requires that priorities be set to:

- 1. *continue to document distribution* among and within islands of the Territory, including any information on habitat-specificity;
- 2. *develop and implement a monitoring protocol*
- 3. *develop a captive rearing capacity locally to facilitate research on predation and host preferences and to prepare for possible re-introduction into areas of Upolu and Savai'i where it has gone extinct*
- 4. *continue to assess natural threats*, such as predators and parasitoids on Tutuila Island and on Upolu and Savai'i if the butterfly is re-introduced there.

--- SENTENCE 1044 ---
When sufficient baseline information on numbers, distribution, and basic biological needs and threats shall have been collected, a species-specific conservation plan should be developed given the potential commercial interest in the species.

### <span id="page-75-0"></span>**5.2 THE COCONUT CRAB,** *Birgus latro*

!

--- SENTENCE 1045 ---
[](_page_75_Picture_3.jpeg)

Coconut crab for sale at the Fagatogo market (Photo by DF Nyhagen)

Local common name: u'u

Distribution: widespread in Oceania & within American Samoa

Abundance: common (immature) to uncommon

(adults)

--- SENTENCE 1046 ---
Status: not federally listed; locally protected

The coconut crab, *Birgus latro*, has been a particularly frequent subject of study elsewhere in Oceania (Fletcher et al. 1990, Kadiri-Jan & Chauvet 1998, Chauvet & Kadiri-Jan 1999, Brown & Fielder 1991).

--- SENTENCE 1047 ---
It is found on all the islands of American Samoa (except Rose Atoll), but is abundant primarily on Swains island (*pers.

--- SENTENCE 1048 ---
Despite its cultural importance as a food resource, there have been no studies conducted on the local species, and estimates of their numbers and information on the extent of their distributional (habitat) range in the Territory are lacking.

### <span id="page-75-1"></span>*5.2.1 THREATS*

Specific threats to the long-term health and viability of populations of coconut crabs in the Territory are unknown due to the lack of studies on the species.

--- SENTENCE 1049 ---
The most logical threats to the species are unregulated harvesting and loss of habitat, and these factors should be investigated.

--- SENTENCE 1050 ---
Subsistence and low-level commercial harvest pose a threat to the species, particularly when harvest is biased towards reproductive adults.

--- SENTENCE 1051 ---
Although existing regulations limit the harvest of crabs by size and reproductive condition (American Samoa Administrative Code Title 24, Chapter 09), it is suspected that a substantial amount of crabs harvested for subsistence and informal sale may violate restrictions.

--- SENTENCE 1052 ---
Anecdotal information from locals who habitually hunt consistently report rocky coastal areas as likely areas for finding coconut crabs.

--- SENTENCE 1053 ---
In Tutuila, rocky coastal plains, particularly in the Tafuna area, are increasingly being bulldozed for housing and other developments.

--- SENTENCE 1054 ---
If, in fact, this type of substrate is important habitat for the species, then continuing developments may have a significant impact on the species, or a developmental stage of the species.

### <span id="page-76-0"></span>*5.2.2 CONSERVATION PRIORITIES*

Based on the minimum information available on the status of and threats to the species, the following priority actions are recommended to enable better conservation planning for species:

1) *assessment of the population and distributional status* in American Samoa

Measures are in place to regulate the take of *B. latro* throughout the Territory, including a prohibition against the take of gravid females.

--- SENTENCE 1055 ---
However, we have insufficient evidence to determine if these regulations are adequate to maintain a healthy population of crabs, or to determine trajectories in abundances through time.

--- SENTENCE 1056 ---
Once initial estimates of population abundance and distributional data become available, it will be possible to derive specific hypotheses pertinent to management decisions.

--- SENTENCE 1057 ---
For example, it might be important to determine which possible causative factors predict heterogeneity in spatial patterns of *B. latro* abundance;

- 2) *research the reproductive ecology of the species*, and
- 3) *conduct a feasibility study on subsistence and commercial farming* of cococutcrabs

Information on the species' ecological requirements, particularly for successful reproduction is vital for management of the species.

--- SENTENCE 1058 ---
Given the cultural interest in the species as a food item, farming of the crabs (whether for subsistence or commercial use) may alleviate pressure on populations in the wild.

--- SENTENCE 1059 ---
As supporting information, it will be beneficial to *systematically compile harvest information* in cooperation with villages.

--- SENTENCE 1060 ---
The information can be used to develop a profile on levels of take, sizes taken, and areas of collection.

--- SENTENCE 1061 ---
Information on methods used for harvest could also be useful when developing a management plan.

### <span id="page-77-0"></span>**5.3 ENDEMIC TERRESTRIAL SNAILS**

!

--- SENTENCE 1062 ---
*Eua* sp. and *Trochomorpha apia*  (Photo used with permission from R. Cowie)

Local common name: sisi

Distribution: endemic to Samoan

Archipelago

Abundance: deemed rare

Status: ESA Endangered Species

Two species of terrestrial gastropods are currently listed as endangered under the US Endangered Species Act: *Eua zebrina* and *Ostodes strigatus*.

--- SENTENCE 1063 ---
Six others are categorized as species of concern: *Diastole matafaoi*, *D. schmeltziana*, *Samoana abbreviata, S. conica*, *S. thurstoni* and *Trochomorpha apia*.

--- SENTENCE 1064 ---
The snail fauna of the Pacific has been well-studied (Cowie 1996 and references therein), but primarily with a view to documenting distributions and diversity from given islands or archipelagos, rather than providing a basis for monitoring and conservation (e.g., Cowie & Cook 1998, Cowie & Rundell 2002, Cowie et al. 2002).

--- SENTENCE 1065 ---
However, these data have been sufficient to detect significant long-term declines in many species (Cowie 2001a, Cowie & Cook 2001).

### <span id="page-77-1"></span>*5.3.1 THREATS*

By far the most significant threat to native snail faunas is posed by introduced species of snails (Cowie 2001b).

--- SENTENCE 1066 ---
Although the efficacy of predatory species for the biocontrol of previously introduced deleterious species such as the African snail *(Achatina fullica*) was largely undocumented, several species have been introduced to the Samoan Archipelago precisely for this reason (see Lydeard et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 1067 ---
The most significant introduced predatory snail may be *Euglandina rosea*, but there are at least 25 other introduced species that may be important predators or competitors (Cowie 2001b).

--- SENTENCE 1068 ---
The predatory flatworm *Platydemus manokwari* could also be disastrous for native species (Cowie & Robinson 2002).

--- SENTENCE 1069 ---
The flatworm was introduced to Samoa to control introduced predatory snails, but itself has become a threat to the native snails.

--- SENTENCE 1070 ---
It is reported that this flatworm is now common on Tutuila, although it has not been reported from other American Samoa islands (Cowie 2005; P. Craig pers.

--- SENTENCE 1071 ---
Finally, for snail species with critically restricted

distributions (e.g., *S. abbreviata*, Cowie & Cook 2001), even small-scale habitat loss is a potential problem.

### <span id="page-78-0"></span>*5.3.2 CONSERVATION PRIORITIES*

--- SENTENCE 1072 ---
Due to the many threats faced by the remaining native snails in the territory, our immediate priorities are:

- 1) *exhaustively document the within-island distributions* of the snails within and among habitats;
- 2) *identify critical ecological linkages between species and habitat factors*

--- SENTENCE 1073 ---
(e.g. microclimate; substrate, plant species); and
- 3) *develop and implement a monitoring program* to determine population performance and trends through time.

--- SENTENCE 1074 ---
Accurate distribution maps would allow focused conservation efforts within small but critically important areas, perhaps by the exclusion of introduced species.

--- SENTENCE 1075 ---
It would also determine the presence of any refugia within which some or all invasives are absent.

--- SENTENCE 1076 ---
Again, measures could then be taken to reduce the chance of immigration in to these refugi by known invasives.

--- SENTENCE 1077 ---
The monitoring program should be designed within an ecological framework.

--- SENTENCE 1078 ---
This would enable identification of factors that may be impacting the species.

--- SENTENCE 1079 ---
The second major priority is to **improve quarantine methods**, both between American Samoa and Samoa to its west, and within American Samoa between Tutuila and the more pristine Manu'a islands to the east.

--- SENTENCE 1080 ---
It is imperative that *P. manokwari* not be introduced from Samoa, and the frequent shipment of root crops such as taro greatly increases the risk of such an accidental introduction.

--- SENTENCE 1081 ---
If indeed *P. manokwari* is already in Tutuila, it is imperative that quarantine measures are observed to precent its spread to other islands.

--- SENTENCE 1082 ---
Likewise, it is critically important that *E. rosea* not be introduced into Manu'a.

--- SENTENCE 1083 ---
These priorities echo a few of the conservation actions outlined in Cowie (2004), and should set the stage for more in-depth treatment in subsequent updates of this strategy.

### <span id="page-79-0"></span>**5.4 MARINE TURTLES**

!

--- SENTENCE 1084 ---
Local common name: laumei

Distribution: throughout the Pacific

Abundance: uncommon in American Samoa Status:

*E. imbricata* – ESA Endangered Species;

*C. mydas* – ESA Endangered Species

Two species of marine turtle, the federally endangered hawksbill turtle (*Eretmochelys imbricata*) and the federally endangered green turtle (*Chelonia mydas*), are known to nest on American Samoan beaches and regularly occur on the inshore reefs in the territory (Tuato'o-Bartley et al. 1993).

--- SENTENCE 1085 ---
Territorial beaches are known nesting areas for both of these species (MacDonald 2015) and juvenile hawksbills and greens are frequently observed in coastal waters where they forage (Grant et al. 1997a).

--- SENTENCE 1086 ---
Serious declines in Pacific populations of sea turtles and a lack of basic biological information on turtles in our region make marine turtle conservation a top priority (Craig 2002).

--- SENTENCE 1087 ---
The protection of sea turtles and their habitats (i.e., nesting beaches and foraging areas) is critical to the preservation of populations inhabiting the territory and also forestalling the global extinction of these two species.

!

--- SENTENCE 1088 ---
[](_page_79_Picture_10.jpeg)

Green turtle on reef at Swain's Island, American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1089 ---
Photo by M.A. MacDonald.

### <span id="page-80-0"></span>*5.4.1 ABUNDANCE*

Abundance estimates of American Samoa's sea turtle populations are difficult to make due to a dearth of data for the territory.

--- SENTENCE 1090 ---
Ofu-Olosega have been monitored extensively since 2013 yielding avg estimates of ~10 breeding hawksbills and <5 breeding greens (MacDonald 2015).

--- SENTENCE 1091 ---
Continued monitoring over the next several years will be essential in refining these number to better describe the situation in Ofu-Olosega but these preliminary estimates show the breeding populations of both species to be extremely low.

--- SENTENCE 1092 ---
A comprehensive nesting beach monitoring programme for Tutuila is not feasible given the spread out nature of the beaches and low activity observed.

--- SENTENCE 1093 ---
Nest estimates for these beaches, obtained through detailed interviews and reports from the villages, suggest each beach only sees 1-2 nesters a season.

--- SENTENCE 1094 ---
A conservative estimate for Tutuila nesters, therefore, can be put at somewhere < 50.

### <span id="page-80-1"></span>*5.4.2 DISTRIBUTION*

Sea turtles have been reported in coastal waters and on beaches of all islands in American Samoa, with most reports coming from the most populous island, Tutuila (Utzurrum 2002).

--- SENTENCE 1095 ---
On Tutuila, the majority of sea turtle sightings occur in Pala Lagoon (Lion's Park), Faga'alu Park, and Gataivai although high densities of juvenile recruits can be found on nearly any of the island's reefs (DMWR data).

--- SENTENCE 1096 ---
Figure 9 shows the locations at which turtle sightings were reported during the first eight months of 2005.

--- SENTENCE 1097 ---
Beaches with nesting potential (i.e., sandy areas not inundated by high tide) occur on all aspects of Tutuila and on at least one beach of every island in the territory.

--- SENTENCE 1098 ---
Most of the 11 beaches on Tutuila for which nesting activity has been reported arelocated on the eastern half of the island (DMWR data)

--- SENTENCE 1099 ---
Ofu-Olosega have several confirmed nesting beaches for both greens and hawkbills with the heaviest activity located on Ofu Island (MacDonald 2015).

--- SENTENCE 1100 ---
Ta'u has confirmed nesting for hawksbill turtles on the eastern beach although no formal surveying has been done to date.

--- SENTENCE 1101 ---
Rose Atoll is a confirmed green turtle nesting site with relatively high nesting activity as well on Swain's Island (Craig et al. 2004).

!

--- SENTENCE 1102 ---
[](_page_81_Figure_2.jpeg)

<span id="page-81-1"></span>**FIGURE 9.

--- SENTENCE 1103 ---
** MARINE TURTLE HABITAT ON TUTUILA ISLAND.

--- SENTENCE 1104 ---
### <span id="page-81-0"></span>*5.4.3 KEY HABITATS*

Key habitat for sea turtles includes both potential foraging areas as well as known or potential nesting beaches.

--- SENTENCE 1105 ---
In addition to confirmed hawksbill nesting beaches onTutuila, several potential nesting beaches where extensive sandy areas are notinundated by high tide waters are available (DMWR data).

--- SENTENCE 1106 ---
Although it appears that the selection of nesting beaches may have a geographical basis (i.e., areas at the eastern side of Tutuila) in the territory, further research is needed to determine which beach characteristics affect nesting beach selection.

--- SENTENCE 1107 ---
The limited number of confirmed and potential nesting beaches on Tutuila necessitates extensive protection of all remaining undisturbed habitat to unsure continued breeding on the island.

--- SENTENCE 1108 ---
Based on dietary habits, near shore areas with high coral and seagrass cover are the most probable foraging grounds for hawksbill and green turtles.

--- SENTENCE 1109 ---
Limited data from in-water surveys and incidental sightings, however, indicate that hawksbills are present throughout the surrounding waters of Tutuila Is.

--- SENTENCE 1110 ---
An in-water programme is needed to determine whether certain areas are preferred over others and enable designation of select sectors for management as critical foraging areas.

--- SENTENCE 1111 ---
An analysis of migratory patterns for Manu'a hawksbills and green turtles was undertaken during the 2014/2015 nesting season.

--- SENTENCE 1112 ---
Three hawksbill turtles and two greens were tagged on Ofu Island in the early months of 2015.

--- SENTENCE 1113 ---
Results indicate that green turtle migratory routes from Manu'a to the foraging grounds follow closely those previously recorded from Rose Atoll although the Great Barrier reef as a foraging ground is a new observation.

--- SENTENCE 1114 ---
Hawksbill turtles from Ofu were shown to have radically different migration patterns from those of greens and an increased deployment of tags is required to better identify routes and foraging grounds.

!

--- SENTENCE 1115 ---
[](_page_82_Picture_4.jpeg)

Deployment of a satellite tag on a female Green Turtle.

--- SENTENCE 1116 ---
Toaga beach, Ofu Island, American Samoa, Feb.-2015.

--- SENTENCE 1117 ---
Photo by M.A. MacDonald.

!

--- SENTENCE 1118 ---
THE MIGRATION ROUTES OF 4 TURTLES SATELLITE TAGGED ON OFU.

### <span id="page-84-0"></span>*5.4.4 THREATS*

The construction of seawalls for protection of coastal roads and village infrastructure is a major threat to potential nesting beaches.

--- SENTENCE 1119 ---
Majority of these structures were installed following extensive damages to coastal roads in the aftermath of Hurricanes Ofa and Val in 1991 and 1992, respectively.

--- SENTENCE 1120 ---
Additional construction followed hurricanes in 2004 and 2005.

--- SENTENCE 1121 ---
The areas under increasing threat are confined to portions of the islands were villages and coastal infrastructures arefound in Tutuila and Manu'a.

--- SENTENCE 1122 ---
In Tutuila, any future road development and shoreline revetment (such as a seawall) particularly along the southeastern sector of the island must be reviewed closely as these are areas of known nesting beaches.

--- SENTENCE 1123 ---
Other, more insidious threats to the habitat itself include sand-mining and associated construction projects, inundation with freshwater runoff during floods, and pollution (Witherington 1999).

--- SENTENCE 1124 ---
Shoreline activities may also degrade foraging habitats by indirectly altering the seafloor composition and available food items at those sites (Gibson & Smith 1999).

--- SENTENCE 1125 ---
Direct threats to nesting turtles and hatchlings include light pollution, traffic on roads accessible to turtles, predation, and poaching of eggs and adults (Boulon 1999).

--- SENTENCE 1126 ---
Turtles frequenting territorial waters (both coastal and pelagic) are at greatest risk from poachers and incidental catch in longline fisheries (Craig 2002).

--- SENTENCE 1127 ---
Although the harvest of eggs and turtles are illegal under Territorial and federal laws occasional reports of poaching are not uncommon (DMWR Enforcement/Conservation logs).

--- SENTENCE 1128 ---
Derelict fishing gear has been shown to pose a significant threat to resident turtles on the reefs of Tutuila (MacDonald 2015).

--- SENTENCE 1129 ---
A full 25% of all stranding responses to dead turtles found the cause of death to be related to hooks and monofilament entanglement making this issue the number one threat faced by turtles foraging on reef flats around the island.

--- SENTENCE 1130 ---
American Samoa is fortunate not to be plagued by the ghost gear from industrial fishing seen elsewhere yet the risk of entanglement from derelict line from local fishers needs to be addressed.

--- SENTENCE 1131 ---
Direct hooking of turtles appears to be rare.

--- SENTENCE 1132 ---
Rather, line and hook cut after becoming snagged on the reef are those that are responsible for the majority of entanglements observed.

--- SENTENCE 1133 ---
A programme to reduce the amount of these types of materials left on the reef is critical.

--- SENTENCE 1134 ---
Satellite tracking of green and hawksbill turtles nesting in Ofu Island and Rose Atoll demonstrates that these threats are not limited to American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1135 ---
The migratory habit of these animals expose them to predation by humans in other countries where regulations and restrictions on takes are not as stringent as in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1136 ---
Indeed, the transnational habits of sea turtles necessitate the immediate implementation of a regional recovery plan (Craig 2002).

### <span id="page-85-0"></span>*5.4.5 CONSERVATION PRIORITIES*

A territorial sea turtle recovery plan was drafted in 1999 (AS Sea Turtle Recovery Team 1999) followed by an executive order from American Samoa's governor establishing the territorial waters as sanctuary for sea turtles and marine mammals in 2003 (American Samoa Government 2003:

--- SENTENCE 1137 ---
Thesedocuments in addition to the USFWS recovery plans for Pacific populations of both green sea turtles and hawksbill sea turtles (NMFS 1998) outline American Samoa's sea trtle research needs.

--- SENTENCE 1138 ---
DMWR along with other local recovery team members has initiated a sea turtle research program aimed at implementing the needs identified in the draft recovery plan.

--- SENTENCE 1139 ---
Among the identified conservation needs being acted upon are: 1) *mapping local distributions*; 2) *physical characterization of documented and potential nesting beaches* to be used in the mitigation of threats to nesting sea turtles, nests, and hatchlings; 3) *determination of genetic stocks* to aid in transboundary management; 4) *documentation of migration routes* (with focus on the hawksbill sea turtle); 5) *identification of keyforaging areas*; and 6) *increasing public awareness on conservation issues* affectinglocal populations of sea turtles (NOAA Unallied Management Grants to DMWR: 2004 to present).

--- SENTENCE 1140 ---
Future research will be needed to establish reliable estimates of population size and longterm data sets from which population trends can be determined.

--- SENTENCE 1141 ---
Also, theAmerican Samoa Sea Turtle Recovery Team need to revisit the draft conservationstrategy for the territory, assess what objectives have been accomplished, and identify pragmatic means by which unaccomplished priority objectives can be accomplished.

--- SENTENCE 1142 ---
Although a regional conservation strategy has apparently been drafted (Craig 2002;SPREP Marine Turtle Conservation Action Plan 2002-2007), its implementation appears to be behind schedule.

--- SENTENCE 1143 ---
Communication networks must be re-established inorder to implement a regional conservation plan.

--- SENTENCE 1144 ---
The celebration of the Year of the Sea Turtle in 2006 provides an excellent impetus to reestablish communication lines among nations and readdress the implementation of a regional conservation strategy.

### <span id="page-86-0"></span>**5.5 TERRESTRIAL REPTILES OF CONCERN**

!

--- SENTENCE 1145 ---
[](_page_86_Picture_3.jpeg)

*Candoia bibroni* (Pacific Boa) Local common name: gata

Distribution: throughout the region;

restricted in American Samoa

Abundance: rare

Status: unknown, but maybe in decline;

locally protected

Photo by Mark Macdonald

*Cryptoblepharus poeciloplurus* and *Emoia lawesi* (Snake-eyed and Lawes skinks)

Local common name: pili and pili oua' Distribution: restricted in American Samoa

Abundance: presumed rare

Status: unknown; locally protected

Three species of reptiles are deserving attention and very little is known of their current status in American Samoa except that they are rare and of restricted distribution.

--- SENTENCE 1146 ---
These are the Pacific boa (*Candoia bibroni*) and two species of skinks (*Cryptoblepharus poeciloplurus* and *Emoia lawesi*).

--- SENTENCE 1147 ---
The Pacific Boa is currently known only in Ta'u, although there is fossil evidence of its occurrence in Tutuila (Steadman and Pregill 2004).

--- SENTENCE 1148 ---
A brief summary of threats to these species, and of baseline actions that can enable a preliminary assessment of their statuses are presented in this section.

### <span id="page-86-1"></span>*5.5.1 THREATS*

Major threats to the Pacific boa and the two skinks (Snake-eyed and Lawes) are likely to be loss of habitat and predation.

--- SENTENCE 1149 ---
The Pacific boa is presently known only from the island of Ta'u.

--- SENTENCE 1150 ---
Ta'u remains largely covered with forest and a substantial area of which is protected under the National Park lease agreement with the local villages.

--- SENTENCE 1151 ---
Frequent sightings are reported to DMWR by residents of the villages of Ta'u and Faga.

--- SENTENCE 1152 ---
A major threat to the Pacific boa is being killed by local villagers when found in their plantations due to the false believe the snake is dangerous.

--- SENTENCE 1153 ---
The population may be reduced to critically low numbers requiring active intervention aimed at increasing numbers.

--- SENTENCE 1154 ---
The Snake-eyed Skink (*C. poeciloplurus*) and Lawes Skink (*E. lawesi*) are restricted to or known to prefer coastal habitats, thus making them susceptible to hurricanes, and susceptible to introduced predators.

--- SENTENCE 1155 ---
Both cats and rats have been present in most of the territory for a significant time, and their impacts on the herpetofauna are unlikely to significantly increase.

--- SENTENCE 1156 ---
The exception is Swains island, where cats have been introduced to control rats (W. Jennings, *pers.

--- SENTENCE 1157 ---
since the surveys conducted by Amerson et al. (1982a).

### <span id="page-87-0"></span>*5.5.2 CONSERVATION PRIORITIES*

--- SENTENCE 1158 ---
Although the lack of known, imminent threats on most islands downgrades the overall priority level of herpetofaunal studies, there are at least three areas that warrant attention:

1.

--- SENTENCE 1159 ---
*Update the status of the Pacific Boa in Ta'u*.

--- SENTENCE 1160 ---
Local villagers report frequent sightings in Ta'u village and Faga (on Ta'u) to DMWR Wildlife Division staff and a live snake was delivered to DMWR Wildlife Division staff in November 2015 while they were on Ta'u conducting bird surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1161 ---
The snake was used as a presentation tool to local school children and villagers in order to educate them on the importance of the snake, and demonstrate that they are not dangerous to the public.

--- SENTENCE 1162 ---
Additional, systematic surveys are needed in order to document distribution and population size on the Island of Ta'u.

!

--- SENTENCE 1163 ---
[](_page_87_Picture_6.jpeg)

Conducting boa public outreach through KVZK news.

- 2. *Update the status of the Swains island herpetofauna*, given previousrecords of uncommon species from the island and the introduction of cats (to the island) since the previous surveys.
- 3. *Update the status of the Snake-eyed skink*, since this species was noted as rare during previous surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1164 ---
### <span id="page-88-0"></span>**5.6 NATIVE LAND BIRDS**

A number of land birds are of conservation concern by virtue of their status, distribution, abundance, susceptibility to threats, and/or cultural importance (Table 1).Several of these species are restricted to the Western Polynesian region, while others are more geographically widespread.

--- SENTENCE 1165 ---
Out of 18 species that occur/occured in American Samoa, only two are endemic to the SamoanArchipelago, the Samoan Starling (*Aplonis atrifusca*) and the Ma'oma'o (*Gymnomyza samoensis)*.

--- SENTENCE 1166 ---
The Ma'oma'o has been extirpated from American Samoa, though it continues in low numbers in Western Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1167 ---
Five subspecies are endemic to American Samoa, the Collared Kingfisher of Tutuila (*Todiramphus chloris pealei*), the Collared Kingfisher of Manu'a (*Todiramphus chloris manuae*), the Polynesian Starling of Tutuila (*Aplonis tabuensis tutuilae)*, the Polynesian Starling of Manu'a (*Aplonis tabuensis manuae)*, and the Lesser [Fiji/Samoan] Shrikebill of Manu'a (*Clytorhynchus vitiensis powelli*)*.*.

--- SENTENCE 1168 ---
Following are brief descriptions of threats to the species.

--- SENTENCE 1169 ---
Information on distribution and abundances, and general assessment of status are summarized in Table 1 and cited in Subsection 3.2.2.

--- SENTENCE 1170 ---
Gaps in information and priority actions pertinent to their conservation are identified in theaccounts.

### <span id="page-88-1"></span>*5.6.1 SPECIES ACCOUNTS*

Baseline data remains patchy for most terrestrial bird species found in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1171 ---
Surveys conducted over the past decade may give indication of relative abundance and population trends but not of population size.

--- SENTENCE 1172 ---
Among these species this information gap is particularly apparent in the Shy [friendly] Ground-dove (*Alopecoenas* stairi), Manycoloured Fruit-dove (*Ptilinopus* perousii), Pacific Black

--- SENTENCE 1173 ---
[Grey] Duck (*Anas superciliosa*), Purple Swamphen (*Porphyrio porphyrio*), and Spotless Crake (*Porzana tabuensis*).

--- SENTENCE 1174 ---
Less is known of populations in the Manu'a islands due to the difficulty in maintaining continuous monitoring in these more remote locations.

--- SENTENCE 1175 ---
Ten species are featured below as deserving priority attention, and if available, includes such information as habitat, distribution, abundance and threats.

!

--- SENTENCE 1176 ---
[](_page_89_Picture_2.jpeg)

### <span id="page-89-0"></span>*5.6.1.1 Purple Swamphen (manuali'i)*

### *Porphyrio porphyrio*

The Purple Swamphen is widespread and likely has never been abundant in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1177 ---
Their low numbers are an expected result of habitat restrictions, as it relies on agroforests and wetland areas.

--- SENTENCE 1178 ---
In American Samoa, it occurs on allislands except Rose Atoll (Amerson et al. 1982a) and Swains Island.

--- SENTENCE 1179 ---
This species is of cultural significance, as its

Samoan name *manuali'i* translates to "bird of chiefs."

--- SENTENCE 1180 ---
However, it is also perceived as an agricultural pest to crops such as banana (*Musa spp.*) and taro (*Colocaia sp.)*.

--- SENTENCE 1181 ---
Because of this, it isoccasionally killed by farmers.

--- SENTENCE 1182 ---
This species may be inherently vulnerable due to its habitat restrictions.

--- SENTENCE 1183 ---
Wetlands in American Samoa are few, and these limited areas may continue to decrease and degrade as the island becomes more developed.

--- SENTENCE 1184 ---
Swamphens may also be vulnerable to the changes in agricultural lands that result from farming practices converting to modern, more intensive methods as mixed, traditional methods are left behind (Hole et al. 2002).

--- SENTENCE 1185 ---
Its diet elsewhere in its range is omnivorous, and thus, it may be beneficial in agricultural settings if it also consumes snails or other agricultural pests.

--- SENTENCE 1186 ---
It is therefore important to accurately determine the abundance and distribution of this species, and to determine its actual dietary preferences and habits.

--- SENTENCE 1187 ---
Suchdistribution and density data would allow for the construction of detailed maps to identify and protect the mostsuitable habitats for *P. porphyrio*, a method successfully used in other study systems (Li et al. 2002).

### <span id="page-89-1"></span>*5.6.1.2 Spotless Crake*

### *Zapornia tabuensis*

The Spotless Crake remains a high priority species due to its low abundance and extreme geographical restriction.

--- SENTENCE 1188 ---
It is thought to have once occurred on Tutuila but no recent sightings have been noted, and it is presumed to have been extirpated from the island.

--- SENTENCE 1189 ---
The only stronghold remaining to this species is Ta'u island; it is found nowhere else in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1190 ---
The Spotless Crake was first discovered on Ta'u in the 1920s and had been thought extirpated until one specimen was rediscovered near Ta'u village in the 1980s.

--- SENTENCE 1191 ---
It was not recorded again on Ta'u until 2001 when one bird was briefly observed in dense montane scrub at the summit of Mt. Lata.

--- SENTENCE 1192 ---
In 2011, one was caught incidentally via a live trap during a rat sampling expedition at the summit of Mt. Lata.

--- SENTENCE 1193 ---
It should be noted that during this sampling period, no rats were captured at or near the summit of Mt. Lata while, during the same sampling period, both Polynesian (*Rattus exulans*) and Norway rats (*R.*  *norvegicus*) were frequently captured at forested lowland and mid-elevation sites on Ta'u.

--- SENTENCE 1194 ---
In May – September 2013 surveys were undertaken on Ta'u in order to help describe the distribution, habitat preference, and population of the Spotless Crake.

--- SENTENCE 1195 ---
The Crake is currently thought to be restricted to an area within the National Park of American Samoa on Ta'u island, and although formerly reported from lowland wetlands thereon, recent records are exclusively from dense montane scrub habitats near the summit of Mt. Lata (~1000 m elevation).

--- SENTENCE 1196 ---
It is known to be associated with ephemeral upland stream habitats in Western Samoa (*pers.

--- SENTENCE 1197 ---
, and the most recent surveys have indicated that they are closely tied to wetland habitat on Ta'u.

--- SENTENCE 1198 ---
Intensive follow up surveys targeting the Spotless Crake are crucial to augmenting the current knowledge of this species on Ta'u, as well as to closely monitor for sudden declines in numbers.

--- SENTENCE 1199 ---
We are only beginning to understand its status and it is recommended that transects establishedin 2013 continue to be surveyed for the presence of Crake.

--- SENTENCE 1200 ---
Other survey sites should also be added to include the Liu area, which is very similar and geographically near to the area in which Crakes were detected.

--- SENTENCE 1201 ---
Efforts should be made to survey the northwestern foothills of Mt. Lata, as well as the extreme southwestern side of Ta'u island, as these areas were not covered by past sampling efforts.

--- SENTENCE 1202 ---
It appears that the major threat to the persistence of this species in American Samoa is the presence of introduced rats.

--- SENTENCE 1203 ---
Rat trapping on Ta'u hasdocumented the presence of Norway rats (*Rattus norvegicus*) in both lowland agroforest and summit montane scrub habitats, while Polynesian rats (*R. exulans*) occupy forested habitats on the north side of the island (O'Connor & Rauzon 2003, DMWR *unpub.

--- SENTENCE 1204 ---
These surveys found lower rat abundance on the south side of the island where, as suggested then, a refuge for the Crake was discovered in the Laufuti area.

--- SENTENCE 1205 ---
The well-documented propensity for flightlessness of insular rails in this genus (Slikaset al. 2002) makes them particularly susceptible to within-island threats.

--- SENTENCE 1206 ---
Other threats may include feral cats, habitat destruction and modification by feral pigs and cattle, and increased subsistence farming resulting in habitat loss.

--- SENTENCE 1207 ---
It is highly likely that this species will become extinct in the foreseeable future without active conservation efforts.

!

--- SENTENCE 1208 ---
** SPOTLESS CRAKE TRANSECT SURVEYS CONDUCTED IN 2013.

--- SENTENCE 1209 ---
<span id="page-91-1"></span>![](_page_91_Picture_4.jpeg)

*5.6.1.3 Samoan Starling (fuia) Aplonis atrifusca*

<span id="page-91-0"></span>One of six Class III terrestrial bird species listed in Table 2 is the Samoan archipelago endemic, the Samoan Starling (*Aplonis atrifusca*).

--- SENTENCE 1210 ---
Survey data suggests reasonably abundant populations of this species, which occurs on all islands of American

Samoa except for Rose Atoll and Swains Island.

--- SENTENCE 1211 ---
It is a habitat generalist, and is not subject to significant human predation.

--- SENTENCE 1212 ---
Highest densities occur in Manua's agro-forest edges (*pers.

--- SENTENCE 1213 ---
*), where it is commonly seen feeding on crop fruits such as papaya (*Carica* papaya).

--- SENTENCE 1214 ---
It is listed as a priority species due to its endemism (restricted globally to the Samoan archipelago), its significantly high loads of endoparasites (Atkinson et al. 2006), and because of its cavity nesting habit which may pose a vulnerability to the burgeoning Tutuila populations of introduced myna species (*Acridotheres sp*.).

--- SENTENCE 1215 ---
Continued surveys suggest stable populations, though a thorough analysis of existing data is necessary to detect finer trends.

--- SENTENCE 1216 ---
Intensive mark-recapture surveys targeting forest bird species have resulted in additional baseline data for this species.

--- SENTENCE 1217 ---
New ageing and sexing criteria have been developed so as birds can be accurately aged and sexed in hand (Pyle et al. in review).

--- SENTENCE 1218 ---
With this, data is available to begin constructing demographic makeup of the populations and analysis will likely begin in 2016.

--- SENTENCE 1219 ---
Moulting appeared to be protracted in this species, and began June – August and completed in December – March.

--- SENTENCE 1220 ---
One female in definitive plumage was captured undergoing staffelmauser-like replacement of primary feathers.

--- SENTENCE 1221 ---
If such proves to be the case, it would be the first documented passerine showing such a feather replacement pattern.

--- SENTENCE 1222 ---
This data, along with those of Banks (1984), suggests a primary breeding season of May – November with lower-level breeding in December – April.

!

--- SENTENCE 1223 ---
*5.6.1.4 Polynesian Starling (miti vao)*

<span id="page-92-0"></span>*Aplonis tabuensis tutuilae*

*Aplonis tabuensis manuae*

The Polynesian Starling is listed as a priority species.

--- SENTENCE 1224 ---
Unlike its endemic congener, the Samoan Starling, the Polynesian Starling has a much wider range and is found in Fiji, Tonga, Niue, and the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 1225 ---
Two subspecies of the Polynesian Starling, which can be visually separated, however, are endemic to American Samoa (*A. t. tutuilae and A. t. manuae*).

--- SENTENCE 1226 ---
The Polynesian Starling appears to be less common than the Samoan Starling and is restricted to native forest habitat; rarely seen in urbanized areas such as plantations and villages.

--- SENTENCE 1227 ---
They appear to be more common in Maloata on the west end of the island, between Afono and Vatia on the north side, and along the Mt. Alava Road.

--- SENTENCE 1228 ---
The Polynesian Starling is also a cavity nester, but because of a demarcated habitat preference, it may be less impacted by the presence of the introduced Common Myna (*Acridotheres tristis*) which may compete with Samoan Starlings over nesting spaces.

--- SENTENCE 1229 ---
However, the introduced Jungle Myna (*Acridotheres fuscus*) has been seen in forest habitat bordering urban areas and may potentially compete with Polynesian Starlings if its range continues to expand into native forested areas.

--- SENTENCE 1230 ---
Mark-recapture surveys have resulted in sufficient data to develop aging and sexing criteria for this species (Pyle et al. in review), though likely not enough individuals were captured to give a good indication of the demographic makeup of the population.

--- SENTENCE 1231 ---
This may be resultant of the natural low densities of the species and more intensive sampling methods may be necessary to gather baseline demographic trends.

!

--- SENTENCE 1232 ---
*5.6.1.5 Lesser [Fiji] Shrikebill (sega o le vau)

--- SENTENCE 1233 ---
Clytorhynchus vitiensis powelli*

<span id="page-93-0"></span>Lesser Shrikebill in American Samoa are currently listed as being an endemic subspecies, but this subspecies is a candidate for elevation to full species status (Pratt 2010 and Andersen 2015).

--- SENTENCE 1234 ---
Already, it is considered a separate species, Samoan Shrikebill (*Clytorhynchus powelli*) to the Fiji/Lesser Shrikebill

(*Clytorhynchus vitiensis*), by some authors (Pyle et al. in review).

--- SENTENCE 1235 ---
More data is needed for the status change, but the Samoan subspecies is morphologically distinguishable from populations found in Fiji and Tonga.

--- SENTENCE 1236 ---
Once it is officially elevated to full species, it will be the only bird endemic to American Samoa (the Samoan Starling is endemic to the Samoan archipelago).

--- SENTENCE 1237 ---
The Shrikebills in American Samoa arerestricted to the Manu'a island group where they are locally abundant in suitable habitat, though uncommon throughout the territory.

--- SENTENCE 1238 ---
In Ta'u they are restricted to mid-elevation mature native forest, and are rarely seen in lower elevations.

--- SENTENCE 1239 ---
This habitat specificity makes this species more vulnerable to population decline; this is particularly the case in American Samoa where hurricanes cause large scale habitat destruction (hurricane Heta in January 2004, and Olaf in February 2005).

--- SENTENCE 1240 ---
Continued monitoring of this species before, and after such events should be a high priority to ensure subsistence of the population.

--- SENTENCE 1241 ---
Bird banding data has resulted in criteria to accurately age and sex individuals in the hand.

--- SENTENCE 1242 ---
These data indicate that breeding probably occurs primarily in November – January, followed by moulting of feathers.

!

--- SENTENCE 1243 ---
*5.6.1.6 Pacific Imperial-pigeon (lupe) Ducula pacifica*

<span id="page-93-1"></span>The Pacific Pigeon ranges from the islands north of New Guinea east through Fiji, Tonga, Samoa, Niue, and the Cook Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1244 ---
Throughout its range, it is usually found on small islands and atolls, and rarely on large 'high islands' where it is replaced by a different species of pigeon.

--- SENTENCE 1245 ---
Its natural habitats are tropical moist lowland forests on smaller islands and tropical moist montane forest on larger islands.

--- SENTENCE 1246 ---
The Pacific Pigeon is the largest forest bird found in American Samoa, and as a fructivor, it likely plays an important role in dispersing the largest seeds of native trees.

--- SENTENCE 1247 ---
They have been observed carrying nesting material during the months of January – September.

--- SENTENCE 1248 ---
A pair will lay one egg, which is incubated by both parents.

--- SENTENCE 1249 ---
The hatched chick will be fed by both parents for close to a month while in the nest.

--- SENTENCE 1250 ---
Timing suggests that this species could nest more than once a year, but because most young birds are seen from July – August, it is likely that nesting occurs just once during each year.

--- SENTENCE 1251 ---
This points towards a low reproductive rate, in particular because one chick needs two adults to successfully care and rear for it.

--- SENTENCE 1252 ---
In event of a population crash this strategy would make population growth and recovery a slow process.

--- SENTENCE 1253 ---
Such sharp declines in populations have occurred after hurricanes.

--- SENTENCE 1254 ---
While some mortality may occur directly as a result of the hurricane itself, evidence suggests that much mortality is due to increased hunting vulnerability after such an event.

--- SENTENCE 1255 ---
When the native forest is stripped of leaves and fruit, birds must forage widely and in open habitats.

--- SENTENCE 1256 ---
In response to the hurricanes in the 1990s, both Western and American Samoa imposed a hunting ban to allow the species time to recover.

--- SENTENCE 1257 ---
Unfortunately, hunting still occurs in American Samoa, and the ban is not being effectively enforced.

--- SENTENCE 1258 ---
Populations on Tutuila apparently reached moderately stable levels approximately 7 years after the majorhurricanes of 1991 and 1992 (Hurricanes Ofa and Val) (Utzurrum & Seamon 2001) although the numbers observed are a fraction of those reported in earlier studies (Amerson et al. 1982b).

--- SENTENCE 1259 ---
With the advent of more exploitive hunting using modern methods (Craig et al. 1994), hunting pressure will increasingly effect populations at low densities.

--- SENTENCE 1260 ---
Pigeons are still hunted for ceremonial purposes or for consumption during certain holidays such as Easter and White Sunday, though customs such as these are probably practiced less frequently due to a dwindling population.

--- SENTENCE 1261 ---
Perhaps more than any other species, the *Lupe* is of irreplaceable importance to Samoa; both because of its natural role in spreading rainforest seeds, and its cultural role in the stories, proverbs, and heritage of the Samoan people.

--- SENTENCE 1262 ---
Early missionary accounts tell of whole villages spending weeks in the forest around the *tia seulupe* (pigeon catching huts upon star mounds) during pigeon catching season, and that this was a time of feasting.

--- SENTENCE 1263 ---
Lupe once must have been very abundant to support such a cultural activity.

--- SENTENCE 1264 ---
The rainforest that once covered the Tafuna plain is now gone, likely taking away with it the best habitat available for the Pacific Pigeon in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1265 ---
Conserving remaining lowland rainforest habitat, and either enforcing the hunting ban or implementing bag limits is needful for the protection of this species.

!

--- SENTENCE 1266 ---
<span id="page-95-0"></span>*5.6.1.7 Shy [Friendly] Ground-dove (tuaimeo)*

*Alopecoenas stairi*

### **ESA endangered species**

The Shy Ground-Dove, formerly of the genus Gallicolumba (del Hoyo et al. 2014), has a discontinuous and poorly documented distribution across central Polynesia.

--- SENTENCE 1267 ---
This

species has been extirpated throughout much of its range and the small and fragmented population that remains is suspected to be declining.

--- SENTENCE 1268 ---
The IUCN lists this species as vulnerable worldwide, meaning that the best available evidence indicates that it is facing a high risk of extinction in the wild.

--- SENTENCE 1269 ---
In American Samoa, the population is restricted to the islands of Ofu and Olosega where it appears to occupy low to mid-elevation forest.

--- SENTENCE 1270 ---
Low population sizes in Manu'a may be a natural phenomenon, since it apparently prefers steep but forested slopes in close proximity to exposed or talus substrates.

--- SENTENCE 1271 ---
Observations from a decade ago suggest that the largest population occurred on Olosega, particularly in areas that were ravaged by Hurricane Heta (2004).

--- SENTENCE 1272 ---
It feeds on seeds, fruit, buds, young leaves, snails, insects and caterpillars on the forest floor and in the undergrowth (Watling 1982 and Cluine 1984).

--- SENTENCE 1273 ---
The Shy Ground-Dove appears particularly sensitive to human disturbance, usually leaving areas with logging or planting activities within days of occurrence, and not re-inhabiting even five years after the cessation of human activity (Kretzschmar 2000).

--- SENTENCE 1274 ---
The reasons for this may relate to change in forest characteristics (e.g. leaf-litter, food resources), structure (e.g. openness) or invasion by ground predators.

--- SENTENCE 1275 ---
The major threat to the small Western Samoan population of Shy Ground-Doves is predation by Polynesian rats (*Rattus exulans*) (Roberts 2009).

--- SENTENCE 1276 ---
There is almost no information available about the population in Ofu-Olosega, much of what exists being anecdotal.

--- SENTENCE 1277 ---
It is essential to gather baseline data by conducting surveys to assess this species' distribution, population numbers and conservation requirements.

--- SENTENCE 1278 ---
Data to age and sex birds accurately should also be established in order to acquire an understanding of the demographic makeup of the population.

--- SENTENCE 1279 ---
Timing for breeding and molting cycles should be documented in order to identify important periods of the year.

!

--- SENTENCE 1280 ---
[](_page_96_Picture_2.jpeg)

*5.6.1.8 Many-colored Fruit-dove (manuma)*

<span id="page-96-0"></span>*Pilinopus perousii*

**Male P. perousii (Photo by M. MacDonald)

--- SENTENCE 1281 ---
**

The Manuma (Many-colored Fruit Dove)

--- SENTENCE 1282 ---
*Ptilinopus perousii* is a species that is found in Fiji, Tonga, Samoa with an endemic subspecies in American Samoa (*P. p. perousii*).

--- SENTENCE 1283 ---
The IUCN has classified this species as Least Concern because of its wide distribution but it has also indicated that its population has been in decline.

--- SENTENCE 1284 ---
The Manuma is considered rare and local over much of its range.

--- SENTENCE 1285 ---
Manuma were not considered rare on Tutuila in the 19th and early 20th centuries.

--- SENTENCE 1286 ---
The first systematic bird surveys were done in the mid-1970s recorded only a small number of Manuma.

--- SENTENCE 1287 ---
In a follow up survey done in mid-1980s the population on Tutuila was estimated at 80 birds (Craig 2009).

--- SENTENCE 1288 ---
The Manuma has also declined in Tonga.

--- SENTENCE 1289 ---
The island of Tongatapu, which is the largest island in the Tongan archipelago, no longer supports Manuma.

--- SENTENCE 1290 ---
Habitat loss and hunting have been the primary causes of decline (Craig 2009).

--- SENTENCE 1291 ---
The Manuma shows a strong preference for Aoa (*Ficus prolixa* and *F. obliqua*) and are usually seen in associate with these two species of trees.

--- SENTENCE 1292 ---
Birds will congregate on the trees when eating, but Manuma have also been seen aggressively fighting other Manuma to keep them out of the tree and protect the food resource.

--- SENTENCE 1293 ---
Since the Manuma feed almost exclusively on *Ficus prolixa* and *F. obliqua* their population size is directly related to status of these two species of trees.

--- SENTENCE 1294 ---
The relatively low population size of Manuma in American Samoa make them particularly vulnerable to catastrophic weather events or other population level disturbances.

--- SENTENCE 1295 ---
A restricted diet may also make this bird more difficult to manage in rehabilitation and recovery situations which necessitates the development of protocols that will aid DMWR in Manuma conservation.

!

--- SENTENCE 1296 ---
*5.6.1.9 Blue-crowned Lory (segaula)*

<span id="page-97-0"></span>*Vini australis*

**(Photo by RCB Utzurrum)**

American Samoa's only parrot, the Bluecrowned Lory is another culturally important species.

--- SENTENCE 1297 ---
Its bright feathers were used in traditional headgear and used as decorations within woven mats.

--- SENTENCE 1298 ---
This species is restricted to Manu'a, and appears to be more abundant on Ta'u.

--- SENTENCE 1299 ---
Post-hurricane records of them exist from Tutuila but strangely, they have not become established on the larger island, and there are no historical records of them ever being a resident on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 1300 ---
Although the populations appear to be stable, they are known from elsewhere in their range to be vulnerable to both introduced rats and introduced cavity nesting birds, such as the common myna.

--- SENTENCE 1301 ---
Detailed life-history studies, as well asstudies of seasonal shifts in resource usage, are priorities for this species.

!

--- SENTENCE 1302 ---
[](_page_97_Picture_7.jpeg)

5.6.1.10 Pacific Black

--- SENTENCE 1303 ---
[Gray] Duck (toloa)

*Anas superciliosa* **(Photo M. MacDonald)

--- SENTENCE 1304 ---
**

The Pacific Black Duck is a rare sighting on the wetlands of Tutuila and is only seen regularly on the small nearshore island of Aunu'u.

--- SENTENCE 1305 ---
The number of ducks appear to be low even on Aunu'u, with maximum recorded counts of 20-25 individuals (DMWR unpubl.

--- SENTENCE 1306 ---
Periodic counts of the Aunu'u population indicate numbers are low and stable at best (i.e., there are no indications that numbers are increasing).

--- SENTENCE 1307 ---
Its appearance in many Samoan proverbs speaks to its cultural significance.

--- SENTENCE 1308 ---
Due to the population's isolation from regular enforcement activities, there exists a significant potential for poaching.

--- SENTENCE 1309 ---
Demographic and ecological studies are needed to better understand the status of the species and to determine whether management actions are appropriate or if their low abundance is a natural phenomenon.

--- SENTENCE 1310 ---
It is likely that their decline over the decades ties into the loss of appropriate wetland habitats within the territory.

!

--- SENTENCE 1311 ---
*5.6.1.10 Ma'oma'o (toloa)*

<span id="page-98-0"></span>*Gymnomyza samoensis*

**ESA endangered species**

The Ma'oma'o or Mao (*Gymnomyza samoansis*) honeyeater is endemic to the Samoan archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 1312 ---
It is currently listed as endangered by the IUCN red list and endangered under a 2016 ESA ruling.

--- SENTENCE 1313 ---
Though the Ma'oma'o species previously occurred on Tutuila, American Samoa it is now presumed to be locally extinct and currently is only found in declining numbers on the largest islands on Upolu and Savaii in Samoa (Olson, and Butler 2006).

--- SENTENCE 1314 ---
Museum specimens of this species in Tutuila were last collected in the 1800's and the last confirmed sightings were in the 1920's (Craig 2009).

--- SENTENCE 1315 ---
The decline of the Mao in Samoa is linked to habitat loss and the associated increase in black rat abundance.

--- SENTENCE 1316 ---
Research in Samoa suggests that the species has a wide diet and is found both in the uplands of Savaii and at low elevation in areas with some larger tree canopy cover.

--- SENTENCE 1317 ---
It is not found in areas which have been logged or been burnt suggesting it requires forest with some canopy cover.

--- SENTENCE 1318 ---
Recent cyclones in Upolu appear to have impacted the remaining population and reduced the amount of canopy forest even further.

--- SENTENCE 1319 ---
The reasons for the Mao's local extinction in American Samoa are unclear as the same suite of introduced predators exists in Samoa as American Samoa and, furthermore, habitat loss in American Samoa has been minimal compared with Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1320 ---
It is possible that a combined effect of a disease, cyclonic damage affected the survival rates.

--- SENTENCE 1321 ---
Though we are never likely to know why this species has disappeared from American Samoa its reintroduction to American Samoa is likely to be a critical component towards its continual survival since forest loss in Samoa continues to increase making populations more susceptible to the effects of cyclones (due to even further fragmentation).

--- SENTENCE 1322 ---
Furthermore the Mao is an important pollinator and seed disperser in Samoa and its reintroduction to American Samoa may be important for a number of plant species.

### <span id="page-99-0"></span>*5.6.2 SUMMARY OF THREATS TO THE AVIFAUNA*

Threats to birds vary from species to species (see above) but fall into five general classes.

--- SENTENCE 1323 ---
The first is the threat of **predation**, typically from introduced rats but also from cats or humans.

--- SENTENCE 1324 ---
This applies not only to ground nesting species, but also tocavity nesters such as *V. australis*.

--- SENTENCE 1325 ---
The second is **loss of habitat**, whether of particular plant species such as *Ficus prolixa* or of entire habitat types such as wetlands.

--- SENTENCE 1326 ---
The third is **catastrophic declines**, especially in species with very small populations, due to natural occurrences such as hurricanes.

--- SENTENCE 1327 ---
The fourth threat is that of **disease**.

--- SENTENCE 1328 ---
Although most indigenous diseases do not appear to be major threats, new or emergent diseases such as West Nile Virus are a significant concern.

--- SENTENCE 1329 ---
The fifth threat is that from **introduced species**, including introduced birds already present in the territory.

### <span id="page-99-1"></span>*5.6.3 CONSERVATION STRATEGIES*

--- SENTENCE 1330 ---
The data suggest several broad strategies are required to meet the conservation needs of the avifauna of the territory.

--- SENTENCE 1331 ---
1. *Protect the Manu'a islands from introduced species* and *prevent the entry of new threats* (such as the brown tree snake) into the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 1332 ---
Enhance quarantine procedures, as well as post-introduction emergency eradication efforts, since there is frequent shipping between Tutuila and Manu'a.

--- SENTENCE 1333 ---
The Manu'a islands contain 16 of the 17 species of conservation concern.

--- SENTENCE 1334 ---
A number ofthese are known to be vulnerable to introduced species, particularly *Rattus rattus*.

- 2.

--- SENTENCE 1335 ---
Initiate extensive surveys to *determine distributions and habitat requirements of poorly known species.

--- SENTENCE 1336 ---
Among the priority species are *A. superciliosa, C. vitiensis, G. stairi, P. porphyrio*, and *P. tabuensis*.
- 3.

--- SENTENCE 1337 ---
Employ well-designed surveys to *derive robust population estimates, and monitor at intervals sufficient to allow detection of multi-annual trends in abundances*.
- 4.

--- SENTENCE 1338 ---
*Determine detailed demographic data for species of concern, since even reliable abundance estimates are often insufficient to determine factors responsible for* dynamics of populations.

--- SENTENCE 1339 ---
- 5. *Create a territorial habitat management plan*.
- 6.

--- SENTENCE 1340 ---
*Develop mechanisms for emergency response to threats of emergent infections*, such as West Nile Virus.

### <span id="page-100-0"></span>**5.7 SEA BIRDS**

!

--- SENTENCE 1341 ---
[](_page_100_Picture_3.jpeg)

Tahiti Petrel (*Pseudobulweria rostrata*) (Photo by RCB Utzurrum)

### <span id="page-100-1"></span>*5.7.1 CLASSIFICATION*

Six sea bird species (Polynesian Storm-petrel *Nesofregetta fuliginosa*, Bridled tern *Sterna anaethaetus*, Collared Petrel *Pterodroma leucoptera brevipes*, Herald Petrel *P. arminjoniana heraldica, Christmas shearwater Puffinus nativitatus*, and Wedge-tailed Shearwater *P. pacificus*) are grouped as Class I due to little, if any information on their breeding populations (Table 2).

--- SENTENCE 1342 ---
These species have a potential to be breeders on American Samoa but no studies have been conducted in order to determine their status.

--- SENTENCE 1343 ---
All but the Bridled tern (*S. anaethaetus)* are expected to be found in remote and difficult to access locations, primarily the montane scrub summits of mountains.

--- SENTENCE 1344 ---
The remoteness of breeding areas has restricted the collection of incidental data on these species during the course of previous studies.

--- SENTENCE 1345 ---
Bridled terns (*S. anaethaetus)* have been seen in the vicinity of Tutuila island(*unpub.

--- SENTENCE 1346 ---
DMWR data*), but surveys have not been systematic enough to derive robust estimates of their abundance.

--- SENTENCE 1347 ---
Two other seabird species (Tahiti Petrel *Pseudobulweria rostrata* and Tropical Shearwater *Puffinus bailloni*) are listed as Class II species (Table 2).

--- SENTENCE 1348 ---
Their breeding sites are well documented, and their presence has been repeatedly confirmed, but abundance estimates are not robust and they are potentially threatened by a known population of *Rattus norvegicus* in the most important breeding area at the summit of Mt. Lata, Ta'u.

### <span id="page-100-2"></span>*5.7.2 PREVIOUS STUDIES*

### <span id="page-100-3"></span>*5.7.2.1 TUTUILA, TA'U, OFU, OLOSEGA ISLANDS SEABIRD MONITORING*

Baseline data on seabirds in American Samoa is patchy and incomplete.

--- SENTENCE 1349 ---
This results from the difficulties in establishing regular access to known seabird areas, which frequently require boat access.

--- SENTENCE 1350 ---
As such, studies have been sporadic over the years and much of the data has been collected incidentally.

--- SENTENCE 1351 ---
Most of the seabird species found on Tutuila Island inhabit the north shore, which is less accessible to people and is important nesting habitat for coastal cliff nesting birds (O'Conner and Rauzon 2004).

--- SENTENCE 1352 ---
In 2012, DMWR undertook a pilot study to inventory and monitor seabirds in American Samoa and to develop protocols for more regular seabird surveys around the islands.

--- SENTENCE 1353 ---
The pilot study had three objectives; 1) To design a survey to estimate annual baseline estimate of population sizes of seabirds in American Samoa and to implement the surveys annually;

--- SENTENCE 1354 ---
2) Determine locations of nesting sites and habitats of various resident seabird species; 3) Document new species records in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1355 ---
Tutuila island, Ta'u island, and Swains island were visited for these surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1356 ---
During September 2012, surveys for Swains island of species observed at sea included white tern, sooty tern, brown noddy, black noddy, brown noddy, red-footed booby, and white-tailed tropicbird.

--- SENTENCE 1357 ---
Seabird numbers were generally low over pelagic areas with increased abundance around Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 1358 ---
Seabird numbers indicated highest abundance over the north-east corner of Swains island and the species found were the same as those found by Amerson et al. during their 1976 visit to Swains.

--- SENTENCE 1359 ---
Roosting and nesting seabirds were identified to primarily use pockets of mixed *Pisonia* forest that are surrounded by dense coconut palms.

--- SENTENCE 1360 ---
Only red-footed booby, brown noddy, black noddy, and white tern were observed either roosting or nesting, indicating that some of the less common species (white-tailed tropicbird, greater frigatebird, lesser frigatebird, brown booby, sooty tern) may not be breeding on the island, or not enough transects were performed to adequately sample these species.

--- SENTENCE 1361 ---
Time limitations due to length of visit to Swains island and time of day restrictions in sampling may also have been a factor.

--- SENTENCE 1362 ---
Breeding phenology of many of these species on equatorial islands is poorly understood and thus the timing of the visit to the island may have affected the results.

--- SENTENCE 1363 ---
In July 2013, surveys were conducted on Tutuila and Ta'u islands in order to evaluate and identify potential sites for seabird population monitoring and for initial assessments of seabird abundance and distribution.

--- SENTENCE 1364 ---
8 sites (both shoreline lookouts close to seabird roosts and flyways as well as high lookouts, which gave a good view of entire colony areas) were identified on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 1365 ---
On Ta'u island, much of the accessible shoreline was also investigated in order to identify areas important for seabirds as well as potential habitat and flyways for petrels and shearwaters.

--- SENTENCE 1366 ---
Overall seabird abundance on Ta'u seems low, likely due to the absence of offshore island and rocky cliff areas that can provide refuge for seabirds.

--- SENTENCE 1367 ---
However, three key areas were identified that contained seabird populations which should be monitored.

--- SENTENCE 1368 ---
All four monitoring locations were easy to access, either on a road or within a short hike in order to make continued monitoring as easy as possible (Figure 2).

--- SENTENCE 1369 ---
Using these observations we are developing standardized protocols which can be used to effectively monitor birds from locations around the islands.

--- SENTENCE 1370 ---
The next step of the project would be to attempt to visit and evaluate sites on the islands of Ofu and Olosega.

!

--- SENTENCE 1371 ---
MAP OF TUTUILA SHOWING THE IDENTIFIED SEABIRD COLONY LOCATIONS (red) and additional high lookouts (yellow).

--- SENTENCE 1372 ---
