In fact, sea cucumbers seem best adapted to extreme depths as various species have been recorded from 5,000 to 10,000 m deep.

--- SENTENCE 1777 ---
They scavenge while moving slow through the seafloors taking in sand with dead animal and plant materials and other debris.

--- SENTENCE 1778 ---
The dead materials are ingested while the sand is expelled.

--- SENTENCE 1779 ---
A single sea cucumber can swallow more than 45 kg of sediment a year, and their excellent digestive capacities allow them to reject a finer, purer and homogeneous sediment.

--- SENTENCE 1780 ---
Therefore, sea cucumbers play a major role in the biological processing of the sea bed (bioturbation, purge, homogenization of the substratum etc.).

--- SENTENCE 1781 ---
In addition, sea cucumbers are important in recycling nutrients and breaking down waste matter.

--- SENTENCE 1782 ---
*Holothuria fuscogilva*, also known as white teatfish, is the most highly prized sea cucumber.

--- SENTENCE 1783 ---
It widely is distributed throughout the Indian and Pacific Oceans from South Africa, north to the Red Sea and Egypt, east to India and to Australia, north to China and Japan, and east to Easter Island, including Pacific Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1784 ---
*Holothuria whitmaei* is found only in the Pacific, from Australia and the Philippines to Guam and Samoa, and to Hawaii and Johnston Island.

--- SENTENCE 1785 ---
*Actinopyga mauritiana* is found throughout the central Pacific and Indian Oceans.

--- SENTENCE 1786 ---
It occurs from eastern Africa and the Red Sea in the west, to Hawaii, and the Solomon Islands in the southeast.

### HABITAT

*Holothuria fuscogilva* is found in waters from 0 – 40 m, but is mostly encountered between 15 and 30 m, and may occur occur in deeper waters.

--- SENTENCE 1787 ---
In the Western Central Pacific, *H. fuscogilva* inhabits outer barrier reefs and passes and shallow seagrass beds between 0 and 40 m. In the African Indian Ocean region, it prefers slopes and lagoons over sandy bottoms from 10 -50

--- SENTENCE 1788 ---
The juveniles can occur in much shallower waters.

--- SENTENCE 1789 ---
In Fiji, it recruits in shallow seagrass beds and then moves to deeper waters.

--- SENTENCE 1790 ---
Sexual maturity is reached at 1.2 kg (Conand 1989), slower to compared to other *Holothuria* spp.

--- SENTENCE 1791 ---
This species only reproduces once or twice a year.

--- SENTENCE 1792 ---
In New Caledonia, *H. fuscogilva* is known to spawn from December to March and in Solomon Islands from August to October.

--- SENTENCE 1793 ---
It has a maximum size of 60 cm, with an average weight of 2.4 kg.

--- SENTENCE 1794 ---
Generation length is unknown for this species.

--- SENTENCE 1795 ---
Body size is not a good indicator of age or longevity (reviewed in Conand et al. 2013).

--- SENTENCE 1796 ---
*Holothuria whitmaei* is found along slopes and passes within reef zones.

--- SENTENCE 1797 ---
In the Western Central Pacific, this species can also be found in reef flats, slopes and shallow seagrass beds between 0 and 20 m.

--- SENTENCE 1798 ---
This species has been observed at 30 m, but may possibly occur in deeper waters to about 50 m. In New Caledonia, *H. whitmaei* spawns between June and August.

--- SENTENCE 1799 ---
Genetic studies on the Australian east coast suggested that recruitment to populations of this species occurs from a wide geographic range.

--- SENTENCE 1800 ---
Growth of this species is slow, between 80-170 g\*yr-1, and large animals can shrink during certain periods.

--- SENTENCE 1801 ---
This species may live at least 12 years (reviewed in Conand et al. 2013).

--- SENTENCE 1802 ---
*Actinopyga mauritiana* prefers outer reef flats and fringing reefs, mostly in the surf zone between 0-20 m as part of the medio-littoral reef community in the Western Central Pacific Region.

--- SENTENCE 1803 ---
It holds on to the hard bottom when exposed to wave action.

--- SENTENCE 1804 ---
In Solomon Islands, adults are commonly found around solid reef rock and scattered tables of the coral *Acropora* sp.

--- SENTENCE 1805 ---
This species has a high fecundity and early sexual maturity, around 350 g weight in New Caledonia.

--- SENTENCE 1806 ---
In the Africa and Indian Ocean region, this species reaches sexual maturity at 23 cm TL.

--- SENTENCE 1807 ---
The peak reproductive activity is between December and January in New Caledonia and from October until January in the Solomon Islands and the cycle relates to ambient ocean temperature and photoperiod (reviewed in Conand et al. 2013).

### THREATS

*H. fuscogilva* has been depleted in parts of the South Pacific (approximately 30% of the range).

--- SENTENCE 1808 ---
Depletion refers to commercially unviable collection, and estimated to represent an approximately 60-80% loss or greater, with some refuge for this species in deeper water.

--- SENTENCE 1809 ---
Shallow waters are more heavily impacted.

--- SENTENCE 1810 ---
In areas where it is considered overexploited, harvests have declined.

--- SENTENCE 1811 ---
*H. whitmaei* is commercially exploited throughout its range for its high value as beche-demer to supply the Asian consumers around the world.

--- SENTENCE 1812 ---
Based on a number of quantitative and qualitative studies, populations are estimated to be depleted and have declined by more than 60-90% in the majority of its range (at least 70%).

--- SENTENCE 1813 ---
It is considered overexploited in other parts of its range.

--- SENTENCE 1814 ---
*A. mauritiana* is harvested both commercially as well as in subsistence fisheries throughout its distribution.

--- SENTENCE 1815 ---
In many countries of the Western Central Pacific region, *A. mauritiana* is consumed as bêche-de-mer or their instestines and/or gonads are consumed as delicacies or as the protein component in traditional diets.

--- SENTENCE 1816 ---
*A. mauritiana* is among the top three species for local subsistence consumption (Kinch et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 1817 ---
Although heavily targeted, *Actinopyga mauritiana* is difficult to fish because it is found on the exposed reef crest, and is somewhat cryptic (found in crevices).

### CONSERVATION STRATEGY

1.

--- SENTENCE 1818 ---
We propose **to develop a management plan for the harvest of these sea cucumbers**.

--- SENTENCE 1819 ---
The management plan would include the **development of sea cucumber**  **hatcheries** in collaboration with regional partners such as the Secretariat of the Pacific Community.

--- SENTENCE 1820 ---
The plan would also include **regular monitoring of stocks in the wild, mapping of their distribution and an education and outreach component for fisheries regulation compliance**.

2.

--- SENTENCE 1821 ---
We also propose to conduct **basic scientific research on the growth rate, longevity and reproduction of these sea cucumbers and their population genetic connectivity in the Samoan Archipelago**.

### <span id="page-136-0"></span>*5.11.3 HARD CORALS*

!

--- SENTENCE 1822 ---
[](_page_136_Picture_5.jpeg)

*Acropora speciosa, Pavona diffluens, Euphyllia paradivisa, Acropora globiceps, Isopora crateriformis, Acropora retusa*

Local common name: samu

Distribution: Widely distributed

Abundance: Rare to moderately common

Status: listed as threatened under the Endangered Species Act

DISTRIBUTION

*Acropora retusa* is found in the south-west and northern Indian Ocean, the central Indo-Pacific, the Solomons, the oceanic west Pacific, and the central Pacific and in the Pitcairn Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1823 ---
*Acropora speciosa* is found in the central Indo-Pacific, the oceanic west Pacific, and the central Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 1824 ---
It is distributed from Indonesia to the Marshall Islands in the western and central Pacific, in the Maldives in the Indian Ocean and at least one site in French Polynesia.

--- SENTENCE 1825 ---
*Acropora globiceps* is distributed from the central Indo-Pacific, oceanic west Pacific to the central Pacific as far east as the Pitcairn Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1826 ---
*Euphyllia paradivisa* is distributed mostly in the Coral Triangle area (the Philippines to Timor Leste and east to the Solomon Islands).

--- SENTENCE 1827 ---
*Isopora crateriformis* is distributed within the Coral Triangle area (the Philippines to Timor Leste and east to the Solomon Islands), plus some of the western Pacific too, including New Caledonia, the Samoas, and the Marshall Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1828 ---
*Pavona diffluens* is distributed along part of the east African coast, the Red Sea, and the northwestern Indian Ocean.

--- SENTENCE 1829 ---
These corals are purportedly found in American Samoa but there are no published information in their distribution.

### HABITAT

*Acropora retusa* is a hermaphroditic spawner (contains both male and female gametes).

--- SENTENCE 1830 ---
Its colonies are flat plates with short, thick, finger-like branches.

--- SENTENCE 1831 ---
Its radial corallites vary in length giving the branches 'rough' and spiky appearance.

--- SENTENCE 1832 ---
*Acropora retusa* is found in shallow reef slope and back-reef areas, such as upper reef slopes, reef flats, and shallow lagoons, from 0 to 5 meters in depth.

--- SENTENCE 1833 ---
Like other species of *Acropora*, *Acropora speciosa* is likely to be hermaphroditic (having both male and female gametes) spawners.

--- SENTENCE 1834 ---
Its colonies form thick cushions or bottlebrush branches and are cream or light brown in color with delicately colored branch tips.

--- SENTENCE 1835 ---
*Acropora speciosa* occurs on lower reef slopes and walls, in reefs characterized by clear water from 12 to 40 meters in depth.

--- SENTENCE 1836 ---
*Acropora globiceps* form colonies that have finger-like branches.

--- SENTENCE 1837 ---
The branches are always closely compacted although the size and appearance of branches depend on degree of exposure to wave action.

--- SENTENCE 1838 ---
Colonies exposed to strong wave action have pyramid-shaped branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 1839 ---
Colonies can be uniform blue, purple, cream, brown, or fluorescent green in color.

--- SENTENCE 1840 ---
*Acropora globiceps* is also likely to be hermaphroditic (having both male and female gametes) spawners.

--- SENTENCE 1841 ---
It occurs on upper reef slopes, reef flats, and adjacent habitats in depths ranging from 0 to 8 meters.

--- SENTENCE 1842 ---
*Euphyllia paradivisa* is found in environments sheltered from wave, on the upper reef slopes, mid-slope terraces, and lagoons from 2 to 25 meters in depth.

--- SENTENCE 1843 ---
It is made up of separate and branching corallites and polyps have branching tentacles.

--- SENTENCE 1844 ---
Color is pale greenish-grey or pink (in rare instances) with lighter tentacle tips.

--- SENTENCE 1845 ---
*Isopora crateriformis* is mostly found in shallow, high-wave energy environments, including reef flats and lower reef crests, and upper reef slopes.

--- SENTENCE 1846 ---
It has been reported from low tide to at least 12 meters deep, and may occur in mesophotic depths (<50 meters).

--- SENTENCE 1847 ---
It forms colonies of flat, solid encrusting plates.

--- SENTENCE 1848 ---
Colonies are usually brown in color and can sometimes be over 1 meter in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1849 ---
When a colony occurs on a slope, the lower edge is usually lifted as a plate.

--- SENTENCE 1850 ---
*Pavona diffluens* forms sub-massive and tan-colored colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1851 ---
It occurs in upper reef slopes, mid-slopes, lower reef crests, reef flats, and lagoons in depths of 5 to at least 20 meters.

--- SENTENCE 1852 ---
*Pavona* species are known to be gonochoristic (individual colonies have either male or female gametes, not both) broadcast spawners.

--- SENTENCE 1853 ---
The age of first maturity of most reef building corals is typically three to eight years (Wallace 1999) and therefore we assume that average age of mature individuals is greater than eight years.

--- SENTENCE 1854 ---
Furthermore, based on average sizes and growth rates, we assume that average generation length is 10 years, unless otherwise stated.

--- SENTENCE 1855 ---
Total longevity is not known, but likely to be more than ten years.

--- SENTENCE 1856 ---
However, most corals are also capable of asexual reproduction through fragmentation so longevity can be indeterminate.

### THREATS

Several of the most important threats contributing to the extinction risk of corals are related to global climate change.

--- SENTENCE 1857 ---
The main concerns are regarding the impacts of the magnitude and the rapid increase in greenhouse gas concentrations and atmospheric warming.

--- SENTENCE 1858 ---
These changes are increasing the warming of the global climate system and changing the carbonate chemistry of the ocean (ocean acidification), which affects a number of biological processes in corals, including secretion of their skeletons.

--- SENTENCE 1859 ---
Higher atmospheric CO2 results in: Ocean warming via the greenhouse effect, ocean acidification via oceanic uptake of CO2, and rising sea levels via ice melting and thermal expansion.

--- SENTENCE 1860 ---
Latest studies also indicate that El Nino events facilitate the increase of atmospheric CO2.

--- SENTENCE 1861 ---
Based on current models, global annual mean ocean surface temperatures will likely increase by approximately 0.4-1 °C by 2030, approximately 0.7-2 °C by 2060, and approximately 2-5 °C by 2081-2100 (IPCC, 2013).

--- SENTENCE 1862 ---
The models also indicate that mean surface pH in the tropics (20 °N to 20 °S) is projected to decline from the current pH of approximately 8.05 to approximately 7.95 by 2050, and to approximately 7.75 by 2100, or a reduction of 0.31 by 2100 (IPCC, 2013), with a subsequent large decline in aragonite saturation state in surface tropical waters, exacerbating the impacts of ocean acidification on reef-building corals.

--- SENTENCE 1863 ---
Bleaching is the primary observable coral response to ocean warming, wherein corals turn white as they expel their symbiotic zooxanthellae in response to thermal stress.

--- SENTENCE 1864 ---
For many corals, an episodic increase of only 1 °C-2 °C above the normal local seasonal maximum ocean temperature can induce bleaching.

--- SENTENCE 1865 ---
Corals can withstand mild to moderate bleaching but severe or prolonged bleaching can lead to colony death.

--- SENTENCE 1866 ---
In addition to coral bleaching, ocean warming has been documented to lead to impaired fertilization, developmental abnormalities, mortality, impaired settlement success, and impaired calcification of early life phases.

--- SENTENCE 1867 ---
The impact of ocean warming is expected to variably affect corals in various spatial scales.

--- SENTENCE 1868 ---
Exposure to increased sea surface water temperature will depend on colony location (e.g., latitude, depth, bathymetry, habitat type, etc.) and physical processes that affect seawater temperature and its effects on coral colonies (e.g., winds, currents, upwelling shading, tides, etc.).

--- SENTENCE 1869 ---
Colonies in higher latitudes and/or deeper areas are usually less affected by warming events.

--- SENTENCE 1870 ---
Coral in deeper areas are generally less affected typically because lower irradiance reduces the likelihood of warming-induced bleaching.

--- SENTENCE 1871 ---
Also, some locations are blocked from warm currents by bathymetric features, and some habitat types reduce the effects of warm water.

--- SENTENCE 1872 ---
Physical processes can also moderate exposure of colonies of the species to ocean warming in many ways, including processes that increase mixing (e.g., wind, currents, tides), reduce seawater temperature (e.g., upwelling, runoff), or increase shading (e.g. turbidity, cloud cover).

--- SENTENCE 1873 ---
While exposure of colonies of a species to ocean warming will likely vary annually and decadally, while increasing over time, ocean warming is predicted to substantially increase by 2100.

--- SENTENCE 1874 ---
These projected changes will likely decrease the environmental suitability for shallow coral reef habitats across the equatorial western Pacific and on the Indo-Pacific (Couce et al. 2013).

--- SENTENCE 1875 ---
However, these declines in habitat suitability will vary since seawater temperatures are moderated by physical factors such as higher latitudes or upwelling and aragonite saturation states.

--- SENTENCE 1876 ---
However, severe coral bleaching events will likely start to occur annually (van Hooidonk et al. 2014)

--- SENTENCE 1877 ---
Projecting species-specific responses to global threats is complicated by several physical and biological factors: (1) model uncertainty (emissions assumptions, strength of the climate's response to increased temperature, and large natural variations); (2) spatial variability in projected environmental conditions across the ranges of the species; and (3) species-specific responses depend on many biological characteristics, including, at a minimum, distribution, abundance, life history, susceptibility to threats, and capacity for acclimatization.

--- SENTENCE 1878 ---
Finally, warming will cause increased stratification of the upper ocean because water density decreases with increasing temperature.

--- SENTENCE 1879 ---
Increased stratification results in decreased vertical mixing, leaving surface waters warmer and nutrient-poor.

--- SENTENCE 1880 ---
Consequently, increased water vertical stratification will decrease the overall net productivity of coral reef ecosystems (e.g., fewer nutrients) and probably negatively impact coastal and oceanic fisheries.

--- SENTENCE 1881 ---
The impact of global warming is likely to be compounded by increasing coral disease, trophic effects of fishing, land-based sources of pollution, and other threats to corals.

--- SENTENCE 1882 ---
Increased seawater temperature can lower resistance to coral diseases and reduce coral health and survivorship.

--- SENTENCE 1883 ---
Coral disease outbreaks often have either accompanied or immediately followed bleaching events, and also follow seasonal patterns of high seawater temperatures.

--- SENTENCE 1884 ---
Fortunately, the incidence of coral disease in American Samoa is still relatively low.

--- SENTENCE 1885 ---
Sedimentation is considered a medium and limited to some watersheds in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 1886 ---
However, its impact cannot be downplayed.

--- SENTENCE 1887 ---
High sediment loading can induce sublethal effects, such as reductions in tissue thickness, polyp swelling, zooxanthellae loss, and excess mucus production.

--- SENTENCE 1888 ---
Increased suspended sediment can also reduce the amount of light in the water column, making less energy available for coral photosynthesis and growth.

--- SENTENCE 1889 ---
Sedimentation also impedes fertilization of spawned gametes and reduces larval settlement and survival of recruits and juveniles.

--- SENTENCE 1890 ---
Eutrophication is becoming a problem in American Samoa due to untreated sewage water.

--- SENTENCE 1891 ---
Nutrient can affect corals through direct impact on coral physiology and indirect effects through nutrient-stimulation of other community components (e.g., macroalgal turfs and seaweeds, and filter feeders) that compete with corals for space on the reef.

--- SENTENCE 1892 ---
The main vectors of anthropogenic nutrients are point-source discharges (such as rivers or sewage outfalls) and surface runoff from modified watersheds.

### CONSERVATION STRATEGY

1.

--- SENTENCE 1893 ---
We propose to enhance the development of **active coral reef restoration program that includes ocean nurseries** that can regrow coral fragments to colonies for transplanting to denuded reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1894 ---
This is not limited to the corals already listed as threatened under the Endangered Species Act.

--- SENTENCE 1895 ---
This active restoration program would also include basic **research on various aspects of coral demography such as growth rates, recruitment, reproductive biology, and distribution** that still remain as knowledge gaps in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1896 ---
Finally, we propose to put in place a **long-term program to eradicate coral predation** in American Samoa by crown-of-thorns starfish among other threats.

### <span id="page-142-0"></span>*5.11.4 MESOPHOTIC CORAL REEF FISHES*

!

--- SENTENCE 1897 ---
[](_page_142_Picture_5.jpeg)

*Etelis carbunculus, Etelis coruscans and Pristipimoides filamentosus*

Local common name: Palu-malau (for *E. carbunculus* and *E. coruscans*), Palu-sina (for *P. filamentosus*); Palu-atu and Palu-loa (for *E. coruscans*);

--- SENTENCE 1898 ---
Palu-ena'ena and Palu-pa'epa'e (for *P. filamentosus*)

Distribution: Widely distributed but restricted to deepwater coral reefs

Abundance: Moderately common

Status: Not listed under the Endangered Species Act

### DISTRIBUTION

--- SENTENCE 1899 ---
The ruby snapper *Etelis carbunculus* and the flame snapper *Etelis coruscans* are deep-water fish of the subfamily Etelinae (Lutjanidae) that generally inhabit waters between 100 m and 500 m depth (Carpenter and Niem 2001).

--- SENTENCE 1900 ---
The Ruby Snapper, *E. carbunculus*, is found throughout the Indian and Pacific Oceans.

--- SENTENCE 1901 ---
It is known from the coast of East Africa to the Persian Gulf and Red Sea, across the Indo-pacific, north to Japan and south to Australia.

--- SENTENCE 1902 ---
It is distributed from 300 N and 250 S in the tropical and subtropical waters of the Pacific Ocean.

--- SENTENCE 1903 ---
In the Pacific, its range extends to Hawaii, including French Polynesia.

--- SENTENCE 1904 ---
It has also been recorded from northern New Zealand (Fry and Newman 2010) and out to both Christmas and Cocos Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1905 ---
The flame snapper *E. coruscans* is distributed from East Africa up to the Bashee River 32°S, eastward to the Hawaiian Islands, north to southern Japan, south to Australia (Queensland and New South Wales) and Lord Howe Island.

--- SENTENCE 1906 ---
*Pristipomoides filamentosus* is another eteline snapper of major fishery importance.

--- SENTENCE 1907 ---
Its geographic range extends throughout the tropical and subtropical Indo-Pacific from the Hawaiian Archipelago and the central western Pacific Ocean to the western Indian Ocean and south along northwestern and northeastern Australia (Allen 1985; Kramer et al. 1994; Newman 2006).

### HABITAT AND ECOLOGY

*E. carbunculus* is a demersal species that inhabits rocky bottoms on the continental shelf from a depth range of 90-400 m. It feeds on fishes and large invertebrates such as squids, shrimps and crabs and on planktonic organisms (Fry and Newman 2010).

--- SENTENCE 1908 ---
This demersal species is known to occur in aggregations.

--- SENTENCE 1909 ---
At Vanuatu (New Hebrides) spawning occurs throughout much of the year, with a peak in activity around November (Allen 1985).

--- SENTENCE 1910 ---
Previous studies reported varying estimates of the longevity of snappers from <15 to >30 years with *Etelis* on the younger range (10 to 15 years) (Ralston and Williams 1988, Smith and Kostlan 1991).

--- SENTENCE 1911 ---
Smith and Kostlan (1991) estimated the maximum age of *E. carbunculus* in Vanuatu to be <10 years.

--- SENTENCE 1912 ---
Williams et al. (2013) examined sectioned otoliths and estimated maximum age of *E. carbunculus* as 21 years based on ring counts.

--- SENTENCE 1913 ---
Fry et al. (2006) made the highest estimate at 28 years age for a 760 mm SL *E. carbunculus*.

--- SENTENCE 1914 ---
Recent methodology using bomb carbon dating indicated that *E. carbunculus* can live up to 35-39 years (Andrews et al. 2011).

--- SENTENCE 1915 ---
This has significantly extended the longevity of this deepwater snapper and suggests that natural mortality rate is much lower.

--- SENTENCE 1916 ---
Bomb carbon dating has not been conducted for *E. coruscans*.

--- SENTENCE 1917 ---
Maximum age for *E. coruscans* from otolith ring counts has been estimated to be 18 years.

--- SENTENCE 1918 ---
As is usually the case with the other eteline snappers, many of the life history characteristics of *P. filamentosus* are unknown or incompletely described (Moffitt 2006).

--- SENTENCE 1919 ---
Earlier estimates of the maximum age of *P. filamentosus* ranged from 5 to almost 17 years (Uchiyama and Tagami 1984, Ralston and Miyamoto 1983, Radtke 1987, Ralston and Williams 1988, Fry et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 1920 ---
Bomb radiocarbon dating has now extended estimates of maximum age for *P. filamentosus* to more than 40 years (Andrews et al. 2012).

--- SENTENCE 1921 ---
However, there seems to be geographic differences in maximum age as its estimates for *P. filamentosus* in the Mariana Islands is 27 years.

--- SENTENCE 1922 ---
The growth rate (k) estimated from bomb radiocarbon dating indicated that *P. filamentosus* is a slow-growing fish with *k* equal to 0.24.

--- SENTENCE 1923 ---
Using otolith increment data, Williams et al. (2013) also showed slow growth rates for *E. carbunculus*

--- SENTENCE 1924 ---
(k = 0.28) and *E. coruscans* (k = 0.32).

--- SENTENCE 1925 ---
Overfishing is the main threat to these mesophotic eteline snappers.

--- SENTENCE 1926 ---
The relatively high longevity and slow growth for these fishes suggest low natural mortality, low population turn-over and in turn, low production potential.

--- SENTENCE 1927 ---
These are all life history characteristics for a fish that is highly vulnerable to even low levels fo fishing.

--- SENTENCE 1928 ---
Williams et al. (2013) suggested that a fishing mortality of F < 0.1 is necessary to achieve stock persistence.

--- SENTENCE 1929 ---
In many other fast-growing fishes, F = 0.5 is a reference point for sustainable fishing.

### CONSERVATION STRATEGY

1.

--- SENTENCE 1930 ---
We propose to support and conduct **basic research on the life history of these eteline snappers** in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 1931 ---
Scientific research should include growth rates, longevity, population genetics, reproduction and spatial distribution.

--- SENTENCE 1932 ---
These information will be critical in conducting stock assessment and developing fisheries management policies for these fishes.

--- SENTENCE 1933 ---
# <span id="page-145-0"></span>**6 PROTECTION OF NATIVE HABITATS

--- SENTENCE 1934 ---
AS A STRATEGY FOR WILDLIFE CONSERVATION**

### <span id="page-145-1"></span>**6.1 TERRESTRIAL HABITATS**

### <span id="page-145-2"></span>*6.1.1 STATE OF KNOWLEDGE*

The forested habitats of American Samoa have been and continue to be the focus of scientific study.

--- SENTENCE 1935 ---
The composition and structure of natural habitats, as well as the factors responsible for spatial patterns in these variables, has been extensively documented (Whistler 1980, Amerson et al. 1982b, Webb et al. 1999, Webb et al. 2005, 2006).

--- SENTENCE 1936 ---
Muller-Dombois & Fosberg (1998) identified forest dynamics in Samoa as little studied, but recent efforts have made important advancements to the understanding about the dynamics of wildlife habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1937 ---
In addition to Whistler's (1995) eight 0.1 ha plots in the NPAS Tutuila unit (now largely defunct owing to small size and inconsistent survey methodology, DMWR has installed four 1.0 ha Long-Term Monitoring Plots (LTMPs) to monitor forest dynamics on Tutuila (Webb & Fa'aumu 1999).

--- SENTENCE 1938 ---
In addition, NPAS in collaboration with DMWR installed four LTMPs on Ta'u, totaling 6 ha (Webb et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 1939 ---
And the US Forest Service Plots installed 30 plots (ASCC Land Grant *unpub data*).

--- SENTENCE 1940 ---
Studies on forest phenology, recruitment, and survivorship studies from these plots and elsewhere in American Samoa have been conducted (Trail 1994, Seamon et al. 2006, E. Webb *unpub data*).

--- SENTENCE 1941 ---
Recent research on forest recruitment demonstrates extremely high rates of annual seedling turnover, with mortality rates exceeding 20% (E. Webb, *unpub data*).

--- SENTENCE 1942 ---
LTMPs on Tutuila have documented the intercyclone habitat dynamics, demonstrating that even under periods of low cyclone activity the forest habitat of American Samoa have high rates of mortality and recruitment (Webb et al. 2014), indicating wide spatiotemporal variability in resource streams for wildlife.

--- SENTENCE 1943 ---
LTMPs on Ta'u provided opportunity to monitor habitat reorganization after Cyclone Olaf (Category 5), which struck the island in 2005.

--- SENTENCE 1944 ---
Monitoring post-cyclone habitat recovery is revealing the importance of species traits in survival and resilience of wildlife-relevant tree species (Webb et al. 2015), and is documenting ecosystem-level outcomes, with high relevance to wildlife as resource availability (species composition) changes over time (Webb et al.

--- SENTENCE 1945 ---
Other potential contributors to habitat dynamics such as disease have also been well-documented (Grandison 1996, McKenzie 1996, Brooks 2000, Brooks 2002, Brooks 2004).

--- SENTENCE 1946 ---
The spatial distributions of habitat classes were mapped in the mid-1980s by Cole et al. (1988).

--- SENTENCE 1947 ---
Aerial photos of subsets of the island are available at scattered times (ASG *unpub.photos*), while satellite images are available from 2001 (IKONOS, Space Imaging Systems) and 2004 (QuikBird).

--- SENTENCE 1948 ---
Such remote-sensing methods have been used elsewhere to map habitat modifications (e.g., Estreguil & Lambin 1996, Comber et al. 2003).

--- SENTENCE 1949 ---
In 2009, DMWR initiated an effort to map wildlife habitat on the islands of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1950 ---
The impetus for this effort originated as a result of the findings of the 2005 CWCS for American Samoa which identified a deficiency in updated and detailed habitat maps for the islands (Utzurrum et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 1951 ---
Although the vegetation of American Samoa has been extensively mapped over the past 30 years (Whitesell et al. 1988, Donnegan et al. 2004, Liu et al. 2011)

--- SENTENCE 1952 ---
these products are either outdated or too generalized to meet the needs of local wildlife biologists.

--- SENTENCE 1953 ---
The goal was to produce detailed and accurate habitat maps using a habitat classification scheme specific to American Samoa that would provide a useful management tool for wildlife biologists (see section 4.3 Mapping of Wildlife Habitat in American Samoa).

### <span id="page-146-0"></span>*6.1.2 THREATS*

The main threats to wildlife habitat in American Samoa are categorized under the umbrella terms "deforestation" or "degradation".

--- SENTENCE 1954 ---
Deforestation refers to the process of habitat conversion from forest (or other native habitat) to another land use; in American Samoa conversion is most frequently to agriculture or urbanization.

--- SENTENCE 1955 ---
Deforestation results in the immediate and short- to long-term loss of all resources to wildlife.

--- SENTENCE 1956 ---
Degradation refers to the loss of habitat quality, ecological linkages or structure that may have cascading effects on wildlife through alterations in resource availability.

--- SENTENCE 1957 ---
Specific actions creating these threats, which DMWR prioritizes, are: land clearing, invasive species, and habitat disturbances.

--- SENTENCE 1958 ---
Degradation is the second principle threat to wildlife habitat in American Samoa.

### **Land clearing: immediate and significant loss of resources for wildlife**

Land clearing and conversion to unsuitable habitat types is a growing threat to native habitat as the human population,especially on Tutuila, continues to grow and traditional agricultural methods are utilized to supplement livelihoods.

--- SENTENCE 1959 ---
The native habitats of American Samoa have evolved in a disturbance-prone ecosystem, and are well-adapted to withstand and recover from hurricanes (Shanahan et al. 2001, Webb et al. 2015); these characteristics have in the past buffered the wildlife and habitats of the territory from many of the changes wrought by humans.

--- SENTENCE 1960 ---
However, land clearing for agricultural expansion exceeds the short-term resilience of habitat, although recovery can occur if agriculture is abandoned and left fallow, albeit regeneration that is severely impacted by invasive species (see below).

--- SENTENCE 1961 ---
Unfortunately, there is likely to be a point at which the extent of clearing exceeds the rate of regeneration, potentially causing a cascade of other ecological effects such as depletion.

--- SENTENCE 1962 ---
Clearing habitat for agriculture and urbanization is an essential component of development in American Samoa, however it is essential that decisions to clear land are informed by adequate land use planning and habitat management legislation (Department of Commerce 2003; McCarthy 2005).

--- SENTENCE 1963 ---
ASG has a Project Notification and Review System (PNRS), a multi- agency system of approval for which all development projects must pass.

--- SENTENCE 1964 ---
In theory such a system could suffice, but as currently implemented it has a number of significant weaknesses.

- 1) PNRS applies only to actual development, not clearing of habitats for agriculture.

--- SENTENCE 1965 ---
This has prompted the practice of clearing land for putative 'agricultural' purposes, but then shortly thereafter submitting a proposal to PNRS for development of the now-cleared land.

--- SENTENCE 1966 ---
This skirts the PNRS requirements of mitigation for clearing of forested areas.

--- SENTENCE 1967 ---
The strength of a system such as PNRS could be that it is a collective decision-making body.

--- SENTENCE 1968 ---
However, as currently implemented, enforcement and decisions on approval are placed solely on individual member agencies.

--- SENTENCE 1969 ---
This greatly exacerbates the problem of political, cultural or other pressure being brought to bear on single agencies.
- 3) There is not a clear habitat management plan and attendant authority for the territory.

--- SENTENCE 1970 ---
DMWR has legal authority to manage habitats in the context of wildlife management, but there are numerous gaps and vagaries in the attendant legislation (McCarthy 2005), making enforcement of difficult cases particularly problematic.
- 4) There is unequal implementation of the PNRS system.

--- SENTENCE 1971 ---
In particular, ASG agencies themselves frequently skip PNRS review of their own projects.

--- SENTENCE 1972 ---
Besides the ecological damage directly caused by these projects, there is an attendant backlash in the public, who question the government's commitment to the principles purportedly promoted by PNRS.
- 5)

--- SENTENCE 1973 ---
The zoning process, as implemented in practice by the zoning board, has no significant environmental or ecological requirement in its decisions.

--- SENTENCE 1974 ---
Weaknesses in the PNRS system leave native habitat potentially vulnerable to clearing, whether or not the land is eventually converted to an active land use.

--- SENTENCE 1975 ---
### **Invasive species: modifying and degrading critical wildlife habitat**

There are a host of invasive species already well established, and expanding, in the territory.

--- SENTENCE 1976 ---
The PIER (Pacific Island Ecosystems at Risk project) lists approximately 285 invasive species in the territory, among all life forms (trees, shrubs, herbs, vines, grasses, ferns).

--- SENTENCE 1977 ---
Some of the most common and aggressive invasive species include *Abizzia falcataria*, *Adenanthera pavonina*, *Cinnamomum verum*, *Clidemia hirta*, *Leucaena leucocephala*, *Merremia peltata*, and *Mikania micrantha*.

--- SENTENCE 1978 ---
These species significantly alter wildlife habitat by arresting regeneration (Elmqvist et al. 1994) and outcompeting native trees for space.

--- SENTENCE 1979 ---
Arrested succession by vines significantly reduces the quality of resources available to wildlife, as the regeneration of plants that feed wildlife is reduced or prevented.

--- SENTENCE 1980 ---
The establishment and expansion of invasive tree species into native forest reduces the availability of fruit resources available to frugivores.

### **Habitat disturbances: enhancing the negative consequences of invasive species**

While invasive species may under some circumstances establish in intact, mature habitat, disturbances are required for invasion of many species.

--- SENTENCE 1981 ---
Land clearing, as described above, reduces competition and will create greater opportunities for non-native species to invade.

--- SENTENCE 1982 ---
Cyclones, by removing canopy and killing mature trees, creates similar, but less extensive, conditions.

--- SENTENCE 1983 ---
Small-scale disturbances by people, such as the creation of small parcels of agricultural land, result in similar patterns of invasion.

--- SENTENCE 1984 ---
Therefore, the degradation of wildlife habitat, as well as its management and conservation, must be viewed in terms of not only invasive species, but also in terms of habitat characteristics and disturbance regimes.

### <span id="page-148-0"></span>*6.1.3 CONSERVATION PRIORITIES*

Our habitat protection priorities are to: 1) *obtain frequent, detailed, and accurate maps or images of land use patterns* in the territory, 2) use these data to *identify priority sites for conservation or land-use modifications*, 3) *implement measures and activities to improve habitat quality, expand the extent of native habitats, reduce the threat of invasive species, and protect endangered or critical habitats*, 4) *establish appropriate legal authority* to institute and implement regulatory and statutory guidelines *for habitat conservation* (see Section 9), and 5) establish wildlife reserves through land acquisition or long term conservation leasing of critical or rare wildlife habitats.

--- SENTENCE 1985 ---
DMWR has made significant strides in several of these priorities.

--- SENTENCE 1986 ---
A very high resolution habitat map of the territory has been produced.

--- SENTENCE 1987 ---
This map serves as a foundation for subsequent mapping efforts to evaluate changes to wildlife habitat extent, and for higherorder investigations and actions to enhance and protect critical wildlife habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1988 ---
Efforts to encourage the use of native species in urban and residential landscapes also have significant promise, and preliminary studies of propagation have been conducted for some tree species (Hanson et al. 2005).

--- SENTENCE 1989 ---
Under DMWR's Wildlife Restoration Program (FY2006- 2010) the development and publication of the territory's first guide to native ornamental trees has been published, entitled "A guide to the native ornamental trees of American Samoa".

--- SENTENCE 1990 ---
This handbook has been translated into Samoan and has been published with the title "Tusi Ta'iala mo La'au Teuteu Fanua o Amerika Samoa".

--- SENTENCE 1991 ---
Efforts to control identified invasive species has been undertaken with the American Samoa Invasive Species Team (ASIST), a cooperative working group among local and federal governmental agencies.

--- SENTENCE 1992 ---
In the context of the high resolution habitat map recently finalized, DMWR can now work to evaluate how invasive species interact with habitat type in order to inform its intervention strategies, making them more streamlined and efficient.

--- SENTENCE 1993 ---
A critically important prerequisite for the implementation of habitat management and regulation of habitat alteration is the legal mandate to do so.

--- SENTENCE 1994 ---
To this end, our highest priority is to pass legislation that would formalize the idea of habitat preservation and management in the territory.

--- SENTENCE 1995 ---
Additionally, the creation of wildlife reserves through land acquisition or long-term conservation leases has been explored in order to protect critical wildlife habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1996 ---
DMWR is currently working with the land owners of the Naumati Forest and Olovalu Crater for long term protection of these critica wildlife habitat resources.

--- SENTENCE 1997 ---
We feel that with proper implementation of these plans, the wildlife resources of the territory could be managed in such a way as to promote both economic growth and development, as well as provide adequate protection for native plants and wildlife.

### <span id="page-149-0"></span>**6.2 MARINE HABITATS**

### <span id="page-149-1"></span>*6.2.1 STATE OF KNOWLEDGE*

The focus of our habitat conservation priorities is on the shallow-water coral reef ecosystems and the mesophotic coral ecosystem.

--- SENTENCE 1998 ---
We recognize the importance of these habitats for the various species of greatest concern.

--- SENTENCE 1999 ---
We have included the mangrove ecosystem since there are numerous studies that show the its linkage with the coral reef ecosystem.

--- SENTENCE 2000 ---
Several snapper species, for instance, have been known to use mangroves as nursery habitats with an ontogenetic shift to coral ecosystems as adults.

--- SENTENCE 2001 ---
There are already several studies and on-going programs on coral reef ecosystems that include satellite image analyses of their distribution and surveys on their status.

--- SENTENCE 2002 ---
On the other hand, the study of the mesophotic coral ecosystems in the Territory is still very limited but NOAA has previously conducted a survey of these deep-water reefs using remotely-operated video cameras.

### <span id="page-149-2"></span>*6.2.2 THREATS*

We have previously identified the main threats to coral reefs and various marine species in the Territory:

(1) Coral mortality and reef loss due to sedimentation, severe storms, predation and global climate change.

--- SENTENCE 2003 ---
Habitat degradation brought about by anthropogenic inputs into the coral reef system are of particular concern due to increases in land-based sources of pollution and added nutrients to the coral reef system from the proliferation of piggeries, increased sediment and nutrient loading, especially in reef areas near highly populated villages.

- (2) The predation by the crown-of-thorn starfish has historically and currently caused damage to reefs in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 2004 ---
The extent of coral disease and coralline-algal diseases is relatively recent but this has given us a general picture of their impacts.

--- SENTENCE 2005 ---
The NOAA CRED surveys have shown that diseases were observed in 25 scleractinian coral genera.

--- SENTENCE 2006 ---
The most abundant genera and families seem to be disproportionately susceptible to disease: Acropopridae, Faviidae and Poritidae.

--- SENTENCE 2007 ---
These families accounted for 75% of all cases detected and all the disease categories.

--- SENTENCE 2008 ---
Five coralline-algal diseases were recorded around American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2009 ---
Ofu & Olosega and Swains had elevated site-specific occurrences.

--- SENTENCE 2010 ---
However, estimates of occurrence of coralline-algal diseases still remains low compared to other areas in the Pacific (Vargas-Angel 2010) and with no active outbreaks known.
- (3) There is an increasing concern on the general predicted impacts of global climate change on the coral reefs in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 2011 ---
It is highly predicted that sea-surface temperature will rise, the intensity and frequency of days with extreme heat and extreme rainfall will increase.

--- SENTENCE 2012 ---
The already increasing sea levels will definitely impact growth rates of coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 2013 ---
The predicted changes in the frequency and strength of El Nino Southern Oscillation will impact coral reefs with predicted drastic lowering of tidal cycles and increased frequency of bleaching events.

--- SENTENCE 2014 ---
There are no data available to predict the impact of these threats to the mesophotic coral ecosystems.

--- SENTENCE 2015 ---
### <span id="page-150-0"></span>*6.2.3 CONSERVATION PRIORITIES*

There are already several initiatives that protect the coral reef habitats in the Territory in the form of managed areas.

--- SENTENCE 2016 ---
Marine protected area programs include the Department of Commerce Special Management Areas (Leone Pala, Nuuuli Pala and the Pago Pago Harbor);

--- SENTENCE 2017 ---
Department of Marine and Wildlife Resources Community-Based Fisheries Management Program and No-Take Program; the National Marine Sanctuaries; the National Parks; and the National Wildlife Refuge and Marine National Monuments.

--- SENTENCE 2018 ---
A special mention is the ridge-to-reef initiative in the Fagaalu village that is being implemented by the NOAA Coral Reef Conservation Program.

--- SENTENCE 2019 ---
*Our conservation priority is to support these marine protected area programs, and ecosystem-based and ridge-to-reef management approaches* in recognition to the integrity of the reef and the terrestrial to marine linkages*.

--- SENTENCE 2020 ---
This conservation approach is highly appropriate in addressing the listing of several corals under the Endangered Species Act.

--- SENTENCE 2021 ---
Given the current threats to coral reef reefs, *our conservation priority is to develop a program that responds to coral reef disaster or predation outbreaks*.

--- SENTENCE 2022 ---
This would include a program on coral threat/predator eradication/removal to address the crown-ofthorns starfish outbreaks.

--- SENTENCE 2023 ---
Coral and coralline-algal disease, although still low, is part of this disaster response program.

--- SENTENCE 2024 ---
It is important to identify coral reef ecosystems resilient to current and predicted threats.

--- SENTENCE 2025 ---
*Our conservation priority is to conduct and support various climate change vulnerability assessments to identify resilient and vulnerable areas*.

--- SENTENCE 2026 ---
This would identify priority areas of conservation and management.

--- SENTENCE 2027 ---
Given the chronic threats to coral reef ecosystems, *our conservation priority is to develop an active restoration program to enhance the recovery of these ecosystems*.

--- SENTENCE 2028 ---
This restoration program will cover developing coral nurseries for collected coral fragments and recruits developed from collected coral gametes.

--- SENTENCE 2029 ---
## <span id="page-152-0"></span>**7 WILDLIFE IN THE CULTURE: ANCHORING CONSERVATION TO TRADITION AS A STRATEGY**

The successful implementation of this conservation strategy will depend largely upon the support it receives from the local community, a community in which all aspects of life are influenced by Samoan culture (*fa'asamoa*).

--- SENTENCE 2030 ---
Incorporation of Samoan culture, with emphasis on wildlife's role in the culture, into the conservation strategy can therefore help elicit support through the fostering of a local, culture-based conservation ethic.

--- SENTENCE 2031 ---
Although not exhaustive, this synopsis covers published accounts of wildlife in ancient Samoan culture and can be used as a source in the development of programs designed to foster a conservation ethic in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2032 ---
The marine and terrestrial world are featured in all of the following aspects of ancient Samoan culture: cultural practices, oral tradition, artistic designs, cultural items, hunting and fishing, and religion.

--- SENTENCE 2033 ---
The ocean is integral to the Samoan culture and cultural practices.

--- SENTENCE 2034 ---
Fish were traditionally eaten every day in the Samoan household although some species of fish were not eaten due to being poisonous or unpalatable or because they were deified (Watters 1958, Krämer 1995).

--- SENTENCE 2035 ---
As a result, fishing activities stood in high esteem in traditional Samoan culture, fishing skill brought high social status, and fishing activities figured importantly in mythology.

--- SENTENCE 2036 ---
Since social reciprocity is a distinct cultural practice, fish and other fishery resources (e.g. *palolo* worm or *Palolo viridis*) were and still are part of customary exchanges.

--- SENTENCE 2037 ---
Since fish and fishing is part of everyday life, Samoans have intimate knowledge of the ocean so that related words are parts of phrases.

--- SENTENCE 2038 ---
For example, the phrase '*Ua a'e le tai lo or taivale*', translates as a poor season when fish are scarce (Milner 1966).

--- SENTENCE 2039 ---
*Vaipalolo* was the period of the wet season, beginning with the rise of the *palolo* in October or November.

--- SENTENCE 2040 ---
During the *palolo* season when the epitoke of *Palolo viridis* are released as swarms in the ocean at night, special festivities are organized the night before the harvest.

--- SENTENCE 2041 ---
When the signs of the *palolo* swarm are apparent, food and *ava* (drink extract from the local plant) are prepared by the village *taupou* (virgin).

--- SENTENCE 2042 ---
Food is accumulated and feasting and amusements occurred before the harvest of *palolo* swarm on the final night of the harvest.

--- SENTENCE 2043 ---
Palolo has been culturally considered as a visitor so that Samoans traditionally wore *mosoi* (a local tree)

--- SENTENCE 2044 ---
flowers to welcome this visitor during its swarms.

--- SENTENCE 2045 ---
Like the *palolo*, *atule* (the fish *Selar crumenopthalmus*) harvest is associated with ceremonies, division of labor and legends.

--- SENTENCE 2046 ---
Samoans used 'sacred' stones as lures for *atule* and these undergo ritual washing.

--- SENTENCE 2047 ---
Supernatural influences, deities and taboos (*tapu*) were important in pre-historic Samoan political and social systems.

--- SENTENCE 2048 ---
Turner (1989) provides many examples of fish and sea creatures that were considered sacred in various districts and villages and well as sanctions associated with violating taboos.

--- SENTENCE 2049 ---
For example, the month of May was sacred to the deity incarnate in *fe'e* or octopus.

--- SENTENCE 2050 ---
During this period which can be three months in other districts, no traveller was allowed to pass through the village by public road nor was any canoe allowed in the lagoon off that part of the settlement.

--- SENTENCE 2051 ---
Violators could be beaten, if not killed, for insulting the god.

--- SENTENCE 2052 ---
In Samoan legends, Sina was known to have brought the red-lipped mullet from Fiji and that the family of Toaloa in Puapua Savai'i has the right to rule over the fishing arrangements for her mullet (Hiroa 1930).

--- SENTENCE 2053 ---
Pre-historic Samoan culture had numerous deities both terrestrial and marine animals.

--- SENTENCE 2054 ---
The deities from the sea included:

--- SENTENCE 2055 ---
(1) Faamalu (Shade) and Taapai, separate deities represented by a trumpet shell or a fish; (2) Fuai Lagi (Beginner of Heavens) represented by the eel Maraena; (3) Nonia as incarnated in a cockle; (4) Limulimuta incarnate in the seaweed; (5) Tuialii (King of Chiefs) variously incarnated as mullet, eel and octopus.

--- SENTENCE 2056 ---
In pre-historic Samoan culture, a species of shark called naiufi was regarded as the king of sharks and treated with ceremonial respect.

--- SENTENCE 2057 ---
It was considered a great honor to kill a naiufi.

--- SENTENCE 2058 ---
As the canoe came in with a naiufi, the shell trumpet was sounded and the canoe paraded before the village.

--- SENTENCE 2059 ---
The owner met his canoe at the landing with a fine mat and touched the head of the shark with it.

--- SENTENCE 2060 ---
The mat was given to the *tautai*

--- SENTENCE 2061 ---
(the village head fisherman) and the shark was given to the canoe owner and the village chiefs, where it was ceremonially divided among them.

--- SENTENCE 2062 ---
The shark, like the bonito, was cut into ceremonial divisions, with certain parts to certain people.

--- SENTENCE 2063 ---
The stomach and intestines of the shark were regarded as the best parts of the fish and were distributed to the talking chief and the head fisherman (Hiroa 1930).

--- SENTENCE 2064 ---
Bonito fishing was regarded as the most elegant sport in Samoan culture and was practiced with various ceremonies from canoe-building associated with mats, food and gifts.

--- SENTENCE 2065 ---
Canoes were often decorated with ovula (*Ovula ovum*) shells and associated with social status and distinction.

--- SENTENCE 2066 ---
Bonito fishing was so special that there were many taboos, or restrictions, associated with it.

--- SENTENCE 2067 ---
Bonito was considered a fish for chiefs, and even the supreme god, Tagaloa, wanted a bonito.

--- SENTENCE 2068 ---
There are many other special terms and phrases related to bonito.

--- SENTENCE 2069 ---
For example, the first bonito in a new canoe is called *o le i'a a Tagaloa* (Tagaloa's fish)

--- SENTENCE 2070 ---
In Manu'a, the families of the crews and boat owners were forbidden to do any work while the fleet was out fishing for bonitoes.

--- SENTENCE 2071 ---
They were expected to remain idle and pray to Tuiatua, patron saint of fishing (Holmes 1974).

--- SENTENCE 2072 ---
In distributing the cut bonito, the loin of its back including the head is especially given to the chief.

--- SENTENCE 2073 ---
The bonito hook was very important in fishing so that it was made by a specialist and it was important to tie the hook correctly.

--- SENTENCE 2074 ---
Samoan fish-hooks, in general, were thought to have divine origins because the shell that was used was said to have been brought down from heaven (Krämer 1995).

--- SENTENCE 2075 ---
Oral tradition in the form of legends and proverbs was the means by which Samoa's history and culture was preserved and passed through generations prior to the arrival of Europeans (Lotu-Drabble 2000).

--- SENTENCE 2076 ---
A few of the most well-known **legends** that feature wild animals as key characters are "The Origin of the Earth and People" in which Tuli (golden plover) is the assistant of the supreme god, Tagaloaalagi (Steubel et al. 1976), "The Birds Conference" (Muse & Muse 1982), "The Title (ao) Tonumaipe'a" featuring fruit bats as a woman's rescuers (Kramer 1902-1903), "The Story of the Sega" (Kramer 1902-1903), and "The Wailing Turtle" (Anonymous 2001).

--- SENTENCE 2077 ---
**Proverbs**, many of which were taken from legends, comprise a second component of Samoan oral tradition.

--- SENTENCE 2078 ---
In the book, Proverbs of Samoa compiled by N.M.M. Saipele (2002), approximately 25% of the listed proverbs deal with wild animals in some way.

--- SENTENCE 2079 ---
A large percentage of these center around the ancient sport of lupe (pigeon) hunting.

--- SENTENCE 2080 ---
Some of the other wild animals featured in this book are *manutagi* (purple-capped fruit dove), *toloa* (wild duck), *fuia* (Samoan starling), *pe'a* (fruit bat), and *sega* (blue-crowned lorikeet).

--- SENTENCE 2081 ---
Examples of some of the many proverbs relevant to conservation are "*E le togia Fuia aua o Moso*," which means do not throw stones at the *fuia* because it is actually the feared god, Moso (Saipele, No Date) and "*Ua maua ula futifuti*," which refers to the shredding of rare lorikeet feathers and means "to be careless and wasteful" (Schultz 1980).

--- SENTENCE 2082 ---
There are countless Samoan proverbs associated with fishing showing indeed how the sea is intimate with everyday life.

--- SENTENCE 2083 ---
Selected proverbs and their meanings are:

*Va lelei*.

--- SENTENCE 2084 ---
To keep up friendly relations with one's neighbour.

--- SENTENCE 2085 ---
*O le va'a si'I vale la'u lauga nei*.

--- SENTENCE 2086 ---
My speech is like a canoe launched without a sufficient reason.

--- SENTENCE 2087 ---
*Faiva o Fiti ia lililo*.

--- SENTENCE 2088 ---
Let the Fijian method of fishing remain a secret.

--- SENTENCE 2089 ---
*Ia o gatasi le futia ma le umele*.

--- SENTENCE 2090 ---
The sinnet ring and the stand for the fishing rod must be equally strong.

--- SENTENCE 2091 ---
*To'ai fa'a ia a po*.

--- SENTENCE 2092 ---
To come like a fish in the night.

--- SENTENCE 2093 ---
*Talanoa atu, 'ae le talanoa manu*.

--- SENTENCE 2094 ---
The bonitos swim about thoughtlessly, but the seagulls are on the alert.

--- SENTENCE 2095 ---
*Tau ina uia o le ala o le atu*.

--- SENTENCE 2096 ---
Let it go the way of the bonito.

--- SENTENCE 2097 ---
*Ia moe le ufu, to'a le paipai*.

--- SENTENCE 2098 ---
An admonition to live in peace and harmony.

--- SENTENCE 2099 ---
*Avatu ni lo, aumai ni lo*.

--- SENTENCE 2100 ---
*O le vaivai o le fe'e*.

--- SENTENCE 2101 ---
Despite its soft body, the octopus is a powerful 'fish'.

--- SENTENCE 2102 ---
*Upu alofa, fa'aulaula or fa'anoanoa*.

--- SENTENCE 2103 ---
The chiefs and orators make the decisions, but the common people (tagatalautele) must carry them into effect and suffer all the consequent hurt and damage, e.g., after a declaration of war.

--- SENTENCE 2104 ---
**Song** can be considered a third component of Samoan oral tradition because it is the mode by which many legends are preserved (Lutu-Drabble 2000).

--- SENTENCE 2105 ---
One of the best anthologies of old Samoan songs can be found in the book entitled "Tusi PeseFatuga Tuai a Samoa - A Songbook of Popular Old Songs, Photographs, and Proverbs of Samoa" by T.C. Lutu-Drabble.

--- SENTENCE 2106 ---
By just scanning the table of contents, one can find 15 song titles with references to animals such as the *laumei* (turtle), *lupe*, *manu* (bird), and *isumu* (rat).

--- SENTENCE 2107 ---
These songs express everything from naturalistic observations of honeyeaters sipping flowers to frustration with and aversion towards rats.

--- SENTENCE 2108 ---
Although most of American Samoa's wildlife species are mentioned at some point in legends, proverbs, and songs, only a limited number of them appeared as designs in art or provided raw materials for cultural items.

!

--- SENTENCE 2109 ---
[](_page_155_Picture_9.jpeg)

**Artistic designs** can be found on *siapo* (bark cloth) and in the *tatau* (tattoo).

--- SENTENCE 2110 ---
Apparently, two of the prominent designs used in decorating siapo represented the *pe'a* (fruit bat) and the *pe'ape'a* (sheath-tailed bat) (Kramer 1902-1903).

--- SENTENCE 2111 ---
The men's *tatau*, also known as a "*pe'a*," gets its name from the shape it forms on the lower back.

--- SENTENCE 2112 ---
Perhaps the more significant connection of a wild animal to the *tatau* is the fact that part of the comb used in tattooing was made of tortoiseshell (Mallon 2002).

--- SENTENCE 2113 ---
*Other cultural items that were made with wild animal parts are whale-tooth necklaces, tu'iga (*headdresses), and *'ietoga* (fine mats).

--- SENTENCE 2114 ---
Because Samoans did not hunt whales, they procured their whale teeth from beached whales (Kramer 1902-1903).

--- SENTENCE 2115 ---
*Tu'iga* and *'ietoga* were both decorated with red feathers plucked from the *sega* (Kramer 1902-1903).

--- SENTENCE 2116 ---
Although family heirlooms may retain their original *sega* feathers, dyed chicken feathers are used in making these articles today (Sowell 2000).

--- SENTENCE 2117 ---
In contrast to raising live *sega* for their feathers, ancient Samoans captured *lupe* for sport.

--- SENTENCE 2118 ---
The **lupe hunt** was not only a form of recreation, but also "served as an arena for chiefly competition for prestige, status, and power" (Mallon 2002).

--- SENTENCE 2119 ---
Apparently, in ancient times the *lupe* was not eaten "for it was considered sacred," although it had become a popular food by the late 19th century (Kramer 1902-1903).

--- SENTENCE 2120 ---
Kramer also wrote that the sport (as practiced in ancient times) had been abandoned due mostly to the introduction of guns which "decimated" pigeon populations.

--- SENTENCE 2121 ---
Other birds "hunted" (not necessarily eaten) in addition to lupe were *manutagi*, *manuali'i* (swamp hen), *ve'a* (banded rail), *tava'e* (tropicbird), *gogo* (noddy), and some other seabirds (Saipele 2002).

--- SENTENCE 2122 ---
The *manuali'i* (also called *manusa*, meaning sacred bird), was "a chief's bird" which was caught and tamed (Kramer 1902-1903).

--- SENTENCE 2123 ---
In addition to birds, bats were apparently eaten, but more as a delicacy than a daily food source (Sinavaiana & Enright 1992).

--- SENTENCE 2124 ---
Another animal eaten more often than bats was the sea turtle, also known as the *i'a sa* (sacred fish).

--- SENTENCE 2125 ---
This marine reptile was captured with coconut fiber nets, prepared for consumption according to strict rules, and served to the highest-ranking villagers, with the head going to the high chiefs (Kramer 1902-1903).

--- SENTENCE 2126 ---
As noted above, there were several sacred wild animals in ancient Samoan culture.

--- SENTENCE 2127 ---
In many instances, this sacred status was due to the association of an animal with one of the many gods of the ancient religion (Mallon 2002).

--- SENTENCE 2128 ---
In addition to the *laumei* and *manuali'i*, other "holy animals of godly origin" were *tuli*, *sega fiti* (Fijian lorikeet), *sega*, *pe'a, fuia, ma'oma'o* (a bird), *pili* (lizard), and *lulu* (owl) (Mallon 2002).

--- SENTENCE 2129 ---
The *lulu*, specifically, was "often seen as an incarnation of a god consequently, a dead owl was usually buried quite ceremoniously" (Kramer 1902-1903).

--- SENTENCE 2130 ---
By comparing the literature on ancient Samoa to American Samoa as it is today, one can see that most aspects of the culture have endured in spite of heavy outside influences.

--- SENTENCE 2131 ---
It is this fact that makes a culture-based conservation feasible and more appropriate for the territory.

--- SENTENCE 2132 ---
Whereas some conservation-related aspects of the ancient culture seem to have disappeared (e.g., deification of birds), other practices have been adapted in favor of conservation (e.g., substituting chicken feathers for sega feathers in *'ietoga*), and still others have simply been forgotten (e.g., the idea that *lupe* were not originally hunted for food).

--- SENTENCE 2133 ---
The Dept. of Marine and Wildlife Resources, in collaboration with local agencies such as the "Ofisa o le Failautusi Aoao" (language preservation office), is in a position to help communities in American Samoa remember that wildlife has always been an integral part

of the culture and that the preservation of wildlife is, in fact, integral to the preservation of the *fa'asamoa* for generations to come.

### <span id="page-158-0"></span>**8 STATUTORY AND REGULATORY ASPECTS OF CONSERVATION**

The need for enabling statutory and regulatory provisions that provide the legal basis for effecting conservation, particularly with respect to habitat management, has long been recognized by staff of DMWR, the PNRS, and the Department of Legal Affairs (Attorney General's Office).

--- SENTENCE 2134 ---
This section is an updated review by internal staff to that previously conducted by McCarthy (2005) on existing provisions with the view of developing approaches that could address this particular gap.

--- SENTENCE 2135 ---
The review of the Territory's statutory and regulatory aspects of conservation was further threshed out during recent consultations with the Fono (Legislature).

### <span id="page-158-1"></span>**8.1 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY**

The Department of Marine and Wildlife Resources ("DMWR") has often expressed a need for improved management and protection of terrestrial and marine wildlife in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2136 ---
Threats to conservation previously identified in the last CWCS were rapidly increasing population, limited land area, encroachment by development, and international travel and trade.

--- SENTENCE 2137 ---
As the Territorial agency responsible for managing and perpetuating wildlife resources, DMWR has identified seven areas of immediate concern:1) protection and preservation of terrestrial and marine wildlife habitat; 2) management of endangered and/or threatened species; 3) habitat degradation due to invasive or injurious species, pollution and development; 4) the world-wide problem of species "bioprospecting"; (5) size and catch limits for fish and shellfish not currently covered by existing prohibitions; (6) the perennial problem of effective enforcement; and finally (7) potential impacts of global climate change.

--- SENTENCE 2138 ---
There are similarities and shades of differences of the issues on marine and terrestrial conservation.

--- SENTENCE 2139 ---
The latest revision of the CWCS is an opportunity to re-asses the effectiveness of the existing legal and regulatory frameworks and recommend strategies and directions for improvement in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2140 ---
A review of the fisheries regulations is also an opportunity to compare and contrast the issues being faced by the terrestrial and marine wildlife.

--- SENTENCE 2141 ---
First, the legal and regulatory regimes for protection and management of wildlife habitat in American Samoa are still inadequate in addressing some issues.

--- SENTENCE 2142 ---
The previous review rightfully stated that statutory authority for the DMWR, A.S.C.A.

--- SENTENCE 2143 ---
Title 24, Chapter 03, speaks directly to the management and conservation of marine and wildlife resources.

--- SENTENCE 2144 ---
However, it was not explicit in recognizing that habitats are also critical on the preservation and protection of wildlife.

--- SENTENCE 2145 ---
There was already a previous consensus among regulators and legislators to expand the authority of the DMWR under existing law to manage and protect wildlife habitat independently of the land use permit review system.

--- SENTENCE 2146 ---
There has not been much progress for the terrestrial habitat protection on this issue most probably due to the unique land ownership issues in the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 2147 ---
Most of the land is still communal.

--- SENTENCE 2148 ---
A habitat approach to conservation has only taken traction in the management of marine life in the forms of community-based marine protected areas and the declaration of special management areas in Leone and Nuuuli Pala.

--- SENTENCE 2149 ---
The first form is a co-management between the department and several villages while the second is a form of unilateral declaration by the local government.

--- SENTENCE 2150 ---
There is no analogous form in the terrestrial wildlife conservation and management.

--- SENTENCE 2151 ---
The co-management framework for managing forests and its wildlife is a form the department may pursue since this has showed success for the marine conservation and several models already exist in other areas in the world.

--- SENTENCE 2152 ---
The lease arrangement undertaken by the National Park System in American Samoa with various families is also another successful model, and DMWR is currently exploring options for leasing or purchasing vital wildlife habitat, including the Ottoville Lowland Rainforest and Olovalu Crater on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 2153 ---
In September 2016, the first terrestrial endangered species in American Samoa were listed by the USFWS.

--- SENTENCE 2154 ---
The territorial Endangered Species Act (ESA), A.S.C.A.

--- SENTENCE 2155 ---
Title 24, chapter 07, provides for appointment of a Commission with authority to nominate endangered or threatened species but the provision provides no role for DMWR in the listing process, and no authority by which to manage and conserve habitat once a species is listed.

--- SENTENCE 2156 ---
Therefore, the Territroial ESA has been determined by the USFWS as inadequate to allow the Terririory to manage and conserve ESA listed species.

--- SENTENCE 2157 ---
There is an ongoing effort to draft a new ESA that adequately protects both species and habitat within 2017.

--- SENTENCE 2158 ---
Third, invasive or injurious species of animals and plants are not addressed in existing statutes or rules, except to the extent a plant may be designated as a "noxious weed" harmful to agricultural crops or where animals are banned as "exotic" or "miscellaneous pets" by the territorial Department of Agriculture (DOA).

--- SENTENCE 2159 ---
Accordingly, suggested statute revisions are provided to enable the creation of an injurious species regulatory program within DMWR.

--- SENTENCE 2160 ---
Existing DOA authorities are also expanded to give DMWR a role in the quarantine, eradication or confiscations of injurious species of plants or animals.

--- SENTENCE 2161 ---
Although invasive species is a particularly relevant issue for the terrestrial wildlife, provisions needs to be incorporated to address potential marine invasive species.

--- SENTENCE 2162 ---
Fourth, American Samoa currently lacks any system for controlling, monitoring or protecting the public inventory of biological resources from the "bioprospecting" or mining of commercially valuable species of plants and animals.

--- SENTENCE 2163 ---
A new statutory provision has therefore needs to be created to address bioprospecting, to control the activities of species hunters, and to guarantee that a portion of any profits derived from bioprospecting are reserved to the communities from whose land species were harvested.

--- SENTENCE 2164 ---
Fifth, there is no provision on fish size and limited implementation of catch limits.

--- SENTENCE 2165 ---
There needs to be a formal assessment whether there is growth overfishing for various fish stocks.

--- SENTENCE 2166 ---
Scientific frameworks already exist for this type of assessment.

--- SENTENCE 2167 ---
Size limits established by the Secretariat of the Pacific Community have already been adapted by various countries in the region.

--- SENTENCE 2168 ---
There is a need to re-assess this framework and its applicability to the Territory's fisheries.

--- SENTENCE 2169 ---
The department needs to collaborate with the appropriate federal agencies on the implementation of catch limits in the Territory's fisheries.

--- SENTENCE 2170 ---
The re-authorized Magnuson-Stevens Act has provided a legal basis for the establishment of catch limits.

--- SENTENCE 2171 ---
Based on this authority, the Western Pacific Regional Fishery Management Council is developing a scientific framework to establish catch limits.

--- SENTENCE 2172 ---
Catch limits are annually established but the implementation is non-existent.

--- SENTENCE 2173 ---
Finally, there is a need to conduct a vulnerability assessment of the Territory to global climate change and perhaps to draft appropriate legislations.

--- SENTENCE 2174 ---
There is a working framework for climate change for the Territory under the Coral Reef Advisory Group but related liegislations have not been enacted.

--- SENTENCE 2175 ---
As an archipelagic territory, American Samoa is particularly vunerable to changes in climate and its impacts to its wildlife and exploitable resources.

--- SENTENCE 2176 ---
A vulnerability assessment of wildlife and habitats will also help identify resilient areas and prioritize these areas for protection.

--- SENTENCE 2177 ---
There is a need to incorporate the importance of the impact of global climate change in the Territory's statutes and regulations that pertain to marine and terrestrial wildlife.

--- SENTENCE 2178 ---
This report suggests modifications to existing statutes and regulations to improve enforcement, including providing DMWR with express administrative enforcement authority and the power to issue district court citations to enforce envisioned wildlife habitat protection requirements.

--- SENTENCE 2179 ---
As a whole, the recommended statutory changes provide a blueprint for improved protection of American Samoa's wildlife resources and the habitats upon which they depend.

### <span id="page-160-0"></span>**8.2 ANALYSIS OF EXISTING REGULATORY REGIMES**

The following discussion provides a brief analysis of existing statutes and regulations and identifies gaps or deficiencies in regulatory protections.

--- SENTENCE 2180 ---
Part III provides suggestions for filling the gaps and providing improved protections for wildlife and wildlife habitat.

### <span id="page-160-1"></span>*8.2.1 PROTECTION FOR WILDLIFE HABITATS*

The legal authorities for providing protection of diminishing wildlife habitats in American Samoa are twofold.

--- SENTENCE 2181 ---
First, there is the DMWR enabling statute set forth under Title 24, Chapter 03, of the ASCA.

--- SENTENCE 2182 ---
While this statute does not directly reference wildlife habitat protection or conservation, the DMWR is empowered to adopt regulations and to prepare and develop comprehensive plans for the management and protection of wildlife resources (ASCA 24.0304(2)).

--- SENTENCE 2183 ---
Arguably, a critical component for perpetuating wildlife is protection of wildlife habitat, but DMWR has never tested the limits of its authority by promulgating habitat protection regulations.

--- SENTENCE 2184 ---
Instead, DMWR has adopted Hunting Regulations[4](#page-161-0) that control the taking of various wildlife species, including fruit bats and native birds.

--- SENTENCE 2185 ---
A separate statute, ASCA 24.2305, provides the DMWR with authority to designate for conservation bat roosts and "areas of importance" to viable bat populations.

--- SENTENCE 2186 ---
Habitat protection for these species is therefore impliedly, but such protections require study, delineation, and the adoption of plans and maps via the rulemaking process.

--- SENTENCE 2187 ---
As of this time, no rules have been adopted relating to the preservation or protection of specific bat habitats.

--- SENTENCE 2188 ---
Another source for regulatory authority relating to habitat conservation is the territorial Coastal Management Act.[5](#page-161-1)

--- SENTENCE 2189 ---
This law establishes a land use permit (LUP)system for nearly all developments taking place in the territory, and further allows for the designation of Special Management Areas (SMA) through a delineation and nomination process to be approved by the Governor.

--- SENTENCE 2190 ---
However, it is important to note that a finalized SMA designation could take years for each area of concern, might be ultimately rejected by the sitting governor, and once designated would not necessarily preclude all development within its borders.

--- SENTENCE 2191 ---
Thus, the SMA process is both an uncertain and inadequate means for protecting areas deemed important to ensure the viability of wildlife.

--- SENTENCE 2192 ---
Finally, the SMA process has no clear definition of governance of these SMAs and its implementation.

--- SENTENCE 2193 ---
The regulations adopted under the Coastal Management Act statute (ASCMA) attempt to circumvent this limitation, by providing that critical habitat(s) will be protected and preserved where they are "essential to productivity of plant or an[im](#page-161-2)al species" or are listed as threatened or endangered under territorial (or federal) laws.

--- SENTENCE 2194 ---
<sup>6</sup> (A.S.A.C. § 26.0220.I.2).

--- SENTENCE 2195 ---
This is potent language, but the standard of proof for "critical habitat" may not always be possibly met, particularly on smaller properties or on properties which have already been significantly degraded.

--- SENTENCE 2196 ---
Further, some common activities in the territory such as land clearing for agriculture or traditional Samoan uses do not require a LUP.

--- SENTENCE 2197 ---
These activities will not, therefore, receive scrutiny under the coastal management rules relating to critical habitat.

--- SENTENCE 2198 ---
Still other common uses, such as construction of single family residences, require only "minor" permit program review.

--- SENTENCE 2199 ---
The minor LUP review process involves an

<span id="page-161-0"></span> <sup>4</sup> Hunting Regulations are set forth at Title 24, Chapter 08 of the American Samoa Administrative Code (ASAC).

--- SENTENCE 2200 ---
<span id="page-161-1"></span><sup>5</sup> ASCA §§ 24.0501 et seq.

--- SENTENCE 2201 ---
and regulations at ASAC 26.0201 et seq.

--- SENTENCE 2202 ---
This provision (ASAC 26.0220.I.2) itself may also be inadequately authorized by the ASCMA because it effectively replaces the Special Management Area (SMA) public notice, participation and approval provisions with case by case decision-making.

--- SENTENCE 2203 ---
It also lacks any connection to the presence of endangered or threatened species and their critical habitat.

abbreviated in-house analysis and approval by the Department of Commerce, normally without referral to the PNRS Board or to the DMWR for consideration of wildlife conservation concerns.

--- SENTENCE 2204 ---
Accordingly, issuance of minor permits can result in significant loss of wildlife habitat, as permits will in most cases be approved without the involvement of the DMWR.

--- SENTENCE 2205 ---
In conclusion, general authority exists under current territorial laws to protect individual species as well as wildlife habitats including "critical habitat."

--- SENTENCE 2206 ---
However, these authorities have either not been fully exercised, or existing authorities contain gaps through which many development activities can slip without meaningful review to the degradation of wildlife resources and their habitats.

### <span id="page-162-0"></span>*8.2.2 ENDANGERED SPECIES AND CRITICAL HABITAT*

--- SENTENCE 2207 ---
The designation of species as endangered or threatened can also provide an effective means to protect critical habitat for species as well as habitat for other wildlife.

--- SENTENCE 2208 ---
Under federal as well as state laws, a listing of a species as threatened or endangered triggers protection for not only that species but also the geographic areas deemed to be "critical habitat" necessary for the survival and propagation of that species.

--- SENTENCE 2209 ---
Unfortunately, the existing territorial Endangered Species Act (ESA), A.S.C.A.24.0701 et seq., fails to provide authority to designate critical habitat for any species listed as endangered or threatened pursuant to the Act.

--- SENTENCE 2210 ---
Nor has the Endangered Species Commission taken steps to make any territorial listings or designations.

--- SENTENCE 2211 ---
Therefore, a new Territorial ESA is currently being drafted by DMWR with assistance of the Attorney General's Office and the USFWS.

--- SENTENCE 2212 ---
### <span id="page-162-1"></span>*8.2.3 INJURIOUS OR INVASIVE SPECIES AND "BIOPROSPECTING"*

The world-wide phenomenon of invasive or introduced non-native species and the damage they cause to island wildlife and their habitats are well documented.

--- SENTENCE 2213 ---
Yet territorial legal authorities to deal with this problem remain underdeveloped or unutilized.

--- SENTENCE 2214 ---
Currently, the DMWR has authority under section 24.0304(2) of its enabling statute to adopt regulations addressing this problem.

--- SENTENCE 2215 ---
Because this authority has not been tested or utilized through regulations, the DMWR has striven to improve relations with the quarantine branch of the Department of Agriculture (DOA).

--- SENTENCE 2216 ---
The quarantine branch is tasked with inspecting and prohibiting entry to the territory of "noxious weeds" and undesirable domestic pets and other animals.

--- SENTENCE 2217 ---
Even if this cooperative approach were effective, the DOA derives its quarantine authority from a pair of statutes which do not go far enough to protect the territory's wildlife from non-native species.

--- SENTENCE 2218 ---
